First.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of articles 5.1. f) and 32 of the GDPR  
1. On the implementation of security measures appropriate to the risk  
(a) The AEPD understands that Endesa should have had implemented, from the outset, the security measures that it began to apply once it became aware of the incident.  

ENDESA cites Judgment number 188/2022 of the Supreme Court, dated February 15 (ECLI:ES:TS:2022:543), which indicates that the obligation to implement security measures imposed by data protection regulations has a nature of an obligation of means and not of result.  
And ENDESA explains that the AEPD, when assessing compliance or non-compliance with the obligation to implement security measures by Endesa, should analyze, solely, whether Endesa had, prior to the incident, measures that “in accordance with the state of technology and in relation to the nature of the processing carried out and the personal data in question, reasonably allow for the prevention of their alteration, loss, processing, or unauthorized access” (literal transcription of the aforementioned Judgment).  
ENDESA argues that this has not been the case. This is because the AEPD did not take into account, in that agreement, several pieces of evidence provided by Endesa to justify that the security measures implemented at the time the first Facebook ads were published and the first fraudulent contracts occurred were in accordance with the state of technology, taking into account the nature of the processing and the data processed, as can be inferred, among others: (i) from the Business Impact Analysis and the Risk Assessment prepared by Endesa regarding the tools ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and provided to the AEPD within the framework of the File; (ii) from the fact that, in May 2021, an ethical hacking exercise was conducted to detect possible vulnerabilities of the tools ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and to rectify those identified; and (iii) from the compliance, by both tools, with the policy for managing logical access to IT systems.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
73/214  
ENDESA indicates that it is at that moment, prior to the materialization of the breach, when the level of security of the measures implemented should be evaluated in accordance with the state of technology and in relation to the nature of the processing carried out and the personal data in question, since evaluating it afterwards, once the breach has occurred, would completely undermine the obligation to implement security measures as an obligation of means and not of result. To attribute, afterwards and once the consequences of the breach are known, the lack of implementation of the specific measures that, once the circumstances of the case are known, would have prevented the incident, turns the obligation contained in Article 32 of the GDPR into an obligation of result, contravening what is established by the Supreme Court.  
ENDESA argues that this is what the AEPD does in its Initiation Agreement, when it merely states that Endesa should have implemented the security measures that, once the incident occurred, it decided to implement to improve the security of the tools (turning the obligation of Article 32 of the GDPR into an obligation of result).  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it does not consider that the obligation to implement security measures imposed by data protection regulations has a nature of an obligation of result and not of means.  
However, it is equally true that ENDESA did not have, before the incident occurred, measures that “in accordance with the state of technology and in relation to the nature of the processing carried out and the personal data in question, reasonably allow for the avoidance of their alteration, loss, processing, or unauthorized access.”  
It cannot be understood that it was not in accordance with the state of technology or the processing of personal data in question that a large company like ENDESA, with a business volume of billions of euros and that continuously processes data of millions of people, did not consider it reasonable to implement, at a minimum, for example, as it later did, a multifactor user authentication system, nor that it prevented a user from having multiple sessions initiated at the same time, nor to deactivate or reset immediately (without taking months) users who had the slightest suspicion of being compromised, nor did it have a system that allowed for the traceability of the activity carried out by the aforementioned users, among other possible measures.  
Regarding the Business Impact Analysis documents of the application ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and ***HERRAMIENTA.1, in the “Impact-CheckList” tab, a value of 4 (Medium-High Impact) is assigned to the possibility that the loss of confidentiality and integrity of personal data could result in fines of millions of euros, but nothing is said about possible measures to prevent users of these applications from being compromised.  
As for the document “Assurance check report,” with the report of the results obtained in the Ethical Hacking to detect possible vulnerabilities in the application ***HERRAMIENTA.2, there is also no reference to the possibility of compromised users or to the possible measures to be implemented to prevent or...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
74/214  
to resolve such a situation. A number of other issues have been analyzed, but none related to this matter that gave rise to the present proceedings. Furthermore, the last Ethical Hacking conducted on the application dates back to May 2021, prior to the events that are the subject of the present proceedings, which reveal possible vulnerabilities. Therefore, it cannot be argued that it was an adequate preventive measure when no similar event occurred after the publication of the announcements on Facebook.  
Thus, this allegation is dismissed.  
(b) The AEPD understands that several months passed since Endesa identified the possible improvements it could implement regarding the security measures of the tools  

***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2  

and implemented them.  

Endesa argues that the implementation of additional security measures in the systems is not a simple task, and that it usually has its determined and marked timelines, since: (i) the development of new functionalities or measures usually has a time valuation in hours that cannot be shortened; and (ii) the implementation of new measures does not usually depend solely on Endesa, but also on its infrastructure or software providers.  
In this regard, concerning ***TOOL.1, Endesa highlights that the implementation projects for the possible improvements identified totaled a time valuation of 1,055 hours, so it is normal for the total implementation duration to last 3 months, despite being given the highest priority (attached as Document number 1 is the detailed explanation of each of the developments, as well as their hourly valuation).  
Endesa explains that ***TOOL.2 is developed on the CRM of ***PLATFORM.1. ***PLATFORM.1 is a SaaS (Software as a Service) on which Endesa can develop multiple functionalities, such as the deactivation of multiple logins that it decided to implement after learning the details of the incident. However, this does not mean that Endesa could deactivate this functionality immediately, but rather that it was necessary to carry out specific software development for this, which involved: (i) inventorying technical integration users; (ii) inventorying batch processes and execution users; and (iii) analyzing the use cases that can be given to an agent. In turn, all this development involves overcoming several phases that cannot be completed in less than 3 months (attached as Document number 2 is the detailed explanation of each of the developments and tasks necessary for the implementation of this security measure).  
Endesa argues that the time elapsed from when the possible security improvements were identified until they were implemented is not due to a lack of diligence on Endesa's part in their development (these developments were given the highest priority), but to the fact that this is the usual and reasonably expected time for the implementation of this type of improvements.  
Additionally, Endesa considers that the fact that, despite the effective implementation of the measures requiring time, these had already been identified in detail and proposed in the Endesa Committee demonstrates great diligence on its part.

www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
75/214  
Date: September 13, 2021, at which time the incident did not yet qualify as a Notifiable Data Security Breach.  
In this regard, this Agency acknowledges that the developments implemented take time and that a period of three months may be a reasonable timeframe for this (although it is equally true that, as indicated in the expert report submitted on July 3, 2023, the deployment of multi-factor authentication for users on ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 was completed on March 23, 2022, that is, six months after such a measure had been identified in the Committee's report of September 2021, double the estimated time).

Neither were the compromised users reset or removed immediately from ***HERRAMIENTA.1 or ***HERRAMIENTA.2, and despite the fact that no additional development was needed to remove them, it took months, which allowed unauthorized access to the personal data owned by ENDESA during that entire time and facilitated a large number of fraudulent registrations. A preventive reset of all users of the systems was also not carried out in light of a potential threat (which later turned out to be true).  
On another note, despite what was indicated by FB SPAIN, ENDESA did not contact FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED through any of the available means to request the removal of the published ads offering access to its systems.  
For all the above reasons, this Agency considers that ENDESA's actions have been severely negligent, and therefore, this allegation is dismissed.

(d) The AEPD understands that Endesa was not diligent in contacting Facebook for the removal of the ads since, after receiving the response indicating that Facebook Spain was not the competent entity, it did not send any request to Facebook Ireland Limited in this regard and, therefore, did not implement the proposed security measures.  

:  
ENDESA reiterates that the Initiation Agreement errs in reaching this conclusion since: (i) the decision not to contact Facebook Ireland Limited was made by Endesa consciously to not expressly acknowledge Irish jurisdiction (not due to a lack of diligence); and (ii) the proposed security measures were indeed implemented by Endesa, with great diligence and even prior to sending the first burofax to Facebook Spain, by disabling the credentials of the compromised users so that, although the ads remained active on Facebook, the sold/rented users could no longer access Endesa's systems.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates what was stated in section b) of the previous allegation in the document of allegations to the initiation agreement of this procedure, and therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
(e) The AEPD understands that Endesa took several months to delete the accounts of the compromised users from the Company's systems.  

:  
ENDESA claims that, in the Initiation Agreement, it is alleged that Endesa took several months to delete the accounts of each of the compromised users from the Company's systems.  
In this sense, ENDESA clarifies that, once the account of each user is disabled or reset, the user cannot access it or use it for any purpose, remaining permanently expelled from the system.  
Therefore, the fact that the account has not been completely deleted from ENDESA's systems is due to the purpose of avoiding orphaned records and the loss of  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
77/214  
critical business information, without this meaning that the user could regain access to it as seems to be implied by the Initiation Agreement.  
This is indicated by ***PLATFORM.1 itself in its help manual, which states: “The removal of a user from ***PLATFORM.1 affects many processes within the organization. After leaving the organization, you obviously do not want the user to retain access to their account. However, simply deleting a user can result in orphaned records and the loss of critical business information. For these reasons, deactivating rather than deleting the user is the appropriate action to take. Deactivation removes the user’s login access but retains all activity and historical records, facilitating the transfer of ownership to other users.”  
In other words, ENDESA explains that, for the reasons stated, the fact that a period of time elapsed until the complete deletion of credentials after they had been disabled does not mean, in any case, that these users could continue to access the ***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2 systems. Nor does it mean that other unidentified users who were not flagged as compromised could access the systems, since ENDESA proceeded to implement multifactor authentication measures as well as the deactivation of multi-session access. All of this made any unauthorized access unfeasible, regardless of whether Facebook ads remained published.  
In this regard, this Agency certainly understands that, once the account of each user has been disabled or reset, they cannot access it or use it for any purpose.  
However, what is being attributed to ENDESA is that it delayed for months in disabling or resetting the compromised users, as has been extensively explained in the response to point b) of the previous allegation in the statement of objections to the initiation agreement of this procedure.  
This Agency also wishes to highlight that, regarding ***TOOL.1, the dates of disabling/resetting users and the deletion of users were provided separately, but that, regarding ***TOOL.2, only the date of user deletion was provided, so it is not evidenced that there was any resetting or disabling.  
In any case, and as has been evidenced throughout the procedure, ENDESA delayed for months in disabling or deleting the compromised users, which allowed access to its systems for months, facilitating fraudulent registrations.  
For the reasons stated, this allegation is dismissed.  
2. Regarding the occurrence of violations  
ENDESA indicates that the Initiation Agreement includes a first proposal for a sanction for non-compliance with Article 5.1.f of the GDPR since “it is considered that ENDESA did not adequately guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data of its  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
78/214  
"ownership" and a second proposed sanction for non-compliance with Article 32 of the GDPR because "it is considered that ENDESA did not apply the appropriate technical and organizational measures to ensure a level of security adequate to the risk of a breach occurring as the one that took place."  
ENDESA argues that the same facts would lead, if the AEPD's criteria in said Initiation Agreement were maintained, to the imposition of two sanctions for the infringement of two different provisions, Article 5.1.f) and Article 32, both of the GDPR.  
And that this duplicity of sanctions constitutes a case of concurrence of punitive norms in the administrative sphere, specifically in what doctrine refers to as "concurrence of sanctioning administrative norms." The different types of concurrence of infringements and norms, whose exact delimitation presents some complexity, are linked to the institution of "non bis in idem." These are principles inherent to the criminal sphere (e.g., Article 8 of the Penal Code) and also apply to the sanctioning power of the administration in its material aspect and to the administrative sanctioning procedure in its procedural aspect, initially through jurisprudential application and, later, through express inclusion in some of its representations in positive law (e.g., in Article 29.5 of Law 40/2015, on the Legal Regime of the Public Sector - hereinafter LRJSP).  
ENDESA indicates that the GDPR itself contains a specific regulation of the concurrence of infringements in Article 83.3.  
ENDESA explains that the concurrence of sanctioning administrative norms occurs when a subject, with a single act or set of acts and a single injury to the same legal good, commits the type punished in both. According to doctrine and jurisprudence, for the concurrence of infringements to exist, a triple identity must be present: (i) of subject; (ii) of object; and (iii) of reason or foundation. In the present case, ENDESA considers that it is faced with unique facts, which are regarded by the AEPD as a lack of appropriate security measures to protect the personal data for which ENDESA was responsible. And that, even if it were accepted for mere dialectical purposes that such consideration were true, the Initiation Agreement identifies that such facts infringe two different provisions, one containing a general principle (principle of integrity and confidentiality) and the other containing a specific mandate (appropriate security measures).  
ENDESA emphasizes not only the duplicity of possible infringements but also the extreme identity of the legal good protected in both provisions. Thus, while Article 5.1.f) refers to the obligation to ensure "adequate security" of personal data through the application of "appropriate technical or organizational measures," Article 32 is the specification of that principle when it obliges the controller to apply "appropriate technical and organizational measures" to guarantee "an adequate level of security."  
ENDESA understands that the obligations are equivalent, even qualified with exactly the same indeterminate legal concepts (adequate security and appropriate measures). And it can even be concluded with the assertion that there are two equivalent provisions, one with a general approach and the other with more specific content, so that the same act could not be sanctioned twice, violating the principle of non bis in idem.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
79/214  
referring to the constitutional principle of “non bis in idem.” The consequence would be the impossibility of sanctioning the same facts for the infringement of Article 5.1.f) and Article 32 of the GDPR.  
ENDESA explains that, although “non bis in idem” is not expressly stated in the Spanish Constitution, the Constitutional Court has repeatedly declared that it must be understood as implicit in Article 25.1 of the CE, due to its close connection with the principle of punitive legality (see, among others, Judgment 2/1981, of January 30). Unlike what is provided in International Conventions on fundamental rights, such as the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union, in its Article 50.  
In any case, even if the full application of “non bis in idem” is not accepted on the grounds that the violated provision is not the same (which is formally the case), ENDESA understands that what undoubtedly occurs in the present case is a concurrence of punitive norms in which there is a triple identity of subject (the actor, Endesa), object (the argued insufficiency of appropriate security measures), and reason or legal foundation (the aim of ensuring an adequate level of security).  
When two punitive norms concur, ENDESA points out that the material “non bis in idem” prevents sanctioning twice for the same facts, although it is not regulated which one should be applied. There are different criteria in the jurisprudence for these cases: (i) the application of the special norm over the general norm; (ii) the application of the more severe sanctioning norm, following the criterion of Article 8 of the Penal Code; or (iii) in some cases, the courts have opted to give precedence to the administrative sanction that is imposed first, regardless of the specialty, a criterion that is not applicable to the case at hand.  
In ENDESA's general opinion, and in this sense, only the sanction related to the alleged breach of Article 32 of the GDPR should be imposed, and not the one corresponding to the alleged breach of the general principle of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR.  
All of this is for the hypothetical case in which the AEPD considers that there was indeed a breach of such article and does not take into account what was previously argued regarding the sufficiency of security measures.  
ENDESA understands that this is also the criterion resulting from the application of the methodology established in the Guidelines 4/2022 of the European Data Protection Board (“EDPB”) on the calculation of administrative fines under the GDPR. The EDPB indicates that different situations of concurrence of infringements and/or sanctions may arise, and that a case-by-case analysis is necessary. Following the methodology of the aforementioned Guidelines, the steps to be followed would be as follows (Chapter 3):  
- First, it must be analyzed whether we are dealing with a single fact (related to a single processing activity or linked activities). The criterion must be based on conduct carried out by the same subject, in a unified manner (even if it has different phases), close in space and time so that an external observer may consider it as a single activity. In the present case, ENDESA understands that it should be considered that we are dealing with a single conduct.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
80/214  
- Secondly, it is necessary to determine whether that conduct is sanctionable under two different provisions. In the present case, ENDESA understands that the answer is affirmative (possible infringements of Articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR).  
- Thirdly, it is necessary to determine which of the norms should prevail. ENDESA explains that, as cited by the CEPD in its Guidelines, community jurisprudence has sometimes opted for the principle of specialty (case C-10/18 Marine Harvest). For the application of the principle of specialty, there must be consistency in the objectives pursued by the two applicable norms, as is the case at hand. The consequence would be the application solely of the special norm, excluding the application of the general norm.  
Continuing with the process of choosing the applicable norm, and in the case where the principle of specialty is not taken into account, the next criterion employed by the CEPD, although in ENDESA's opinion it would not be as suitable as the previous criterion for this case, would be the criterion that the CEPD refers to as "unity of act," which is provided for in Article 83.3 of the GDPR, according to which "If a controller or a processor intentionally or negligently infringes diverse provisions of this Regulation for the same processing operations or linked operations, the total amount of the administrative fine shall not exceed the amount provided for the most serious infringements." In the present case, the consequence would be the application of the sanction corresponding to the infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR.  
In conclusion: ENDESA understands that the facts subject to the present procedure cannot be sanctioned simultaneously for infringement of Articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR, as this would be contrary to the principle of "non bis in idem"; if it is understood that the requirements to apply the aforementioned principle are not met, it is understood that there would be a situation of concurrence of sanctioning administrative norms, and the most appropriate criterion for choosing which of them should be applied would be the principle of specialty (“specialia generalibus derogant”); finally, and subsidiarily to all the above, in any case, sanctioning should only occur for the infringement that entails the most severe penalty.  

In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR is violated when there is a loss of confidentiality, integrity, or availability of personal data, which may or may not occur due to the absence or deficiency of security measures.  
This principle merely determines the channel through which the maintenance of confidentiality, integrity, or availability can be achieved when it specifies "through the application of appropriate technical and organizational measures," which are not strictly security measures.  
ENDESA concludes that the appropriate technical and organizational measures referred to in Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR are the security measures of Article 32 of the GDPR. However, this would simplify the essence of the GDPR, whose compliance is not limited to the implementation of technical and organizational security measures; it would mean, in  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
81/214  
In this case, the guarantee required is reduced through the principle of integrity and confidentiality to its achievement solely with security measures.  
This Agency reiterates that there are multiple technical or organizational measures that are not security-related and that the data controller can implement as a means to guarantee this principle.  
However, Article 32 of the GDPR includes the obligation to implement appropriate technical and organizational security measures to ensure a level of security adequate to the risk. Of security. Only of security.  
Furthermore, its objective is to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk, while in the case of Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR, confidentiality and integrity must be guaranteed. As can be seen, the two articles pursue different purposes, although they may be related.  
Regarding the examination of non bis in idem, the Judgment of the National Court of July 23, 2021 (rec. 1/2017) states that,  
“(...) According to the legislation and jurisprudence presented, the principle of non bis in idem prevents punishing the same subject twice for the same act based on the same grounds, understood as the same legal interest protected by the sanctioning norms in question. Indeed, when there is a triple identity of subject, act, and grounds, the sum of sanctions creates a sanction foreign to the proportionality judgment made by the legislator and materializes the imposition of a sanction not legally provided for, which also violates the principle of proportionality.  
But for "bis in idem" to be applicable, a triple identity must exist among the compared terms: objective (same facts), subjective (against the same subjects), and causal (for the same grounds or reason for punishment):  
a) The subjective identity implies that the affected subject must be the same, regardless of the nature or judicial or administrative authority that judges and irrespective of who the accuser is or the specific body that has resolved, or whether it is judged alone or in conjunction with others affected.  
b) The factual identity implies that the judged facts must be the same, and excludes cases of real competition of infringements where there is not a single unlawful act but several.  
c) The identity of grounds or causal identity implies that the sanctioning measures cannot coincide if they respond to the same nature, that is, if they share the same teleological foundation, which occurs between penal and administrative sanctions, but not between punitive and merely coercive measures.”  
Taking into account the above, in the present sanctioning procedure, the principle of non bis in idem has not been violated, since, although broadly understood, the facts are detected as a consequence of a violation of personal data security, the infringement of Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR is specified in a clear loss of confidentiality and integrity, while the infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR is reduced to the absence and deficiency of the security measures (only of security) detected, present independently of the violation of the personal data security in question. In fact, if these security measures (not appropriate to the risk, by the way) that ENDESA had implemented had been detected by this Agency without the loss of confidentiality and integrity of the personal data occurring, ENDESA would only have been sanctioned for Article 32 of the GDPR.  
And all this against the allegations made by ENDESA, which considers that both provisions require a single conduct, which is to implement adequate security. This is not true, since Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR is not limited to guaranteeing security appropriate to the risk, but to guaranteeing integrity and availability. And not only through security measures, but through all kinds of appropriate technical or organizational measures.  
As indicated, Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR sanctions a loss of availability and confidentiality only, and Article 32 of the GDPR sanctions the absence and deficiency of the security measures implemented by the data controller. Absent or deficient security measures that, in themselves, infringe the GDPR regardless of whether the loss of confidentiality has occurred.  
Therefore, the principle of non bis in idem is not considered violated in the present sanctioning procedure.  
Regarding Article 83.3 of the GDPR, it establishes that:  
“3. If a data controller or processor intentionally or negligently violates, for the same processing operations or related operations, various provisions of this Regulation, the total amount of the administrative fine shall not exceed the amount provided for the most serious infringements.”  
In the present case, a fine of 2,500,000 euros is proposed for the alleged infringement of Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR and a fine of 1,500,000.00 euros for the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR. In total, they would amount to 4,000,000 euros.  
The total annual global turnover of ENDESA has been established at ***AMOUNT.1 euros.  
Article 83.2 of the GDPR provides that:  
“5. Infringements of the following provisions shall be subject to administrative fines of up to 20,000,000 EUR or, in the case of an undertaking, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 4% of the total annual global turnover of the preceding financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
83/214  
a) the basic principles for processing, including the conditions for consent under Articles 5, 6, 7, and 9 (…)”.  
In the present case, 4% of the total annual global turnover of ENDESA would amount to ***AMOUNT.2 euros. Therefore, the sum of the proposed fines in this sanctioning procedure would not exceed this amount, and the proportionality rule of the GDPR has not been exceeded.  
Regarding ENDESA's reference to the provisions of Guidelines 4/2022 of the European Data Protection Board on the calculation of administrative fines under the GDPR, these do not apply to the present case, but rather to facts that occurred after the adoption of the aforementioned Guidelines.  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  

3. Violation of the principle of legality.  
ENDESA indicates that the Constitutional Court has declared that the fundamental right to legal sanctions enshrined in Article 25.1 of the Spanish Constitution includes a dual guarantee. One, of a formal and relative scope, refers to the rank that the defining norms must have (principle of legality); and another, of a material and absolute scope, relates to the requirement of the normative predetermination of unlawful conduct and the corresponding sanctions. This second guarantee is identified with the so-called principle of legality.  
Furthermore, the principle of legality contains, in turn, two mandates. The first of these, which ENDESA emphasizes for these purposes, is that of specificity (see, among others, Judgment of the Constitutional Court 297/2005, of November 21). Specificity requires a sufficient description of the behaviors classified as infractions and the corresponding sanctions in each case. The fact that it cannot be determined with sufficient certainty what conduct may constitute a breach would be a case of violation of that fundamental principle. Thus, ENDESA argues that Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR establishes a general principle that lacks specific concreteness through clear positive or limiting acts that the obligated party can know and comply with. On the contrary, it uses indeterminate legal concepts in a chained manner (“adequate security,” “appropriate measures”), which do not allow for a transparent understanding of the scope of obligations or prohibitions.  
Consequently, ENDESA understands that in the application of the norm, the administration must be extremely careful when identifying the facts constituting an infringement and explaining the reason why those facts fall under the poorly defined type.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that infringements in the field of data protection are classified in paragraphs 4, 5, and 6 of Article 83 of the GDPR. This is a classification by reference, fully accepted by our Constitutional Court. In this sense, Article 71 of the LOPDGDD also makes reference to them by stating that “Acts and behaviors that constitute infringements are those to which…”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
84/214  
referring to paragraphs 4, 5, and 6 of Article 83 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679, as well as those that are contrary to this organic law.”  
In this regard, the Opinion of the Council of State dated October 26, 2017, concerning the Draft Organic Law on the Protection of Personal Data states that “The European Regulation does indeed classify, albeit in a generic sense, the behaviors constituting an infringement: indeed, paragraphs 4, 5, and 6 of its Article 83, as transcribed above, contain a catalog of infringements for violation of the provisions of the European norm indicated in such paragraphs. Article 72 of the Draft Law rightly assumes the existence of such a catalog when it states that "the acts and behaviors that constitute a violation of the content of paragraphs 4, 5, and 6 of the European Regulation and of this organic law are considered infringements."  
In this case, as concluded by the Council of State, the European model of sanctions in the GDPR entails a wide range in the amount of the sanction, depending on the concurrence of the circumstances of Article 83.2, which are objective and verifiable by the courts. In fact, as is evident from the STC 150/2020, dated October 22, what is considered contrary to the principle of specificity and lex certa, the material aspect of Article 25 of the Spanish Constitution, is:  
entrusting such correspondence entirely to judicial or administrative discretion, “since this would amount to a mere blank authorization to the administration by a legal norm devoid of its own material content.”  
In the GDPR, a norm that establishes infringements and sanctions, such an undesirable circumstance as entrusting “entirely” (sic) to administrative discretion the correspondence between infringement and sanction does not occur. On the contrary. Article 83.4 GDPR and 83.5 GDPR already establish a first distinction regarding the amount of the sanctions to be imposed based on the infringed provisions. Article 83.6 GDPR, on the other hand, also properly delineates the infringement. Article 83.2 GDPR, furthermore, contains the circumstances that the European legislator considers that the supervisory authorities must take into account to determine the amount of the sanction, which are circumstances that do not depend on “administrative discretion,” but rather on an objective assessment, and reviewable by the courts.  
Therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
Second.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR  
ENDESA argues that it is essential, for the correct understanding of the matter, to clarify beforehand three concepts that cannot be confused or are interchangeable.  
- Security Breach: An irregular event that affects the systems and technological environments of a company from multiple possible areas. A security breach does not necessarily entail an impact on the information and does not entail...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
85/214  
necessarily affects the personal information that may be contained in the systems.  
-  
Personal Data Security Breach: occurs when a Security Breach also affects personal data stored or processed in the entity's information systems. According to Article 4.12 of the GDPR, it is “any breach of security that results in the accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, or alteration of personal data transmitted, stored, or otherwise processed, or the unauthorized communication or access to such data.”  
-  
Notifiable Personal Data Security Breach: is a Security Breach that: (i) in accordance with Article 33 of the GDPR, must be communicated to the supervisory authority (in this case, the AEPD) because it is likely to pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects; and (ii) in accordance with Article 34 of the GDPR, must be communicated to the affected data subjects because it is likely to pose a high risk to their rights and freedoms.  
As an important additional element for interpretation, it should be noted that Articles 33 and 34 of the GDPR refer to notifications of data security breaches and not to security breaches.  
ENDESA indicates that to determine compliance or non-compliance with the obligation contained in Article 33 of the GDPR, the first thing that must be examined is the moment when a data controller or processor is understood to be aware of a Notifiable Personal Data Security Breach, that is, a breach of data security that may constitute a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects. Once that moment has been determined, it must be established whether the notification was made within the timeframe established in Article 33 of the GDPR (72 hours).  
In this regard, and contrary to what is stated in the Initiation Agreement, ENDESA reiterates that it communicated the Personal Data Security Breach to the AEPD at the very moment it had sufficient knowledge of its scope and, therefore, as indicated in Article 33 of the GDPR, when it became aware: (i) that it affected personal data for which Endesa is responsible (i.e., that it was a Personal Data Security Breach); and (ii) that it could constitute a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects (i.e., that it was a Notifiable Personal Data Security Breach).  
It claims that for the aforementioned determination of the exact moment when it is understood that Endesa became aware of the Notifiable Personal Data Security Breach, it is essential to bring up again the chronological sequence of the main facts involving, in addition to Endesa, the companies ***EMPRESA.1 as an external provider of Endesa (processor for Endesa) and ***EMPRESA.9 as a provider of ***EMPRESA.1 and subcontractor of Endesa (sub-processor for Endesa).
