sedeagpd.gob.es
92/214  
The personal data was unlawfully acquired to extract data from Endesa customers, which was used to carry out fraudulent contracts.  
At that time, as stated above, Endesa was indeed aware, contrary to what is indicated in the Initiation Agreement on multiple occasions, that it had suffered a Data Security Breach, but did not consider, at that moment (given the limited information provided by ***EMPRESA.1, the lack of evidence regarding a possible risk to the affected parties, and the fact that Endesa had already taken all possible actions to make it unlikely that the Data Security Breach constituted a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties), that it was reportable in accordance with Articles 33 and 34 of the GDPR.  
With maximum diligence, Endesa constantly reevaluated, as indicated, its conclusion regarding the Data Security Breach. This reevaluation culminated, on February 10, 2022, with the issuance of the corresponding report already provided to the AEPD within the framework of this File, in which, after new evidence revealed a possible access by the compromised users to the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.2, it was concluded that, at that time, it was indeed necessary to notify the Data Security Breach to the AEPD as the requirements established for this in Article 33 of the GDPR were met.  
As previously indicated, until this analysis was conducted and this report was issued, Endesa was facing a Data Security Breach that could not be considered a reportable Data Security Breach as it did not reach the necessary threshold to be communicated to the AEPD.  
Therefore, ENDESA proceeds to analyze both theses:  
(i) Thesis of late notification:  
The AEPD establishes, throughout the Initiation Agreement, that: (a) Endesa denies having suffered a Data Security Breach until February 2022; and (b) Endesa should have notified the breach of personal data security to the AEPD on October 27, 2021, the date on which ***EMPRESA.1 informs it that some agents from ***EMPRESA.9 are carrying out fraudulent contracts.  
The first aspect (a) is fully rejected by Endesa. It is not true that Endesa denies having suffered a Data Security Breach. Endesa denies that the Data Security Breach met, until February 2022, the requirements established in the applicable data protection legislation for it to be mandatory to notify the AEPD.  
The second aspect (b) must also be fully rejected by ENDESA. Firstly, because the AEPD relies, to reach this conclusion, on a report prepared by a third party involved in the Data Security Breach and, therefore, completely biased, which was completely unaware of all the details of the Data Security Breach and the consequences that could arise from it for the affected parties. Secondly, because the AEPD takes this date  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
93/214  
as the date on which Endesa should have notified the Data Security Breach by equating, in violation of the provisions indicated by the GDPR and the Guidelines developed by the supervisory authorities (including the AEPD) and the European Data Protection Board, any Data Security Breach to a Notifiable Data Security Breach.  
(ii) Thesis of timely notification. Why Endesa's thesis on notification is the only legally admissible one:  
Contrary to what the AEPD argues, ENDESA has always claimed, throughout the entire informative administrative procedure, that it complied in a timely manner with the obligation to notify the Data Security Breach imposed by Article 33 of the GDPR. This is so because only 24 hours elapsed from the moment Endesa determined that the Data Security Breach was notifiable (February 9, 2022) to its effective notification through the electronic headquarters of the AEPD (January 10, 2022).  
As indicated by the “Guide for the management and notification of security breaches” published by the AEPD at the time, data controllers (in this case, Endesa) must follow an action plan in managing security breaches that begins, after the preparatory phase, with the detection and identification phase and continues with the analysis and classification phase before reaching, if applicable, the notification phase.  
This Guide was subsequently replaced, on May 25, 2021, by the current “Guide for the notification of personal data breaches.”  
In these phases prior to notification, data controllers must analyze the sources of detection of the incident, the nature, class, type, and level of risk faced by the organization in order to first determine whether they are dealing with a security breach or not.  
And the analysis does not end here. According to the aforementioned AEPD Guide, once the controllers are aware that they have suffered a data security breach, they must: (i) collect and analyze information related to the data security breach; (ii) classify the data security breach; (iii) investigate, communicate, and coordinate internal and external means; (iv) implement the response plan; and, finally, (v) determine the risk that such a data security breach may pose to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects in order to proceed, if that risk is high, to notify the competent authority of the data security breach.  
In this same sense, the “Guidelines on the notification of personal data breaches in accordance with Regulation 2016/679” developed by the Article 29 Working Party (now the European Data Protection Board) indicate, when analyzing when a controller should be understood to “be aware” that a data security breach has occurred, that:  
“The exact moment when a data controller can be considered to ‘be aware’ of a specific breach will depend on the circumstances of the case.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
94/214  
cha violation. In some cases, it will be relatively clear from the outset that a violation has occurred, while in others it may take some time to establish whether personal data has been compromised.”  
“After being informed of a possible violation by a person, a media outlet, or another source, or when the data controller itself has detected a security incident, it may initiate a brief investigation period to determine whether or not a violation has occurred. During this investigation period, it cannot be considered that the data controller ‘is aware’.”  
In a very similar manner, the security breach management protocol developed by Endesa (see Document number 5 attached to this writing) establishes a series of milestones or differentiated phases in the management of Security Breaches that, as could not be otherwise, range from analyzing the characteristics of the Breach to determine whether it can be considered a Data Security Violation, to analyzing and conducting a “risk assessment regarding the rights and freedoms of natural persons” to determine whether, due to its impact and relevance, the Data Security Violation should be reported to the supervisory authority.  
In this case, Endesa determined very early on that the Security Breach suffered in its systems constituted a Data Security Violation. Following this, and in accordance with the information it had access to, it evaluated and re-evaluated multiple times the risk that the affected parties could face as a consequence of the Data Security Violation, ultimately determining, on February 9, 2022, that the Data Security Violation should be reported to the AEPD (as it did 24 hours later).  
It is for all the above reasons that it understands that Endesa proceeded to communicate the Data Security Violation to the AEPD in a very short period of time from when it became aware that it was indeed facing a Notifiable Data Security Violation, since, until all information related to the incident was gathered and the corresponding analysis was conducted, Endesa was only facing a Data Security Violation regarding which it was unlikely to pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties (as has been demonstrated subsequently). Endesa's actions are therefore supported by the very Guide for the notification of personal data breaches currently prepared by the AEPD, which states: “It is not mandatory to notify all personal data breaches, as the GDPR provides for an exception to this obligation when, in accordance with the principle of proactive responsibility, the controller can ensure that it is unlikely that the personal data breach poses a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.”  
Taking all of the above into account, Endesa considers that there was, therefore, no infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR, and that no sanction would be appropriate.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that Article 33 of the GDPR is crystal clear in stating that:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
95/214  
“1. In the event of a breach of personal data security, the data controller shall notify the competent supervisory authority in accordance with Article 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, no later than 72 hours after becoming aware of it, unless it is unlikely that the breach would result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons. If the notification to the supervisory authority does not take place within 72 hours, it shall be accompanied by the reasons for the delay.”  
That is to say, any breach of personal data security must be communicated to the supervisory authority no later than 72 hours after becoming aware of it, except for those that are unlikely to pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.  
The GDPR does not refer to the degree of probability or risk that must exist for such a breach of personal data security to be reportable, in the words of ENDESA. It is sufficient that there is a probability (of any degree) that there is a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons (of any degree). It is also not necessary for that risk to have materialized for such an incident to be reportable; it is enough that it is probable.  
In this case, this Agency considers that ENDESA did not correctly qualify the incident by deeming it non-reportable and reiterates that the incident in question was reportable as soon as ENDESA became aware of it through announcements on Facebook regarding the possible breach. It can be highlighted, among other events, that on September 1, 2021, ***COMPANY.16 already informed ENDESA that D.D.D. was involved in the sale of credentials; on September 10, 2021, ENDESA was already aware, due to the investigation of the incident, of the veracity of the announcement and the identification of the credentials used fraudulently to access the Endesa application ***PLATFORM.1.endesa.es; on October 20, 2021, ***COMPANY.1 informed ENDESA that fraudulent registrations had occurred; and especially following the communication from ***COMPANY.1, ENDESA learned that five users of ***TOOL.1 had been compromised and that there had been improper use of personal data held in its systems, which occurred on October 27, 2021. However, the breach was not reported until February 10, 2022. Regardless of whether the users had been reset or deleted (which has already been sufficiently analyzed as not having been done immediately or diligently), the reality is that the incident had already occurred, the confidentiality of the personal data owned by ENDESA had been lost, and there was a probable risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, given that ENDESA had lost control over access to that data, regardless of whether there had been (or not) unauthorized access, in addition to ***TOOL.2. Therefore, this Agency reiterates that in this case, ENDESA should have notified the breach of personal data security within a maximum period of 72 hours from the moment it became aware that the incident had occurred, as stated in the Guidelines 1/2021 on examples of notification of breaches of personal data security, from the European Data Protection Board, adopted on December 14, 2021, version 2.0, when describing the breach caused by the copying of commercial data from the company's database by its employee:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
96/214  
“74. Although the sole objective of the former employee who maliciously copied the data may be limited to obtaining the contact information of the company's clientele for their own commercial purposes, the data controller is not in a position to consider that the risk to the affected data subjects is low, as the data controller has no guarantees regarding the employee's intentions. Thus, although the consequences of the security breach could be limited to exposure to unsolicited self-marketing by the former employee, further and more serious misuse of the stolen data cannot be ruled out, depending on the purpose of the processing established by the former employee.”  

In this regard, it should be noted that the notification of the breach should not take place after the data controller has conducted a detailed examination of the situation, but rather when the controller detects that the breach has occurred, with this analysis being able to take place in parallel with the notification, as stated in the aforementioned Guidelines 1/2021 on examples of notification of personal data security breaches:  
“8. Data security breaches are problems in themselves, but they can also be symptoms of a vulnerable and possibly outdated data security regime; they may also indicate system deficiencies that need to be addressed. As a general truth, it is always better to prevent security breaches by preparing in advance, as many of their consequences are, by nature, irreversible. Before a data controller can fully assess the risk arising from a breach caused by some type of attack, the root cause of the problem must be identified in order to determine whether the vulnerabilities that led to the incident are still present and, therefore, whether they can still be exploited. In many cases, the data controller may determine that the incident may pose a risk, and thus must notify it. In other cases, it is not necessary to postpone the notification until the risk and impact of the breach have been fully assessed, as the complete risk assessment can occur in parallel with the notification, and the information thus obtained can be provided to the supervisory authority in phases without undue delay.  
9. The breach must be notified when the data controller considers that it is likely to pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subject. Data controllers must carry out this assessment at the moment they become aware of the breach. The data controller should not wait for a detailed forensic examination and (early) mitigation measures before assessing whether the data breach is likely to pose a risk and, therefore, must be notified.  
10. If a data controller self-assesses the risk as unlikely, but it turns out that the risk materializes, the competent supervisory authority may exercise its corrective powers and decide to impose sanctions.”  

And it is reiterated in the Guide for the notification of personal data breaches by the AEPD  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
97/214  
“(...) when at the time of notification it was not possible to fulfill the obligation to provide all necessary information, the GDPR provides that the information will be provided gradually, as soon as possible and without delay. In general, the Spanish Data Protection Agency allows for the possibility of making an “initial” notification before the indicated 72 hours, by filling out the form with the preliminary information available, or, if applicable, the preliminary estimates regarding the personal data breach. Before the maximum period of 30 days from the initial notification, the data controller must complete all the information by means of a “modification” of the previous notification, including the decision made regarding the communication of the personal data breach to the affected individuals (...)” Furthermore, such notification must take place without undue delay and no later than within 72 hours from the moment the personal data breach is known, so that this Agency can exercise the functions assigned to it.  
According to Recital 87 of the GDPR:  
“(87) It must be verified whether all appropriate technological protection has been applied and whether the necessary organizational measures have been taken to immediately determine whether a personal data security breach has occurred and to inform the supervisory authority and the data subject without delay. It must be verified that the notification has been made without undue delay, taking into account, in particular, the nature and severity of the personal data security breach and its consequences and adverse effects for the data subject. Such notification may result in an intervention by the supervisory authority in accordance with the functions and powers established by this Regulation.”  
This issue is also addressed in the Guide for the notification of personal data breaches by the AEPD, specifying that, after notifying a personal data breach, the data controller may receive various communications or electronic notifications from the AEPD, for example:  
“• Communication with information regarding the registration of the notified personal data breach.  
• Notification with a request for additional information regarding the personal data breach or the processing of personal data in question under the functions and powers of this Agency referred to in Article 47 of the LOPDGDD, as well as Article 58 of the GDPR.  
• Notification with an order to inform the affected individuals of the personal data breach under Article 34.4, considering that the risk to the affected individuals is high, under the functions and powers of this Agency referred to in Article 47 of the LOPDGDD, as well as Article 58 of the GDPR.”  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  
Third.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR  
The AEPD indicates in the Initiation Agreement that Endesa, at the time of making the corresponding notification regarding the personal data security breach to the...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
98/214  
affected parties did not provide all the information required by Article 34 of the GDPR.  
First of all, Endesa emphasizes that the notification made to the affected parties was carried out solely in compliance with the corresponding requirement from the AEPD dated March 4, 2022, since, from the analysis of the incident conducted by Endesa, it was determined that the security breach did not pose a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals, as was subsequently demonstrated (in its opinion).  
In fact, as has been evidenced (in its opinion), Endesa only received a single response to the notification made, and none of the affected parties suffered significant inconveniences or very significant or irreversible consequences as a result of the breach, since: (i) Endesa implemented sufficient mechanisms to mitigate the consequences of the breach, assuming all management costs; and (ii) all affected parties were personally attended to by the Endesa team and were adequately informed about the scope of the breach, its consequences, and the possible mitigation measures that could be implemented in a personalized and specific manner based on the characteristics of the user.  
Once again, the report provided by ***COMPANY.1, whose validity and rigor Endesa disputes, and which the AEPD uses, among other arguments, to justify the proposal of a sanction that Endesa considers inappropriate, errs in its conclusions by stating that “the level of severity of the consequences for the affected individuals can be considered high as they could face energy supply cuts, stress, and additional costs.” Even if it were not taken into account that, as has been evidenced (in its opinion), Endesa acted diligently to minimize the consequences that could arise from the breach for the affected parties, in no case would they have faced consequences as severe as energy supply cuts, as described in the report of ***COMPANY.1, since, in accordance with the applicable energy regulation, these do not occur automatically nor can they be carried out without prior notice to the affected parties.  
In fact, by the date of receipt of the AEPD's requirement, Endesa had already taken further measures to ensure that there was no longer a probability of the high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals that the AEPD had identified (for example, it had terminated the contract with ***COMPANY.1 and its subcontractors so that they could not carry out additional fraudulent contracts) and was in contact with many of the affected parties to address their concerns and attend to their requests, thus fulfilling the condition set forth in Article 34.3 b) of the GDPR to determine that communication to the affected parties is not necessary. Notwithstanding the above and in order to show its utmost cooperation with this Agency and in an exercise of transparency that is clearly unnecessary from a regulatory standpoint, Endesa decided to comply with the AEPD's requirement and provide the requested information to the affected parties.  
To this end, and since Endesa's intention was to attend to each of the affected parties individually (as it had been doing since it became aware of the first fraudulent contracts and continued to do so after the...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
99/214  
Regarding the notifications of the Data Security Breach, Endesa opted, as inferred from the content of the notification, to provide the information related to the Data Security Breach required by Article 34 of the GDPR in a two-layer format, similar to that validated by the LOPDGDD.  
In the first layer (the notification itself), useful information for the affected parties is provided, such as the description of the Data Security Breach and its nature (“unauthorized access to certain commercial systems of Endesa”), the confirmation that Endesa had implemented, prior to the time of the notification, “the appropriate security measures, both technical and organizational, to prevent a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms” of the affected parties (as mentioned in the previous paragraphs, at the time of the notification, the high risk assessed by the AEPD could no longer exist since the appropriate measures had already been taken to mitigate it), and the contact address of ***PUESTO.2 through which one can “learn the details of the measures taken or obtain more information.”  
In the second layer, to which the affected parties: (i) could access, if they wished, by contacting ***PUESTO.2; or (ii) would have access once Endesa contacted them to address, in a personalized manner, their situation and reverse, if applicable, any fraudulent contracting they may have suffered, the Company would provide the affected parties with the remaining information required by Article 34 of the GDPR in a personalized manner, since not all affected parties could suffer the same consequences following the Data Security Breach nor were they affected in the same way, so the possible risk mitigation measures would vary depending on the specific case.  
Through this layered information system, Endesa ensured not only that information about possible consequences and mitigation measures was provided in a personalized manner and appropriate to the specific case as mentioned above, but also that the affected parties were attended to by a team, that of ***DEPARTAMENTO.4 of Endesa specialized in the commercial field, specialized in data protection matters and knowledgeable about the possible consequences that the Data Security Breach could have on the rights and freedoms of the affected parties.  
In this regard, it is of utmost importance to highlight that any communication or inquiry that the affected parties may have sent to the DPO mailbox provided in the communication (***EMAIL.1) would be addressed in a particular and individualized manner by ***DEPARTAMENTO.4 of Endesa specialized in the commercial field (hereinafter, “***DEPARTAMENTO.4”). This demonstrates the proactivity and diligence displayed by Endesa in providing the affected parties with all the necessary information related to the Security Breach, which task was centralized in ***DEPARTAMENTO.4. This department was authorized to attend to and respond in a personalized manner to the inquiries that, if applicable, had been directed by the affected parties. However, it should be noted that, as Endesa demonstrated in its response to the request received on June 27, 2022, ***DEPARTAMENTO.4 only received one communication, the inquiry of which was not raised by the affected party.  
It is for this reason that Endesa decided to carry out a notification of the Data Security Breach in two layers, to avoid conveying to all affected parties information...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
100/214  
generic information that may not be applicable to your specific case. In either of the two previous cases (proactive action by the interested party at an initial moment or subsequent proactive action by Endesa), the interested parties would have access to the rest of the information required by Article 34 of the GDPR, such as the details of the types of affected data, the description of the specific security measures implemented by Endesa to mitigate potential adverse effects, and the description of the possible consequences that the Data Security Breach suffered by Endesa could cause them. As can be seen, none of these points is extrapolable to all affected parties, and it is for this reason that Endesa, in order to achieve effective communication of the incident, decided to carry it out in two layers (one more generic and another individualized).  
In this regard, the AEPD itself, in its infographic on how to carry out the corresponding notifications of personal data breaches to the interested parties, indicates that it is necessary to take into account that the notification "does not imply a single action or act" and that, therefore, "it can be executed in different actions depending on the information that the controller has about the magnitude of the breach and the affected persons" (the underlining and bold text are ours).  
Endesa did so in this case to achieve more effective communication with the affected interested parties, taking into account the characteristics of the Data Security Breach.  
Furthermore, it is worth noting that both ***DEPARTMENT.6 of Endesa and ***DEPARTMENT.7 conducted an audit and review procedure of all the contracts managed by ***COMPANY.9 during the period from June 1 to October 15, 2021. This is because, as has been demonstrated, ***COMPANY.1 did not provide Endesa with complete and detailed information about the Security Breach; as a control measure and being highly protective, Endesa decided to review all the contracts processed by ***COMPANY.9 over a generous period of time.  
Therefore, in the interest of objectivity and in terms of strict legal defense, in light of the statements made by ***COMPANY.1 in its response to the AEPD's request regarding its proposal "to Endesa to make calls to customers who may have been affected to confirm both the legality of the contracting and the verification of the account number provided," but, "Endesa prohibited it," it should be noted that Endesa did carry out the review and individualized attention of each contract processed by ***COMPANY.9.  
These tasks were delegated to independent providers of ***COMPANY.1 and to ***DEPARTMENT.7.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that the necessity or otherwise of communicating the breach of personal data security in question has already been analyzed, and it was concluded that it was necessary to communicate it to the affected parties, which is why a resolution dated March 7, 2022, was issued ordering ENDESA to do so, in compliance with the provisions of Article 34 of the GDPR. Therefore,  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
101/214  
The communication to the interested parties regarding this incident by ENDESA was mandatory compliance and not an exercise of "collaboration" on their part.  
From the fact that only one response was received to the sending of such communication (by the way, in which it is indicated that this person had no relationship with ENDESA), it cannot be inferred that the incident in question did not constitute a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties. Rather, it could be inferred that no response was received from the affected parties because the communication was deficient and did not clearly indicate that a violation of the security of the personal data of these affected parties had occurred, which could lead to a series of unwanted consequences for them.  
It cannot be stated categorically, as ENDESA does, that "none of the affected interested parties suffered significant inconveniences or very significant or irreversible consequences as a result of the breach," since ENDESA itself acknowledged that it was unable to know the specific number of unauthorized accesses or their extent. Simply put, there is no record that any such occurrences had taken place.  
Regarding ENDESA's assertion that "all affected interested parties were attended to personally by the Endesa team and were adequately informed of the scope of the breach, its consequences, and the possible mitigation measures that could be implemented in a personalized and specific manner based on the characteristics of the user," it seems that ENDESA is referring to those affected by the actions of ***EMPRESA.1/***EMPRESA.9. However, the scope of the incident in question exceeded such actions, as the reality is that more users were compromised than those communicated by these providers, and the personal data contained in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 were exposed to more individuals, not only to those working in these companies. Therefore, it is not true that all affected interested parties were attended to.  
Furthermore, ENDESA itself states that only one communication was received as a result of the communication made to the affected parties, which suggests that personalized attention would only have occurred regarding those individuals in whom a fraudulent account was detected as a result of the actions of ***EMPRESA.1/***EMPRESA.9. In any case, it has not been proven throughout this procedure that such personalized attention took place.  
Regarding the reference to the report from ***EMPRESA.1 in which it is stated that one of the consequences could be the cutting off of the energy supply, in addition to stress and additional costs, while it is true that such supply cannot be cut off automatically or without prior notice, it is equally true that even having notified, if the person in question does not pay what is owed, even if the account was fraudulently opened, the supply would indeed be cut off. And it is also true that, in any case, once that person became aware that ENDESA was claiming a certain debt for a service they did not request, that individual would be subjected to a situation of stress and would have to face a series of expenses that, had the violation of the security of their personal data not occurred, would not have taken place. All this without forgetting that the data could be subjected to other uses whose consequences ENDESA was unaware of, thus the risk to the rights and freedoms of individuals was high.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
102/214  
Regarding the fact that ENDESA had already taken further measures to ensure that there was no longer a likelihood of a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected party, such as terminating the contract with ***EMPRESA.1 and its subcontractors and being in contact with many of the affected parties due to the actions of such companies, it cannot be understood that these were measures that eliminated the high risk for the affected parties, given that the scope of the incident was greater than what the employees of those companies had carried out. Therefore, not all unauthorized accesses were through ***EMPRESA.1 and its subcontractors, nor was it only the confidentiality of the data of those individuals who were fraudulently registered that was at risk; rather, the confidentiality of all personal data from the ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 systems of ENDESA was compromised, which is why this Agency ordered ENDESA to send the aforementioned communication to all affected parties.  
Regarding the information provided by ENDESA, without assessing the appropriateness of using a layered model, the following information was provided to the affected parties:  
“We are contacting you to inform you that we have detected a possible unauthorized access to certain commercial systems of Endesa Energía and that from the moment we became aware of this fact, we have adopted the appropriate security measures, both technical and organizational, to prevent a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms of our clients, ensuring that the confidentiality and integrity of your personal data has not been compromised.”  
This information provided is not truthful, as the confidentiality of the personal data of the affected parties had already been compromised (and perhaps in some cases, the integrity as well). It is also not true that the measures adopted prevented a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms of the affected parties. The only thing that ENDESA could prevent was, at most, fraudulent contracting with ENDESA. However, the personal data that may have been accessed could be used for other purposes, such as obtaining more personal data from these affected parties through social engineering and impersonating their identity in other situations.  
In any case, it seems that ENDESA forgets that Article 34 of the GDPR does not require that damage must have occurred, but rather that it is about communicating to the affected parties that there could be a risk to their rights and freedoms, for example, being a victim of phishing, another cyberattack, or a change of supply company. There is a very high risk of being a victim of identity theft and fraud, as this Agency is more than accustomed to seeing in its day-to-day operations. And this information is not derived from the communication made by ENDESA.  
Finally, regarding the fact that ENDESA reviewed all the contracts managed by ***EMPRESA.9 between June 1 and October 15, 2021, this Agency understands that this was what it was supposed to do as the data controller and has nothing more to add.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
103/214  
For the reasons stated, the present allegation is dismissed.  
Fourth.- On the inadmissibility of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR  
ENDESA argues that when working with suppliers located outside the European Economic Area and, therefore, carrying out international data transfers, it implements adequate safeguards in accordance with Articles 44 and 46 of the GDPR, contrary to what is indicated in the Initiation Agreement.  
ENDESA indicates that it is truly surprising that the AEPD, in its Initiation Agreement, proposes the imposition of an alleged infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR, when in none of the four (4) requests for information sent to Endesa (three directed directly to the Company and one to its DPO) over the more than seven (7) months that the preliminary investigation proceedings of the present case have lasted, this Agency has requested any proof from Endesa of the implementation of adequate safeguards for the corresponding international transfers.  
Had it done so, Endesa, as it does through the written allegations to the initiation agreement of the present procedure, would have provided this Agency with the corresponding Standard Contractual Clauses signed with its suppliers located outside the European Economic Area, in compliance with its internal procedure for carrying out international transfers (Document number 6).  
Notwithstanding the above and in order to prove the adequate implementation of sufficient safeguards for carrying out international transfers in accordance with Articles 44 and following of the GDPR, the aforementioned written allegations include, as Documents numbers 7, 8, and 9, the corresponding Standard Contractual Clauses signed with the suppliers ***COMPANY.15 (...), ***COMPANY.12 (...) and ***COMPANY.9 (...).  
In this regard, the following are attached: (i) the Standard Contractual Clauses signed at the time of contracting; and (ii) the updated Standard Contractual Clauses in accordance with the Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/94 of the Commission, of June 4, 2021, which have been signed subsequently.  
ENDESA argues that prior to carrying out the corresponding international data transfers identified in the Initiation Agreement, it implemented sufficient safeguards in accordance with the applicable data protection regulations.  
Specifically, it signed with its processors and sub-processors located in ***COUNTRY.2 and ***COUNTRY.1 the corresponding standard contractual clauses approved by the European Commission as provided in Article 46.2 c) of the GDPR.  
For all these reasons, Endesa considers the proposed sanction for the alleged infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR contained in the Initiation Agreement to be inadmissible.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that Article 67 "Preliminary investigation proceedings" of the LOPDGDD establishes that "1. Before the adoption of the initiation agreement of the procedure, and once the complaint has been admitted for processing, if any, the..."  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
104/214  
The Spanish Agency for Data Protection may carry out preliminary investigation actions in order to achieve a better determination of the facts and circumstances that justify the processing of the procedure. In other words, these actions are not mandatory in any way; rather, it is at the discretion of this Agency to carry them out.  

Furthermore, in the present case, ENDESA has made international transfers to ***COUNTRY.1 and ***COUNTRY.2, and continues to make transfers to ***COUNTRY.2, countries with respect to which the Commission has not declared that they guarantee an adequate level of protection, in the terms set forth in Article 45 of the GDPR.  

Article 46 of the GDPR deals with "transfers subject to adequate safeguards" and states in its paragraph 1 that, in the absence of a decision under Article 45, paragraph 3, the data controller or processor may only transfer personal data to a third country or international organization if it has provided adequate safeguards and provided that data subjects have enforceable rights and effective legal remedies.  

Article 46, paragraph 2, of the GDPR adds that "adequate safeguards" may be provided, without the need for express authorization from a supervisory authority, among others, by standard data protection clauses adopted by a supervisory authority and approved by the Commission in accordance with the examination procedure referred to in Article 93, paragraph 2. (Article 46, paragraph 2, letter c).  

In this regard, the European Commission has adopted the Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914 of the Commission, dated June 4, 2021, concerning standard contractual clauses for the transfer of personal data to third countries in accordance with Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council.  

These standard contractual clauses adopted by the European Commission constitute a method for providing "adequate safeguards" in the absence of an adequacy decision under Article 46, paragraph 2, letter c), of the GDPR, establishing new standard contractual clauses that can be used by both the controller and the processor in order to offer adequate safeguards within the meaning of Article 46, paragraph 1 of the GDPR, with the previous decisions of 2001 and 2010 being repealed as of September 27, 2021. However, according to its clause 4.4, contracts entered into before September 27, 2021, in accordance with Decision 2001/497/EC or Decision 2010/87/EU, are considered to provide adequate safeguards within the meaning of Article 46, paragraph 1, of the GDPR until December 27, 2022, provided that the processing operations covered by the contract remain unchanged and that the standard contractual clauses ensure that the transfer of personal data is subject to adequate safeguards.  

Having established the above, it is worth highlighting that clause 14 of the decision refers to the "Law and practices of the country that affect compliance with the clauses." This clause contains four modules related to the transfer from controller to controller; transfer from controller to processor; transfer from processor to processor; and transfer from processor to controller, to which the provisions it contains apply. It establishes the following:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
105/214  
"MODULE ONE: transfer from controller to controller  
MODULE TWO: transfer from controller to processor  
MODULE THREE: transfer from processor to processor  
MODULE FOUR: transfer from processor to controller (only if the processor in the EU combines the personal data received from the controller in the third country with the personal data collected by the processor in the EU)  
a) The parties ensure that they have no reason to believe that the law and practices of the third country of destination applicable to the processing of personal data by the data importer, particularly the requirements for the communication of personal data or the measures for access authorization by public authorities, prevent the data importer from fulfilling the obligations imposed by this clause. This assertion is based on the premise that the law and practices that essentially respect fundamental rights and freedoms and do not exceed what is necessary and proportionate in a democratic society to safeguard one of the objectives listed in Article 23(1) of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 do not oppose this clause.  
b) The parties declare that, in providing the guarantee referred to in point a), they have duly taken into account, in particular, the following aspects:  
i) the specific circumstances of the transfer, such as the length of the processing chain, the number of parties involved, and the channels of transmission used; the anticipated further transfers; the type of recipient; the purpose of the processing; the categories and format of the personal data transferred; the economic sector in which the transfer takes place; the location of the storage of the transferred data;  
ii) the law and practices of the third country of destination—especially those requiring the communication of data to public authorities or authorizing access by such authorities—that are relevant given the specific circumstances of the transfer, as well as the applicable limitations and guarantees (12);  
iii) the relevant contractual, technical, or organizational guarantees provided to complement the guarantees set out in this clause, particularly including the measures applied during the transfer and processing of personal data in the destination country.  
c) The data importer ensures that, when carrying out the assessment referred to in point b), it has done everything possible to provide the data exporter with the relevant information and commits to continue collaborating with the data exporter to ensure compliance with this clause.  
d) The parties agree to document the assessment referred to in point b) and make it available to the competent supervisory authority upon request.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es"
106/214  
e) The data importer agrees to promptly notify the data exporter if, after being bound by this set of clauses and during the term of the contract, it has reason to believe that it is or has been subject to regulations or practices that do not comply with the requirements of letter a), even as a result of a change in regulations in the third country or a measure (such as a request for communication) indicating an application of such regulations in practice that does not comply with the requirements of letter a). [Module three: The data exporter will notify the controller.]  
f) If the notification referred to in letter e) is made or if the data exporter has reason to believe that the data importer can no longer fulfill the obligations attributed to it by this set of clauses, the data exporter will promptly determine the appropriate measures (for example, technical or organizational measures to ensure security and confidentiality) that must be adopted by the data exporter and/or the data importer to remedy the situation [module three: if applicable, after consulting the controller]. The data exporter will suspend the transfer of data if it considers that there are no adequate guarantees or if so provided by [module three: the controller or] the competent supervisory authority. In this case, the data exporter will be entitled to terminate the contract regarding the processing of personal data under this set of clauses. If the contract has more than two contracting parties, the data exporter may only exercise this right of termination with respect to the relevant party, unless the parties have agreed otherwise. In the event of termination of the contract under this clause, clause 16, letters d) and e) will apply.  
Thus, clause 14 establishes an obligation for both the exporter and importer to carry out an assessment that takes into account all the issues determined by the clause itself.  
Article 46.2.c) must be interpreted in light of the doctrine of the CJEU as set out in its judgment in Schrems II:  
“132 Since, as is evident from paragraph 125 of this judgment, it is inherent in the contractual nature of standard data protection clauses that they cannot bind public authorities of third countries, but that Articles 44 and 46, paragraphs 1 and 2, letter c), of the GDPR, interpreted in light of Articles 7, 8, and 47 of the Charter, require that the level of protection of natural persons guaranteed by that Regulation is not compromised, it may be necessary to supplement the guarantees provided for in those standard data protection clauses. In this regard, Recital 109 of the said Regulation states that ‘the possibility for [the] controllers […] of processing to rely on standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission […] should not prevent controllers […] from adding other clauses or additional guarantees’ and specifies, in particular, that ‘controllers […] should be encouraged to offer additional guarantees […] that complement the standard data protection clauses.’  
133 It is therefore evident that the standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission under Article 46, paragraph 2, letter c), of the same Regulation are intended solely to provide controllers or processors established in the Union with contractual guarantees that are…”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
107/214  
apply uniformly in all third countries and, therefore, regardless of the level of protection guaranteed in each of them. To the extent that such standard data protection clauses cannot provide, due to their nature, guarantees that go beyond a contractual obligation to ensure compliance with the level of protection required by Union law, such clauses may require, depending on the situation of a specific third country, the adoption of additional measures by the data controller to ensure compliance with that level of protection.  
134 In this regard, as the Advocate General pointed out in paragraph 126 of his conclusions, the contractual mechanism provided for in Article 46(2)(c) of the GDPR is based on the accountability of the data controller or processor established in the Union, as well as, subsidiarily, of the competent supervisory authority. It is therefore primarily the responsibility of that data controller or processor to verify, on a case-by-case basis and, if necessary, in collaboration with the recipient of the transfer, whether the law of the destination third country guarantees an adequate level of protection, in light of Union law, for the personal data transferred based on standard data protection clauses, providing, when necessary, additional guarantees to those offered by such clauses.  
In this context, the Recommendations 01/2020 on measures that complement transfer instruments to ensure compliance with the level of protection of EU personal data, adopted on November 10, 2020, must also be taken into account, in which, when interpreting the aforementioned ruling of the CJEU, it states that “the Court leaves open the possibility for exporters to apply complementary measures that mitigate these protection gaps, so that it reaches the level required by Union law. The Court does not specify what measures could be. However, the Court emphasizes that exporters will have to determine them on a case-by-case basis. This aligns with the principle of proactive accountability in Article 5(2) of the GDPR, which requires that data controllers be responsible and able to demonstrate compliance with the principles of the GDPR regarding the processing of personal data (…)”  
It should be noted that Endesa has provided, regarding the transfer to ***COUNTRY.1, carried out through ***COMPANY.12, a resolution of the contract between Endesa and ***COMPANY.16, dated November 15, 2022. It has also provided proof of the termination of ***COMPANY.9 as a supplier of Endesa, dated November 2, 2021. Therefore, these contracts were terminated before December 27, 2022.  
Regarding the transfer to ***COUNTRY.2, ENDESA has provided the Impact Assessment on Transfers to ***COUNTRY.2, which aims to evaluate, in relation to the transfer of personal data from the exporter to the importer, the level of adequacy of the destination third country, as well as the adoption of appropriate technical and organizational security measures to mitigate the risk of such transfer.  
In this regard, this assessment is limited to evaluating the technical and organizational security measures applied by the data importer, without addressing the necessity or otherwise of  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
108/214  
adopt additional contractual measures complementary to the standard contractual clauses that may be necessary.  
In this regard, it is noteworthy that, concerning the legislative assessment of the importing country, the evaluation yields the result "3 – Partially adequate," limited to considering the existence of data protection legislation and an independent supervisory authority in the third country, but it does not even analyze the functions and powers of this authority.  
Furthermore, it provides the document titled "analysis of legislation and practices" of ***COUNTRY.2, aimed at evaluating whether the personal data being transferred is adequately protected concerning the potential access of authorities in the countries to which the data is transferred. This document states that the Constitution of ***COUNTRY.2 guarantees the fundamental right to personal and family privacy and includes the necessity of a court order for the interception of communications. The existence of a data protection law is also taken into account in this document.  
From the analysis of this documentation, it cannot be asserted that the evaluation includes a sufficient analysis of the law and practices of ***COUNTRY.2 that would allow for an assessment of whether the destination third country offers an adequate level of protection. Thus, it does not contain any evaluation regarding the existence of norms that require the communication of data to public authorities or authorize access by such authorities, as well as the applicable limitations and guarantees. According to the aforementioned CJEU ruling, this analysis should include the elements set out in Article 45.2 of the GDPR, which includes the analysis of the existence of international treaties signed by the third country or other international commitments adopted (art. 45.2.c) GDPR), the study of the national legislation of the third country in the sense intended by Article 45.2.a) of the GDPR:  
“the rule of law, respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, relevant legislation, both general and sectoral, including that relating to public security, defense, national security, and criminal legislation, and access of public authorities to personal data, as well as the application of such legislation, data protection standards, professional standards, and security measures, including standards on further transfers of personal data to another third country or international organization observed in that country or international organization, case law, as well as the recognition of effective and enforceable rights and administrative remedies and judicial actions that are effective for the data subjects whose personal data are being transferred.”  
Analysis that is also reflected in recitals (20) of the Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914.  
“The parties must take into account, in particular, the specific circumstances of the transfer (such as the content and duration of the contract, the nature of the data transferred, the type of recipient, and the purpose of the processing), the law and practices of the destination third country that are applicable given the circumstances of the transfer, and the guarantees established to complement the guarantees provided in the standard contractual clauses (such as contractual measures,  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
109/214  
relevant technical and organizational measures applicable to the transfer and processing of personal data in the destination country). Regarding the effect of the mentioned law and practices on compliance with the standard contractual clauses, various elements can be assessed in a comprehensive evaluation; for example, reliable information on the practical application of the law (jurisprudence, reports from independent supervisory bodies, etc.), the existence or absence of requests in the same sector, and, under strict conditions, the documented practical experience of the data exporter and/or the data importer."  
The deficiencies observed in the Data Protection Impact Assessment of ***COUNTRY.2 as a country importing personal data prevent it from being considered that the guarantee of the aforementioned clause 14 has been provided.  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  
Fifth.- On the grading of the sanction imposed on Endesa and the relative lack of proportionality of the Initiation Agreement of the sanctioning procedure  
ENDESA argues, first of all, the strangeness regarding the criterion that the AEPD has followed when analyzing and ultimately proposing to sanction Endesa in comparison with other cases related to data security breaches against which sanctioning or investigation procedures have been initiated.  
For example:  
a. The resolution of the AEPD regarding procedure E/08809/2019 concluded with the filing of the proceedings, indicating that the investigated entity "had reasonable preventive technical and organizational measures in place to avoid this type of incident and in line with the level of risk," when that Data Security Breach:  
- affected approximately 22 million records worldwide (compared to the 760 affected individuals in the Data Security Breach of Endesa currently at hand);  
- involved the attackers extracting basic and economic-financial data such as credit card information, in addition to other data that were not compromised in the incident affecting Endesa, such as usernames and passwords of the affected individuals;  
- resulted in the data controller receiving up to eighty (80) information requests from the affected individuals, compared to the single request received by Endesa and provided in the context of the File; and  
- occurred even with the implementation of security measures.  
b. The resolution of the AEPD regarding procedure PS/00118/2020 concluded with the imposition of a warning sanction, considering that "in light of the complexity of the affected information systems, as well as the actions taken to minimize the negative consequences of the aforementioned breach of personal data security of its clients, it is deemed lawful not to impose a sanction consisting of an administrative fine," when in that Data Security Breach:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
110/214  
- The notification of the data security breach to the AEPD was made on August 28, 2019, when the investigated entity became aware that its customer information system had been subject to unauthorized access on July 10, 2019 (almost two months later);  
- The investigated entity in this case did not provide the AEPD with the risk analysis of the processing for which it is responsible, whereas Endesa did provide it, responding in a timely manner to this Agency on four (4) occasions when it was requested for information; and  
- The investigated entity stated that “the machine affected by the security incidents was technically obsolete,” and therefore did not have adequate security measures corresponding to the risk posed by the processing.  
c. The resolution of the AEPD regarding procedure E/05937/2021 concluded with the imposition of a warning sanction, considering that “no evidence has been found to support the existence of an infringement within the jurisdiction of the Spanish Agency for Data Protection,” when in said Data Security Breach:  
- The notification of the data security breach to the AEPD was made, in accordance with what was indicated by the AEPD in its resolution, “beyond the 72 hours from the moment it was known that it had occurred.” In this case, the AEPD indicates that the reasons for such delay have been properly substantiated, as the investigated entity claimed that the notification was delayed “due to the investigations carried out to clarify whether there was data access and/or fraudulent activity, which required multiple analyses by the security teams.” This reason could be considered similar to that alleged by Endesa. - The investigated entity in this case processes categories of data such as name, identification number, email address, economic and financial data, etc., as it is a credit card issuer. Therefore, the impact of the incident appears to be similar to that suffered by Endesa.

- It involved unauthorized access to the personal data of up to 30,000 affected individuals (compared to the 760 individuals affected by the Data Security Breach of Endesa that we are currently addressing); and  
- The AEPD considered that, from the investigations carried out, a lack of diligence by the responsible party in implementing security measures was evident.

On the contrary, it has been clearly harmed, as it has seen how third parties outside the organization accessed its systems and the data contained therein for illegitimate purposes.  
Among some of the harmful effects that Endesa has suffered as a consequence of the Data Security Breach are: (i) Endesa has had to dedicate significant resources to the evaluation, analysis, and management of the Data Security Breach; and (ii) it has required the implementation, by Endesa, of greater security measures, both technical and organizational, in order to try to prevent an attack of this nature from happening again, with the investment that such implementation entails.  
In its opinion, Endesa has not benefited in the slightest from the Data Security Breach but has been, along with the affected parties, the main victim of it. In this regard, Endesa believes that this fact should be considered by the AEPD as a mitigating circumstance regarding the conduct of the signing entity in relation to the grading of the sanction to be imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 32.1 of the GDPR.  
Firstly, regarding the alleged disparity of criteria of the AEPD, this Agency wishes to point out that each case is different and must be analyzed according to its own circumstances.  
Article 32.1 of the GDPR establishes that “(…) the controller and the processor shall implement appropriate technical and organizational measures to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk (…)”. That is to say, the probability and severity of the risks posed by the processing of personal data in the specific case must be evaluated, for the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, in order to adopt the measures that are appropriate to that specific case.  
There is no universal formula for this, nor even a general one, nor does a violation of Article 32 of the GDPR automatically occur in the event of a personal data security breach. It is a matter of analyzing in each case what risks a particular processing could entail for the data subjects and assessing what measures would have been implemented to prevent such a risk from occurring, in order to determine whether such measures would be appropriate for that specific case.  
Taking this into account, this Agency decided to archive the proceedings in the aforementioned cases E/008809/2019 and E/05937/2021, since there was no evidence that the measures adopted by the data controllers, before the incident and once it occurred, were not appropriate based on the existing risk in those processes. In the case of ENDESA, this is not the case; the measures adopted beforehand were not appropriate based on the risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, which is why this Agency decided to initiate sanctioning proceedings in order to sanction it for a possible infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR, among others.  
Regarding the delay in notifying this Agency of the personal data security breach, in the procedure E/05937/2021, Article 33.1 of the GDPR states that: “(…) If the notification to the supervisory authority does not take place within 72 hours, it shall be accompanied by an indication of the reasons for the delay.”  
At C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
115/214  
In the cited procedure, the data controller duly indicated the reasons for the delay in notifying the supervisory authority, which is why such article was not considered infringed, contrary to what occurs with ENDESA in the present procedure, where months passed since the incident should have been reported to this Agency without any justification for such delay, as was reasonably explained in the response to the second allegation in the written submissions regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
Regarding procedure PS/00118/2020, the incident (which lasted only three days and affected a maximum of 75,000 interested parties, compared to the ENDESA incident that lasted months and affected millions of interested parties, contrary to ENDESA's claims) was considered such that the degree of reproachability of the actions of the data controller allowed for the imposition of a warning instead of a fine, which is not the case in the present sanctioning procedure against ENDESA.  
In relation to procedure PS/00254/2019, a similar situation occurs, given that the incident in question did not affect millions of people and the circumstances of the breach differ from the severely negligent actions of ENDESA.  
Secondly, regarding the application of aggravating factors, the serious lack of diligence on the part of ENDESA has been evidenced throughout the file not by the report of ***COMPANY.1, but by all the documentation on record, all of which has been detailed in the proven facts and extensively explained in the legal grounds, especially in the response to the allegations made against the initiation agreement.  
Regarding the alleged infringement of Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR, ENDESA's conduct has been severely negligent as it took months to reset or delete the compromised users, which allowed access to personal data held in ENDESA's systems for months and enabled fraudulent user registrations. It was also severely negligent in requesting Facebook to remove the relevant ads, as it did not address FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED, as it was indicated to be the correct channel.  
Regarding the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR, ENDESA's severely negligent conduct is evident in its behavior before and after the incident occurred, as it had not even anticipated the possibility of misuse of the users of ***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2, and thus had not implemented adequate measures to ensure user authentication with the necessary guarantees, preventing unauthorized access to the systems and allowing for proper tracking of these users' activities in those systems.  
Compromised users were also not reset or deleted immediately from either ***TOOL.1 or ***TOOL.2; it took months, which allowed for unauthorized access to the personal data processed by ENDESA during that entire time and facilitated a large number of fraudulent registrations. There was also no preventive reset of all users in the systems in the face of a potential threat (which later turned out to be true).  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
116/214  
Furthermore, despite what was agreed on September 13, 2021, by the Security Gaps Committee and the measure adopted in August 2021 to request the removal of Facebook ads offering access, ENDESA did not follow the guidance provided by FB SPAIN, nor did it contact FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED through any of the available means to have the published ads offering access to its systems removed.  
Regarding the alleged violation of Article 33 of the GDPR, ENDESA's serious negligence is evident in that it took months to identify the violation of personal data security in question as reportable to the supervisory authority.  
And regarding the alleged violation of Article 34 of the GDPR, ENDESA's serious negligence is evident in that it did not properly identify that the incident in question needed to be communicated to the affected individuals. ENDESA did not even communicate the breach to those affected by the potential fraudulent registrations and, furthermore, once this Agency ordered it to issue the corresponding communication, it did not even inform properly.  
As for ENDESA's assertion that when it contacted Facebook Spain, the credentials of the compromised users had already been disabled, this is incorrect, as has been addressed in the response to section b) of the previous allegation in the written submission regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
Regarding ENDESA's demonstration that it notified this Agency of the incident within 24 hours of becoming aware that it could pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, this Agency refers to the response to the third allegation in the written submission regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
ENDESA has also not demonstrated that this incident could not result in a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects nor that it had duly provided the data subjects with the information required by Article 34 of the GDPR, as has been addressed in the responses to the second and third allegations in the written submission regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
Thirdly, regarding the consideration of the fact that ENDESA's activity is linked to the processing of personal data as an aggravating factor, this Agency wishes to point out that Article 83.2 of the GDPR states that “When deciding on the imposition of an administrative fine and its amount in each individual case, the following shall be duly taken into account: (…) k) any other aggravating or mitigating factor applicable to the circumstances of the case…”.  
In this regard, the Spanish legislator has considered including in Article 76 of the LOPDGDD that: “2. In accordance with the provisions of Article 83.2.k) of Regulation (EU) 2016/679, the following may also be taken into account:  
(…)  
b) The link of the infringer's activity with the processing of personal data.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
117/214  
This Agency simply takes into consideration that circumstance, provided by the legislator, when deciding on the imposition of the administrative fine.  
It is worth noting that, of course, the same consideration cannot be given for the purpose of deciding the imposition of an administrative fine for an infringement committed by a natural person or a small company unaccustomed to processing personal data, as for a large company like ENDESA, accustomed to processing personal data of millions of customers and non-customers, with a long history in this regard. It is certainly considered that the infringement is more serious for the purposes of imposing a fine if the data controller is among the latter, as is the case with ENDESA. This has been pointed out by the Audiencia Nacional in its SAN 65/2017, of February 7, 2017, when it collected the doctrine of the Supreme Court, "in assessing the degree of diligence, special consideration must be given to the professionalism or lack thereof of the subject, and there is no doubt that, in the case examined, when the activity of the appellant involves constant and abundant handling of personal data, it must be insisted upon the rigor and exquisite care to comply with the legal provisions in this regard."  
Fourthly, having reviewed the contracts made by ***EMPRESA.9 to prevent new fraudulent registrations cannot be considered a mitigating factor, as it is an obligation imposed by the regulations. For its part, the fact that certain security measures were adopted a posteriori to mitigate risks has already been assessed as a mitigating factor regarding the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR.  
Fifthly, the mitigating factor related to the degree of cooperation with the supervisory authority is not applicable, since it has already been extensively explained that more than 24 hours (in fact, months) had passed since ENDESA became aware that a data security breach had occurred that was reportable (this Agency refers to the response to the second allegation in the written submissions to the initiation agreement of this sanctioning procedure).  
Regarding ENDESA's response to each of the information requests sent by this Agency, it is not considered a mitigating factor since it is an obligation contemplated in the applicable regulations.  
In this regard, Article 52 "Duty of cooperation" of the GDPR states: "1. Public Administrations, including tax and Social Security authorities, and individuals shall be obliged to provide the Spanish Data Protection Agency with the data, reports, background information, and justifications necessary to carry out its investigative activity. (…)”  
And Article 72 "Very serious infringements" establishes that: "1. Based on what is established in Article 83.5 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679, the infringements that constitute one of this organic law when it is enforceable are considered very serious and shall prescribe after three years.  
(…)  
ñ) Failing to provide access to the competent data protection authority's personnel to personal data, information, premises, equipment, and processing means required by the data protection authority for the exercise of its investigative powers. (…)”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
118/214  
Finally, this Agency wishes to point out that the lack of benefits obtained by ENDESA cannot be considered a mitigating factor.  
This is in accordance with the Judgment of the National Court, dated 05/05/2021, rec. 1437/2020, which states: “On the other hand, it considers that the non-commission of a previous infringement should be appreciated as a mitigating factor. Well, Article 83.2 of the GDPR establishes that, among other things, the circumstance 'e) any previous infringement committed by the controller or the processor' must be taken into account for the imposition of the administrative fine. The absence of the prerequisite for its application constitutes an aggravating circumstance; therefore, it cannot be taken into consideration, but it does not imply or allow, as the claimant intends, its application as a mitigating factor.” Applied to the present sanctioning procedure, the lack of the prerequisite for its application regarding Article 76.2.c) of the LOPDGDD, that is, obtaining benefits as a consequence of the infringement, does not allow its application as a mitigating factor.  
This criterion of gradation is established in the LOPDGDD in accordance with the provisions of Article 83.2.k) of the GDPR, according to which administrative fines will be imposed taking into account any “aggravating or mitigating factor applicable to the circumstances of the case, such as the financial benefits obtained or the losses avoided, directly or indirectly, through the infringement,” understanding that avoiding a loss has the same nature for these purposes as obtaining benefits.  
If it is added that the sanctions must be “effective, proportionate, and dissuasive in each individual case,” as provided in Article 83.1 of the GDPR, admitting the absence of benefits as a mitigating factor is not only contrary to the factual prerequisites contemplated in Article 76.2.c), but also contrary to what is established in Article 83.2.k) of the GDPR and to the indicated principles.  
Thus, valuing the absence of benefits as a mitigating factor would nullify the dissuasive effect of the fine, as it diminishes the effect of the circumstances that effectively impact its quantification, providing the responsible party with a benefit to which they are not entitled. It would be an artificial reduction of the sanction that could lead to the understanding that infringing the regulation without obtaining benefits, financial or otherwise, would not produce a negative effect proportional to the seriousness of the infringing act.  
In any case, the administrative fines established in the GDPR, in accordance with what is provided in its Article 83.2, are imposed based on the circumstances of each individual case, and it is not considered that the absence of benefits is an appropriate and determining factor for assessing the seriousness of the infringing conduct.  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.

In relation to the allegations made regarding the proposed resolution of the present sanctioning procedure, responses are provided in the order presented by ENDESA:
