Preliminary.- Specification of certain essential factual points for the legal analysis of the file  
ENDESA reiterates that in the aforementioned resolution proposal, this Agency has identified issues that, while highly relevant for the assessment of ENDESA's conduct, have not been interpreted correctly by the AEPD.  
(a) The importance that both the incident and the protection of personal data have had and continue to have for Endesa  

It is alleged that the incident and data security breach suffered by Endesa, which resulted in its notification to the AEPD, was addressed from the very beginning with the utmost rigor and seriousness, and received maximum attention in all analyses conducted. Information security is a matter of vital importance for Endesa.  
Although the Resolution Proposal refers to a “scandalous lack of diligence” on the part of Endesa in managing and implementing remediation measures for the data security breach, and despite the fact that the instructing body of the file continues to apply the aggravating factor of “serious lack of diligence” to the proposed sanctions, the control of its customers' data is, for Endesa, a fundamentally strategic issue to which it dedicates a huge amount of economic and human resources.  
The importance and seriousness with which the incident was addressed is reflected not only in the notification of the security breach to the AEPD and in the collaboration that Endesa has provided to the AEPD since the beginning of the file, but also in the investigation, containment, and remediation measures that were deployed at the time.  
To undervalue these efforts and measures, as the Resolution Proposal does in this party's opinion, merely because the AEPD does not share the completely reasonable interpretation that Endesa has made of the applicable data protection regulations, undermines the principle of responsibility that should govern the administrative sanctioning procedure.  
In this regard, it is an essential requirement, as established by jurisprudence, that the subjective element of culpability is present in Endesa's conduct, whether in terms of intent (dolo) or negligence (imprudence). What is neither appropriate nor acceptable is to impose sanctions without any culpability being present, based solely on objective responsibility, as the Sanctioning Resolution does in some points that will be discussed later.  
Additionally, it is noted that, within the framework of a sanctioning file such as the one at hand, a rigorous and objective legal analysis regarding the notification obligation, the legal qualification of certain security measures implemented before and after the incident, and the facts, must be conducted prior to the proposal for imposing economic sanctions as substantial as those that the AEPD proposes to impose on Endesa, to determine whether or not they constitute an administrative infringement of non-compliance with the GDPR, which is not an automatic consequence of an incident.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
120/214  
ENDESA has consistently sought to argue with legal rigor and objectivity regarding all of the above, and therefore understands that it is not appropriate to trivialize this aspect and confuse what constitutes legitimate legal defense in a proceeding such as the present one with the importance or seriousness that the entity assigns to the underlying facts, which, as stated, is maximal.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it does not question that the security of information is an important matter for ENDESA. It is simply that, in the present case, this Agency considers that ENDESA has not acted with the due diligence that it should have.  
This Agency does not undervalue the efforts made by ENDESA, but the fact of notifying the data security breach to the AEPD, as well as responding to the information requests sent by this Agency and the measures of "investigation, containment, and remediation" adopted by ENDESA, are merely obligations imposed on ENDESA by the applicable regulations on the processing of personal data and not a reflection of "the importance and seriousness with which the incident was addressed."  
Regarding the assertion that this Agency intends to sanction ENDESA based solely on objective liability, such a statement can only be rejected. The existence of the requirement of the subjective element of the infringement has been duly evidenced in the file, as well as in the response to ENDESA's allegations regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure, and will be detailed in the response to the present allegations as well as in the legal grounds of this resolution.  
This Agency also wishes to point out that, of course, in the present case, it conducted a rigorous and objective legal analysis regarding the notification obligation, the legal qualification of certain security measures implemented before and after the incident, and the facts, to determine whether they constitute an administrative infringement of non-compliance with the GDPR.  
Finally, this Agency emphasizes that it has never trivialized the present issue nor diminished the importance or seriousness of the underlying facts, which is maximal.  
For the reasons stated, the present allegation is dismissed.

(c) Interactions with Facebook to request the removal of advertisements through which the sale/rental of access credentials to the Endesa systems was offered:

ENDESA is surprised that the AEPD uses this decision of Endesa, made consciously and in a totally reasoned manner (in its opinion), to try to justify that Endesa's actions have been seriously negligent. This is for two main reasons:  
i) The decision was made consciously and reasonably by Endesa, for reasons unrelated to those presented by the AEPD in the Proposal for Resolution. That is to say, although the AEPD uses the Proposal for Resolution to present an analysis of the role played, from a data protection perspective, by Facebook Spain and Facebook Ireland regarding the data they process, concluding that Facebook Ireland would be responsible for the processing of personal data at the European level, the truth is that: (a) at no time has Endesa denied this fact nor does it intend to decide who is responsible for the processing (as implied by the Proposal for Resolution on page 69); and (b) in no case has Endesa considered filing a data protection complaint against Facebook, so the analysis carried out by the AEPD in the Proposal for Resolution is completely irrelevant to the adjudication of this case.  
In this regard, when Endesa indicated in its written allegations to the Initiation Agreement that it did not wish to recognize "Irish jurisdiction, which would imply, if necessary, having to direct any claim before the Irish courts, which was and is an inappropriate and unfair burden," it was not made for data protection purposes, but for the purpose of a possible judicial or administrative claim against Facebook for the possible commission of an infringement or crime of disclosure of secrets or intellectual property.

Notwithstanding that, as previously indicated, the analysis regarding who would hold the role of data controller concerning the data processed by Facebook (Facebook Ireland or Facebook Spain) made by the AEPD in the Proposal for Resolution should not be taken into account for the purpose of assessing Endesa's conduct, since such analysis is entirely irrelevant to the decision made by Endesa at the time and, therefore, it cannot be blamed for conducting such analysis incorrectly, it is surprising that the AEPD even suggests that such analysis should be carried out by Endesa.  
It appears that the AEPD, in a matter as extremely complex and one that has been the subject of numerous jurisprudential debates both at the national and European levels, such as the determination of who acts as the data controller for the personal data that Facebook collects at the European level, blames Endesa for not having conducted the corresponding analysis when requiring its execution would be a completely disproportionate and unfair burden.

In this regard, Endesa believes that it cannot be blamed for a serious lack of diligence from the perspective of data protection merely for not addressing Facebook Ireland to demand the removal of the ads, since their removal would not have had (in its opinion) any effect on the data subjects affected by the data security breach.

First of all, this Agency wishes to point out that it is only competent to assess whether ENDESA complied with the regulations on personal data protection. Not to assess issues related to intellectual property. In this sense, in the present case, a series of ads had been published offering access to ENDESA's systems, which allowed access to personal data owned by ENDESA, which it processed in its capacity as data controller. And as such, it should have adopted a series of measures, among others, that such ads be removed, as decided in August 2021, due to the risks they posed. However, Endesa did not approach Facebook Ireland. It should be noted that at that time multifactor authentication was not enabled, which was not adopted until April 2022.

First.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of articles 5.1. f) and 32 of the GDPR  
1. On the implementation of security measures appropriate to the risk  
The AEPD, in the Proposal for Resolution, attributes to Endesa a delay in resetting or disabling certain users when, according to the information provided in the case file, Endesa was already aware that they had been compromised.  
However, ENDESA argues that this is not the case. And it is not the case because:  
(i)  
In ENDESA's opinion, it has been demonstrated that many of the compromised user accounts were disabled almost immediately after becoming aware that these users may have been compromised.  
As merely an example and referring to the section of "Proven Facts" in the Proposal for Resolution, it is verified that:  
a  
(a) On September 1, 2021, ***COMPANY.16 informs Endesa that it has recognized one of the individuals who published an ad on Facebook through which access credentials to ***TOOL.1 are offered, and Endesa, just 2 days later, on September 3, proceeds to disable said user “***USER.3”;

www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
125/214  
b  
(b) On September 10, 2021, Endesa sent an email to ***COMPANY.15 informing them that it had identified an employee as a suspected user who was posting advertisements on Facebook, and on the same day, proceeded to disable said user “***USER.8”, as well as to remove them from the systems of ***TOOL.2; and  
c  
(c) On September 21, 2021, Endesa received an email informing them of the identification of one of the individuals who was selling Endesa credentials on the Internet, and on the same day, Endesa proceeded to disable said user “***USER.5”.  
Regarding points (a) and (c) above, and although the AEPD indicates that these users were not removed from ***TOOL.2 until January 12, 2022, ENDESA reiterates, as stated in its written allegations to the Initiation Agreement, that once the account of each user is disabled or reset, they cannot access it or use it for any purpose, being permanently expelled from the system, so that although the account has not been deleted, the user is prevented from accessing it.  
The same applies to the date of last access. The fact that the date of last access is later than the date of disabling or resetting the user does not mean that the person whose user has been reset has accessed Endesa's systems again; rather, it may respond to possible accesses to the user by Endesa's own teams that are investigating the incident or by new sales representatives to whom that user has been assigned.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that, in the information provided to this Agency and which is part of the file, the date on which the compromised users of ***TOOL.2 were disabled or reset is not recorded, but rather the date on which they were removed from the system.  
As indicated in section 2 of Article 5 “Principles relating to processing” of the GDPR, “The data controller shall be responsible for compliance with the provisions of paragraph 1 and shall be able to demonstrate it (‘proactive responsibility’).” Based on this principle, ENDESA should have been able to prove, throughout the more than 9 months that the preliminary investigation proceedings lasted and the nearly 9 months of the current sanctioning procedure, the date on which the compromised users of ***TOOL.2 had been disabled or reset (as was done regarding the users of ***TOOL.1), and not simply provide the date on which such users were removed from the system. Therefore, this Agency is unaware whether the compromised users of ***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2 were disabled at the same time, a matter that is not evidenced.  
In any case, this Agency considers that ENDESA's actions in disabling and removing the compromised users (both from ***TOOL.2 and ***TOOL.1) have been negligent, as will be detailed further on.  
Regarding the date of last access to the systems, it is true that it cannot be determined who accessed the systems on that date provided to this Agency. However, this only reinforces the existence of the infringement.

28001 – Madrid

of Article 32 of the GDPR, since it is not possible to know who last accessed the systems, due to the improper configuration of a logging system that would allow this information to be reliably known, and the existing system allowed multiple sessions to be initiated simultaneously, all of which is the subject of this sanctioning procedure.

In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that the fact that the lack of diligence of ENDESA in managing the incident in question is not classified as “scandalous” in light of the new information provided along with the allegations to the proposal for resolution of this sanctioning procedure does not imply that ENDESA's actions were sufficiently diligent according to the applicable regulations. Quite the contrary. In the best of cases, ENDESA itself...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es

sedeagpd.gob.es
128/214  
It took ONE MONTH to perform such a simple action as resetting the users that its own provider indicated had been compromised (to the point that a police report was even submitted along with the aforementioned emails). Even if it took a month to conduct the necessary investigations to verify the truthfulness of the information provided by ***COMPANY.1, this Agency considers that ENDESA should have acted with the utmost diligence, taking into account the volume of personal data being processed as well as its characteristics, especially when it had already been decided in the first meeting of the Breach Committee on September 13, 2021, to reset the users that were compromised and a series of vulnerabilities had been detected, for which it was proposed (…), among others. Therefore, being aware of this situation, ENDESA should have reset the users that ***COMPANY.1 had informed it were compromised as soon as possible, instead of waiting an entire month to do so, a behavior that entails a serious lack of diligence.  
Regarding ENDESA's sending of the burofax to Facebook Ireland requesting the removal of advertisements selling credentials to access ENDESA's systems, ENDESA itself acknowledges that not all compromised users had been reset, but that at least one remained unreset, of which ENDESA was aware. And here lies the crux of the matter. The fact that ENDESA knew that a certain number of users had been compromised and that it was through the sale of credentials on Facebook via advertisements published on that social network did not ensure that those users were ALL the compromised users. In other words, ENDESA was unaware if there were more compromised users than those communicated by ***COMPANY.1 and those it had learned about on its own. Therefore, this Agency considers that ENDESA should have made every effort within its reach to request the removal of these advertisements and to contact Facebook Ireland if necessary, as Facebook Spain had indicated. And this should have been done as soon as it became aware, and not waited several months, until February of the following year, to do so.  
ENDESA additionally claims, apart from the above, and although the AEPD avoids mentioning it in the Proposal for Resolution (which clearly causes defenselessness to Endesa), some of the conclusions reached in the expert report dated June 23, 2023, submitted to this File, which perfectly align with what Endesa alleges in this document and demonstrate the utmost diligence with which Endesa acted. The report, prepared by an impartial third party with extensive knowledge of the state of technology and security measures applicable in the industry (unlike the report submitted by ***COMPANY.1, which the AEPD, as indicated, seems to give so much credibility to and which this party contests), concludes that:  
(i) For a large part of the incident, ***TOOL.1 had "a comprehensive event collection system that allows for a detailed trace of the data accessed by the user, and even at times which elements of the interface have been selected," which allowed Endesa to have greater control over the users and the actions they performed on the platform;  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
129/214  
(ii) "According to the evidence [Capturas_Borrado***HERRAMIENTA.1], users were deactivated as soon as it was detected that they could be compromised" (the underlining is ours);  
(iii) "...the management of the incident up to its categorization as a data breach has been correct" (the underlining is ours);  
(iv) "The implementation of multifactor authentication (MFA) has been correctly completed in ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and ***PLATAFORMA.1, as well as in other ENDESA applications that manage user data (such as ***USUARIO.17), preventing unauthorized users from accessing these systems" (the underlining is ours); and  
(v) "User management is correct and systematic; user terminations are managed in a timely manner. It has been verified that the users identified during the incident management have been deactivated and that, from that moment on, it was not possible to use these users to access the applications, even though the announcements published remained active on social media" (the underlining is ours).  

It is surprising to see how the Resolution Proposal completely omits the previous conclusions, which demonstrate Endesa's good practices in incident management and the utmost diligence displayed by this party. In fact, the report itself concludes that: (a) "the immediate deactivation of the compromised users whose sale was announced in the FB publications for access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and/or ***HERRAMIENTA.2 means that, from that moment on, it was impossible to access the systems even though the FB announcements remained active" (the underlining is ours); (b) "the implementation of the multifactor authentication system is an additional security measure that prevents possible unauthorized access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and/or ***HERRAMIENTA.2"; and (iii) that the previous measures "are sufficient to prevent similar violations reported to the AEPD by ENDESA (without assessing the unlawful conduct that may be carried out by third parties acting in bad faith)" (the underlining is ours).  

As can be seen, the previous conclusions reflected in the expert report demonstrate, once again, how precise and truthful the allegations made by this party regarding the implementation of security measures are within the framework of this file. This is why it surprises Endesa that the AEPD, after requesting the submission of said forensic report, not only does not echo the conclusions of the same in the Resolution Proposal but also contradicts them in such a flagrant manner, maintaining the proposal for the imposition of a totally disproportionate sanction of 1,500,000 euros.  

Firstly, this Agency wishes to point out that ENDESA has not been left defenseless at any time. This Agency simply limits itself to highlighting the sections of the expert report in question that are pertinent to the purposes of this resolution.  

Regarding ENDESA's observation on the report of ***EMPRESA.1, this Agency refers to what has already been previously answered.  

Regarding the sections that ENDESA has highlighted:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6

28001 – Madrid

(i) As has been demonstrated throughout this procedure, the ***TOOL.1 system did not have an event collection system that would allow for sufficient knowledge of the actions performed by the various users. In fact, already in the first gap committee in September 2021, it was proposed to expand the system logs to improve this aspect. Furthermore, the fact that multiple active sessions were allowed simultaneously and the lack of multi-factor authentication specifically hindered the ability to track which person behind each user was performing certain actions.  
(ii) Regarding the claim that users were deactivated as soon as it was detected that they were compromised, it has been established that this was not the case. ENDESA itself acknowledges that it took more than a month to reset the users that ***COMPANY.1 had reported as compromised. Additionally, the user ***USER.1 was only reset on February 15, 2022.  
(iii) As for the management of the incident until its categorization as a data breach being correct, this Agency limits itself to verifying whether ENDESA's actions have been diligent concerning the adoption of appropriate security measures in data protection, which has not occurred in this case.  
(iv) This Agency does not dispute that multi-factor authentication was implemented after the incident in question; rather, it considers that it was a necessary measure that should have been adopted by ENDESA much earlier to prevent incidents like the one that is the subject of this procedure.  
(v) It is also not disputed that once users were deactivated, they could not access the applications. What this Agency considers is that the users should have been deactivated more swiftly.  
ENDESA believes that it cannot be concluded, as the Proposal for Resolution does, that the measures taken by Endesa prior to the security breach were in violation of the GDPR (evidence that such a claim is incorrect is that these measures have been in place for years with full effectiveness), nor that the security measures implemented after the incident were ineffective in minimizing the impact that the incident had on the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects and in preventing a recurrence of an incident like the one Endesa suffered. From a teleological perspective, if the security measures implemented by Endesa prior to the incident were sufficient, for 5 years in the case of ***TOOL.1 and almost 3 years in the case of ***TOOL.2, to prevent a data security breach, the fact that a breach occurred subsequently does not imply, given that the guarantee of the integrity of the measures does not exist and that zero risk does not exist, that the measures were insufficient. In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it is aware that zero risk does not exist and wants to clarify that it is not making a teleological interpretation. It must be taken into account that security measures should be adopted based on the state of the art, the costs of implementation, and the nature, scope, context, and purposes of the processing. These circumstances vary over time, especially in the current moment.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
131/214  
in which the evolution of information technologies poses new challenges for security, thus the threats and risks to the rights and freedoms of the interested parties are not the same as they were five years ago; therefore, it is necessary for the responsible party to evaluate the new risks and apply measures to mitigate them.  
In this case, there was no multifactor authentication, it was possible that multiple sessions were open simultaneously, it was not possible to control access to ENDESA's systems nor to trace activity through logs. Therefore, it cannot be asserted that the measures adopted by Endesa were adequate to prevent unauthorized access, taking into account, among other circumstances, the amount of data processed, the number of individuals affected by the processing, the involvement of data processors in the processing, and even the technological context.  
Furthermore, regarding the measures related to access security, as noted in the procedure, Endesa had implemented a measure based on passwords. However, it did not implement a complementary authentication system until April 2022, despite the fact that on September 13, 2021, the Security Breaches Committee, after verifying the truthfulness of the announcement published on Facebook, proposed the establishment of a multifactor authentication system, and despite the publication of new announcements on Facebook.  
If ENDESA had adopted appropriate measures to reduce the impact or prevent the risk from materializing, there would be no infringement on its part. Moreover, if ENDESA had implemented the measures that it put in place as a result of the violation of the personal data security in question, it is likely that this violation would not have occurred and the infringement might not have existed. But this was not the case, which is why the present sanctioning procedure was initiated. Furthermore, the fact that 3 or 5 years had passed without a violation of personal data security does not imply that the measures were appropriate, but simply that there had been no unauthorized access, which is a very different matter.  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  
2. The eventual imposition of two sanctions for the alleged infringement of articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR would be contrary to law due to a violation of the principle of non bis in idem or, where applicable, of the rules applicable in cases of conflicts of punitive norms.  
2.1.- Presentation  

The notified Proposal for Resolution maintains the simultaneous imputation to Endesa of the infringement of article 5.1.f) of the GDPR and that of its article 32, and proposes the imposition of a sanction amounting, in total, to 4,000,000 euros.  
ENDESA argues that these provisions enshrine, on the one hand, the principle of integrity and confidentiality, which translates into the obligation to process data "in such a way as to ensure an adequate level of security for personal data, including protection against unauthorized or unlawful processing and against accidental loss, destruction or...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
132/214  
accidental damage, through the application of appropriate technical or organizational measures” [Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR], and, on the other hand, the obligation to adopt “appropriate technical and organizational measures” to ensure, among other things, “an adequate level of security” (Article 32 of the GDPR).  
ENDESA believes that what has been alleged up to this point, combined with the evidence provided in the file, duly demonstrates that the technical and organizational measures in place at Endesa at the time of the incident that gives rise to this procedure complied with the provisions of Article 32 of the GDPR. It is not acceptable to require that they be infallible, which is what (in its opinion) the Proposal for Resolution seeks to justify in maintaining the accusation made against this provision.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it is not intended that the security measures adopted to protect personal data be infallible, but rather that they be appropriate based on the risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, which has not occurred in the present case.  
Notwithstanding the above, ENDESA argues that, even assuming for purely argumentative purposes that a violation of Article 32 of the GDPR occurred, it would not be appropriate to not only sanction Endesa for the same but also to impose an additional sanction for the alleged violation of Article 5.1.f) of that regulation. To put it graphically: it would not be appropriate to impose a sanction for supposedly lacking adequate measures to ensure data security and, in addition, for not having effectively guaranteed that security.  
Otherwise, it understands that two sanctions would be imposed for facts that in this specific case are so intimately connected that it can be stated that both refer to the same issue. Moreover, those sanctions would be imposed based on two punitive norms that aim to protect the same legal good, namely, the guarantee of data security.  
Contrary to what the Proposal for Resolution states regarding the idea that both provisions pursue different aims (a statement that, in its opinion, is made without the slightest argumentative support), ENDESA argues that, if the GDPR is examined, it is easily concluded that it always follows the dynamic that all principles related to data processing contained in Article 5 of the GDPR are subsequently concretized in the obligations specifically imposed on the data controller and the data processor, so that, with regard to security, the principle embodied in paragraph 1.f) of that Article 5 materializes in the obligations established in Article 32 to ensure an adequate level of security relative to the risk.  
ENDESA asserts that the legal good protected by both provisions is, in summary, exactly the same. And that there is nothing in Article 5.1.f) that is not intended to be protected by the obligations imposed on the data controller and the data processor in Article 32.  
Precisely for this reason, ENDESA maintains that, in the event that both proposed sanctions are imposed, the sanctioning resolution would directly violate the principle of non bis in idem.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
133/214  
in idem, which would determine its nullity by full right due to the violation of the fundamental right to sanctioning legality enshrined in Article 25 of the Constitution.  
All of this was extensively presented in the allegations against the Initiation Agreement, which are hereby reproduced for greater specificity, in which it was also noted that, even if there were doubts about whether the triple identity of subject, fact, and basis required for the application of the aforementioned principle is met, what is evident to ENDESA is that at a minimum we would be facing a concurrence of punitive norms that must necessarily be resolved according to the normative and jurisprudential rules established for this purpose, which would never lead to the imposition of two independent sanctions.  
And it argues that none of the reasons put forward in the Proposal for Resolution to reject these allegations is correct from a legal perspective nor, therefore, allows for the maintenance of the intended double punitive sanction that has been referenced.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it could not disagree more with the analysis carried out by ENDESA regarding the principle embodied in Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR materializing in the obligations of Article 32 of the GDPR.  
If this were the case, the violations of these articles would not be classified as different violations.  
Regarding the classification of these violations, it should be clarified, beforehand, that the confidentiality and security of data are fundamentally reflected in two independent provisions of the GDPR: in Article 5.1.f) and in Article 32 of the GDPR.  
Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR is one of the principles related to processing. The principles related to processing are, on one hand, the starting point and the closing clause of the legal framework for data protection, constituting true guiding rules of the system with intense expansive force; on the other hand, having a high level of specificity, they are mandatory norms that can be infringed.  
Well, Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR encompasses the principle of integrity and confidentiality and determines that personal data shall be processed in such a way as to ensure an adequate level of security of personal data, including protection against unauthorized or unlawful processing and against accidental loss, destruction, or damage, through the application of appropriate technical or organizational measures.  
There are multiple technical or organizational measures that are not security measures and that the data controller can implement as a means to guarantee this principle.  
For example, raising awareness among employees of a data controller regarding what it means and implies for the rights and freedoms of the data subjects to guarantee the fundamental right to the Protection of Personal Data is not a security measure. It is a compliance management measure that clearly contributes to the guarantee of confidentiality.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
134/214  
Linking the guarantee of confidentiality and integrity solely to security measures is a simplistic understanding of what the GDPR imposes and means. This is because it reduces it to security measures, when security measures constitute the last link in the chain, before starting anew.  
Of course, the appropriate technical or organizational measures referred to in Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR can be security measures, but not exclusively.  
On the other hand, Article 32 of the GDPR regulates how the security of processing should be articulated in relation to the specific security measures that must be implemented, taking into account the state of the art, the costs of implementation, and the nature, scope, context, and purposes of the processing, as well as the varying probabilities and severity of risks to the rights and freedoms of natural persons. The data controller and the processor shall apply appropriate technical and organizational measures to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk, which includes, among other issues, but not limited to, the ability to ensure the confidentiality of the data.  
As can be seen, this provision, Article 32 of the GDPR, although related to Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR, like many of the articles of the GDPR that are also related, does not encompass the principle in its entirety. Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR explicitly requires that confidentiality be guaranteed and requires a loss of confidentiality for its application. There may be cases where there are inappropriate technical and organizational security measures without a loss of integrity and confidentiality. But the opposite can also occur, with appropriate technical and organizational security measures, and a loss of integrity and confidentiality of personal data having taken place.  
In fact, the violation of any of these provisions is classified independently and autonomously in different sections of Article 83 of the GDPR, so the simultaneous violation of both provisions, as occurs in this case, is possible and something anticipated by the legislator, without the violation of one preventing the violation of the other, which does not in itself constitute a breach of the principle of non bis in idem. Furthermore, one of them, Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR, provides for administrative fines of up to 20 million euros, which is reduced to half in the case of Article 32 of the GDPR.  
Similarly, in the LOPDGDD, when addressing the classification of infringements solely for the purposes of prescription, very serious and serious infringements are clearly differentiated for the purposes of prescription, whether it concerns the violation of Article 5.1(f) of the GDPR or the violation of Article 32 of the GDPR, respectively.  
Regarding the fact that all principles related to the processing of personal data cited in Article 5 of the GDPR have their specification in another article of the Regulation, this does not occur with Article 5.1(c) of the GDPR, which contemplates the principle of data minimization and does not have its "reflection" in another section of the Regulation.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
135/214  
Furthermore, this Agency reiterates what has already been stated in the resolution proposal regarding the aforementioned allegations to the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
For all the reasons stated, the present allegation is dismissed.  

2.2.- The reasons put forth in the Resolution Proposal cannot lead to the non-application of the principle  
 
non bis in idem  
 
or, failing that, the rules regarding the concurrence of punitive norms.  
ENDESA argues that a mere reading of the two provisions deemed infringed by this Agency is sufficient to appreciate their intimate connection regarding the legal good protected by both.  
It is not a matter of them "being related," as the Resolution Proposal somewhat lightly claims (in its opinion), but rather of provisions that are intimately related (in ENDESA's view), to the extent that one represents the formulation of a principle and the other the specification of the obligations to fulfill it.  
Indeed, ENDESA understands that while Article 5.1.f) refers to the obligation to ensure "adequate security" of personal data through the application of "appropriate technical or organizational measures," Article 32 seeks to specify that principle by imposing on the data controller and the processor the obligation to apply "appropriate technical and organizational measures" to ensure "an adequate level of security."  
Viewed from another perspective, which nonetheless leads to the same conclusion, ENDESA explains that Article 32 establishes an obligation of means to prevent a result from occurring, and that obligation consists of having the appropriate technical or organizational measures, while Article 5.1.f) precisely enshrines the result that is intended to be achieved with such means, which is to guarantee the security and integrity of the data.  
Precisely for this reason, ENDESA disagrees with what the Resolution Proposal states regarding the notion that the violation of Article 5.1.f) "may occur, or not, due to the absence or deficiency of security measures," since any potential infringement of that specific provision will always depend on the type of security measures that were implemented. In fact, to reach this logical conclusion, it is sufficient to note that if the security measures were infallible (which, obviously, even the GDPR does not require, as it makes them dependent on factors such as the state of the art, implementation costs, risks, etc.), then the cited Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR could never be violated.  
And if this is the case from a strictly legal perspective based on an integrative interpretation of the GDPR that respects the mechanics it adopts of outlining principles and establishing corresponding obligations to guarantee them, ENDESA argues that it is even more so in a case like the one at hand, where from a factual point of view - and always in the opinion of this Agency - the alleged non-compliance with Article 5.1.f) would have responded to the claim that, supposedly, the security measures were "not appropriate to the risk," that is, to an alleged non-compliance with Article 32.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
136/214  
There can be no doubt, in such cases, that any eventual infringement of the principle of integrity and confidentiality would merely be a consequence of the prior infringement of the obligation to have appropriate technical and organizational measures in place (assuming the approach of the Instruction).  
And precisely for this reason, it is also incorrect, in the opinion of ENDESA, what the notified Resolution Proposal states regarding the possibility that a company may not have adequate measures and that, nevertheless, this does not imply an infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR, but only of Article 32. ENDESA explains that a breach of Article 32 may or may not materialize in a loss of confidentiality and integrity, but it will always represent a breach of Article 5.1.f), since it will imply failing to uphold the fundamental principle of ensuring adequate security.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that at no time in the aforementioned resolution proposal has it been stated that ENDESA's breach of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR was due to the security measures not being appropriate to the risk. What was stated verbatim was: “Taking as a reference what has been previously stated, in the present sanctioning procedure, the principle of non bis in idem has not been violated, since, although understood broadly, the facts are detected as a consequence of a violation of the security of personal data, the infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR is specified in a clear loss of confidentiality and integrity, while the infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR is reduced to the absence and deficiency of the security measures (only security) detected, which are present independently of the violation of the security of the personal data in question. In fact, if these security measures (not appropriate to the risk, by the way) that ENDESA had in place had been detected by this Agency without the loss of confidentiality and integrity of the personal data having occurred, ENDESA would only have been sanctioned for Article 32 of the GDPR.”  
On the contrary, this Agency completely separates what would be the infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR for failing to adequately guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of personal data from the infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR for not having appropriate security measures (measures solely for security). All of which was extensively explained in the response to the allegations against the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure, to which this Agency refers.  
ENDESA argues that the equivalence between Article 5.1.f) and Article 32 is total, and that the forced independence or separation that the Resolution Proposal claims as a fact, without any argument to demonstrate it, cannot be established between the two. Both provisions protect (in the opinion of ENDESA) the same legal asset, the first from the conceptual level of the principles and the second from the specification of the means to ensure their observance.  
Therefore, ENDESA understands that sanctioning its breach separately means sanctioning the same thing twice, which is why the thesis followed by the Resolution Proposal is contrary to the principle of non bis in idem, which—should it be necessary to remind—materially prohibits the same subject from being sanctioned twice for the same act; or, at the very least, it is contrary to the rules relating to concurrency.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
137/214  
of punitive norms, given that, even in the event that it were understood that the legal interest protected by both provisions is different (which it is not), what could never be doubted is that the alleged infringement of Article 5.1.f) would be an immediate consequence of the infringement of Article 32 and would solely and exclusively obey it, such that a single act would have given rise to two indissoluble infringements.  
ENDESA asserts that the above is supported by the criterion repeatedly applied by our Courts in cases where they have judged whether it was possible to sanction a subject twice for two infringements when one was merely the consequence of the other, as -it insists- this Agency should accept would occur in this case if it did not accept that the separate sanctioning of the breach of Article 5.1.f) and that of Article 32 of the GDPR is not permissible.  
A jurisprudential criterion that -in ENDESA's opinion- is fully transferable to the case at hand, as it is the Proposal for Resolution itself that recognizes that the alleged loss of confidentiality and integrity determining the presumed breach of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR was due to the fact that the security measures implemented by Endesa were not appropriate and, therefore, to the presumed breach of its Article 32.  
ENDESA cites as the first example of this consolidated jurisprudence the Judgment 2/2003, of January 16, of the Constitutional Court, which, following the doctrinal line set by the European Court of Human Rights, declared that when a detailed examination of facts leads to the conclusion that one illicit act encompasses the elements of other illicit acts that a person may have committed, only the first can be pursued:  
“(…) there are cases in which an act, at first glance, seems to constitute more than one illicit act, while a more ***EMPRESA.15 detailed examination shows that only one illicit act should be pursued because it encompasses all the illicit acts contained in the others (…). An obvious example would be an act that constituted two illicit acts, one of which contained precisely the same elements as the other plus one additional element.”  
The citation of this precedent is relevant because this is exactly what would occur in the present case according to ENDESA, in which it is evident that the alleged breach of the principle of integrity and confidentiality that is reproached would be a direct consequence of the alleged inappropriate nature of the security measures implemented by Endesa, such that the breach of Article 32, which would be prior, would absorb the subsequent breach of Article 5.1.f). And this is in the hypothesis that it is ultimately considered by that Agency that these are provisions that protect different legal interests, which ENDESA cannot agree with for the reasons already stated.  
ENDESA also invokes the Judgment of June 30, 2021, of the Superior Court of Justice of Madrid (ECLI:ES:TSJM:2021:774), in which it was analyzed whether it was possible to sanction a subject for breaching an order to cease activity of a premises and, at the same time, because that breach involved having that premises open without a functioning license.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
138/214  
The Court considered that this duality of sanctions was not legally valid, as under no circumstances can one simultaneously sanction both a result (in this case, opening a premises without the necessary permits) and the action from which that result actually derives (breaching a cease order by opening to the public a premises without a license):  
“This is, by opening the establishment without the required operating license, one is simultaneously disobeying the cease and closure order. Ultimately, the commission of the infringement typified in Article 37.5 cannot occur without simultaneously committing the infringement typified in Article 37.2, both of the LEPAR, and vice versa.”  
The above leads the Court to the conclusion that there is a “medial concurrence between both infringements” and that the imposition of a double sanction “violates the principle of non bis in idem.”  
Translating this criterion to our factual scenario, even in the hypothesis that it had to be accepted that Articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR are not equivalent and that, therefore, their breaches would represent distinct infringements (which ENDESA can under no circumstances agree with), there would be no doubt that in this case one would indeed have been a consequence of the other, leading to the same conclusion as the Court, in the sense that the imposition of two sanctions, instead of punishing them according to the rules of medial concurrence, would violate the principle of non bis in idem.  
It is also worth recalling the Judgment of May 13, 2004, of the High Court of Justice of Madrid (ECLI:ES:TSJM:2004:6240), in which the sanctions imposed on a company for failing to comply with the determinations of an emergency plan and, at the same time, for having discharged fuel into the waters of a port as a consequence of the former were judged.  
ENDESA states that this is a case with which an absolute parallelism can be established because both a specific result (the discharge, equivalent in this case to the security breach) and the act that led to it (on that occasion, the failure to comply with the emergency plan, equivalent to the failure to have adequate technical and organizational measures) were sanctioned.  
Well, ENDESA explains that what is relevant for these purposes is that the Court rejected the possibility of sanctioning both behaviors separately, understanding that the connection between them required considering them as one for punitive purposes:  
“The detailed reading of both files reveals that the connection between the facts pursued in both is absolute and that, in any case, the failure to comply with the contingency plan and the presence of fuel in the ballast tank constitute the factual and culpability premises of both sanctions.”  
In the Court's opinion, “what is relevant is not so much that two distinct behaviors can be identified, but what degree of relationship or connection exists between them,” and since one fact derives from the other, there was an “evident medial relationship between one...”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
139/214  
conduct and another (the spill occurred because there was fuel oil in a ballast tank and the presence of the fuel in that tank occurred because the contingency plan was not complied with).  
This is exactly the same as what happens in this case, in the opinion of ENDESA.  
ENDESA argues that, beyond the legal discussion about whether the provisions whose infringement is attributed to ENDESA are equivalent - as this party defends with absolute conviction - or, on the contrary, can theoretically be violated independently - as stated in the notified Resolution Proposal - what must be considered from a sanctioning perspective in cases like this is whether there is or is not a connection or consequential relationship between the behaviors that are deemed unlawful.  
And in this case, in ENDESA's opinion, that connection is more than evident, because, even if the principle of integrity and confidentiality had indeed been compromised, it would have been because, supposedly, the obligation to have adequate measures to guarantee it had been breached, thus existing an "evident medial relationship between one conduct and the other," using the same words as the Superior Court of Justice of Madrid.  
ENDESA argues that all of the above ultimately demonstrates the impossibility of separately sanctioning the alleged infringement of articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR in the present case, as the first would merely be the consequence of the second and, therefore, in accordance with the jurisprudence presented, the principle of non bis in idem would be violated or, at a minimum, the rules governing the concurrence of punitive norms would be breached.  
ENDESA argues that, beyond the legal discussion about whether the provisions whose infringement is attributed to ENDESA are equivalent - as this party defends with absolute conviction - or, on the contrary, can theoretically be violated independently - as stated in the notified Resolution Proposal - what must be considered from a sanctioning perspective in cases like this is whether there is or is not a connection or consequential relationship between the behaviors that are deemed unlawful.  
And in this case, in ENDESA's opinion, that connection is more than evident, because, even if the principle of integrity and confidentiality had indeed been compromised, it would have been because, supposedly, the obligation to have adequate measures to guarantee it had been breached, thus existing an "evident medial relationship between one conduct and the other," using the same words as the Superior Court of Justice of Madrid.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that the jurisprudence cited by ENDESA is not applicable to the case. This is not a case of an unlawful act that encompasses by itself the elements of other unlawful acts (as in Judgment 2/2003, of January 16, of the Constitutional Court), since, as explained in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement, these are distinct issues: through article 5.1.f) of the GDPR, a loss of integrity and confidentiality is sanctioned, solely, and through article 32 of the GDPR, the absence and deficiency of the measures of  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
140/214  
security measures implemented by the data controller. Absent or deficient security measures that, in themselves, violate the GDPR regardless of whether confidentiality has been lost.  
It is also not about sanctioning a result and the action from which that result derives (Judgment of June 30, 2021, of the High Court of Justice of Madrid), since the loss of confidentiality and integrity of personal data is not solely subject to the adoption of security measures. This Agency reiterates that there are multiple technical or organizational measures that are not security-related and that the data controller can implement as a means to ensure this principle.  
However, Article 32 of the GDPR includes the obligation to implement appropriate technical and organizational security measures to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk. Of security. Only of security.  
For the same reasons, it is also not the case that the conduct that violates Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR and the conduct that violates Article 32 of the GDPR should be considered as a single conduct for punitive purposes (as in the Judgment of May 13, 2004, of the High Court of Justice of Madrid), since one fact did not derive from the other.  
This Agency reiterates that Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR is violated when there is a loss of confidentiality, integrity, or availability of personal data, which may or may not occur due to the absence or deficiency of security measures.  
And this principle only determines the means through which the maintenance of confidentiality, integrity, or availability can be achieved when it explicitly states "through the application of appropriate technical and organizational measures," which are not strictly security-related (as is the obligation referred to in Article 32 of the GDPR).  
ENDESA adds that these are rules that in this case are contained in Guidelines 4/2022, of the European Data Protection Committee ("EDPC"), on the calculation of administrative fines under the GDPR, whose application should lead, in accordance with the principle of specialty, to the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR being the only one that could be sanctioned.  
ENDESA argues that the Proposal for Resolution merely states at this point - again without any support - that the aforementioned Guidelines would only apply to facts that occurred after their adoption and that, for that reason, they could not be applied in this case. However, the truth is that nothing prevents the application of the Guidelines to ongoing cases (even if the facts may be prior), as they are merely rules and criteria for the calculation of sanctions and because, furthermore, it is the only thing truly coherent with the objective of harmonization that these Guidelines expressly encompass and which motivated their approval by the EDPC. In fact, given that their application is more beneficial in this case considering the different amounts of the proposed sanctions for the alleged infringements, their application would be required by what is established in Article 9.3 of the Constitution.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
141/214  
ENDESA adds that, even if this Agency were to consider that the aforementioned Guidelines are not applicable (which for this party would be a serious violation of the constitutional principle requiring favorable retroactivity in sanctioning matters), it would necessarily have to adhere to Article 29.5 of Law 40/2015, of October 1, on the Legal Regime of the Public Sector (LRJSP), whereby only the sanction for non-compliance with Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR would be applicable.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the aforementioned Guidelines do not apply to the present case. However, even if they were applicable, this Agency considers that there are two distinct infringing behaviors, as previously stated in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement and the resolution proposal of the present sanctioning procedure, and therefore Chapter 3 of the aforementioned Guidelines would also not apply.  
Finally, regarding the applicability of Article 29.5 of the LRJSP as claimed by ENDESA, this Agency wishes to point out that Article 29 of the LRJSP does not apply to the sanctioning regime imposed by the GDPR.  
This is because the GDPR is a closed and complete system.  
The GDPR is a directly applicable community regulation in the Member States, which contains a new, closed, complete, and global system aimed at ensuring the protection of personal data uniformly across the European Union.  
Specifically, in relation to the sanctioning regime established therein, its provisions apply immediately, directly, and integrally, providing a complete and gapless system that must be understood, interpreted, and integrated absolutely, completely, and integrally, thereby preserving its ultimate purpose, which is the effective and real guarantee of the fundamental right to the Protection of Personal Data. The contrary would result in a reduction of the guarantees of the rights and freedoms of citizens.  
In fact, a specific example of the absence of gaps in the GDPR system is Article 83 of the GDPR, which determines the circumstances that may operate as aggravating or mitigating factors regarding an infringement (Article 83.2 of the GDPR) or specifies the existing rule concerning a possible concurrent offense (Article 83.3 of the GDPR).  
Additionally, it must be noted that the GDPR does not allow the development or specification of its provisions by the legislators of the Member States, except for what the European legislator has specifically provided, delimiting it very concretely (for example, the provision of Article 83.7 of the GDPR). The LOPDGDD only develops or specifies some aspects of the GDPR to the extent that it allows and with the scope that it permits.  
This is so because the intended purpose of the European legislator is to implement a uniform system throughout the European Union that guarantees the rights and freedoms of natural persons, corrects behaviors contrary to the GDPR, promotes compliance, and enables the free movement of such data.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
142/214  
In this regard, Recital 2 of the GDPR states that:  
“(2) The principles and rules regarding the protection of natural persons with regard to the processing of their personal data must, regardless of their nationality or residence, respect their fundamental freedoms and rights, in particular the right to the protection of personal data. This Regulation aims to contribute to the full realization of an area of freedom, security, and justice and of an economic union, to economic and social progress, to the strengthening and convergence of economies within the internal market, as well as to the well-being of natural persons.” (the underlining is ours)  
It further indicates in Recital 13 of the GDPR that:  
“(13) To ensure a consistent level of protection for natural persons throughout the Union and to avoid divergences that hinder the free movement of personal data within the internal market, a regulation is necessary that provides legal certainty and transparency to economic operators, including micro-enterprises and small and medium-sized enterprises, and offers natural persons in all Member States the same level of enforceable rights and obligations and responsibilities for controllers and processors, in order to ensure consistent supervision of the processing of personal data and equivalent sanctions across all Member States, as well as effective cooperation between the supervisory authorities of the different Member States. The proper functioning of the internal market requires that the free movement of personal data in the Union is not restricted or prohibited for reasons related to the protection of natural persons with regard to the processing of personal data.” (the underlining is ours)  
In this system, what is decisive in the GDPR is not the fines. The corrective powers of the supervisory authorities provided for in Article 58.2 of the GDPR, combined with the provisions of Article 83 of the GDPR, show the prevalence of corrective measures over fines.  
Thus, Article 83.2 of the GDPR states that “Administrative fines shall be imposed, depending on the circumstances of each individual case, as an additional or substitute measure to those provided for in Article 58, paragraph 2, letters a) to h) and j).”  
In this way, the corrective measures, which are all those provided for in Article 58.2 of the GDPR except for the fine, take precedence in this system, relegating the economic fine to cases where the circumstances of the specific case determine that a fine is imposed alongside the corrective measures or in substitution thereof.  
And all this with the aim of enforcing compliance with the GDPR, preventing non-compliance, promoting compliance, and ensuring that the infringement is not more profitable than non-compliance.  
Therefore, Article 83.1 of the GDPR provides that “Each supervisory authority shall ensure that the imposition of administrative fines in accordance with this Regulation...”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
143/214  
Article for the infringements of this Regulation indicated in sections 4, 5, and 6 must in each individual case be effective, proportionate, and deterrent.”  
The fines must be effective, proportionate, and deterrent to achieve the purpose intended by the GDPR.  
For this system to function with all its guarantees, it is necessary for several elements to be deployed in an integral and complete manner. The application of rules external to the GDPR regarding the determination of fines in each of the Member States applying their national law, whether due to aggravating or mitigating circumstances not provided for in the GDPR - or in the LOPDGDD in the Spanish case - or due to the application of a different medium competition than that provided in the GDPR, would undermine the effectiveness of the system, which would lose its meaning, its teleological purpose, resulting in fines imposed for different infringements ceasing to be effective, proportionate, and deterrent. In this way, it would also deprive the interested parties of the effective guarantee of their rights and freedoms, weakening the uniform application of the GDPR. The mechanisms for protecting the rights and freedoms of citizens would be diminished, which would be contrary to the spirit of the GDPR.  
The GDPR is endowed with its own principle of proportionality that must be applied in its strict terms.  
Therefore, there is no legal gap, nor is there supplementary application of Article 29 of the GDPR.  
In addition to the above, it should be noted that there is no legal gap regarding the application of medium competition. Neither the GDPR allows nor does the LOPDGDD provide for the supplementary application of the provisions of Article 29 of the LRJSP.  
In Title VIII of the LOPDGDD relating to “Procedures in the event of a possible violation of data protection regulations,” Article 63 that opens the Title states that "The procedures processed by the Spanish Data Protection Agency will be governed by the provisions of Regulation (EU) 2016/679, by this organic law, by the regulatory provisions issued in its development, and, as long as they do not contradict them, subsidiarily by the general rules on administrative procedures." While there is a clear reference to the LPACAP, there is absolutely no establishment of a subsidiary application regarding the LRJSP, which does not contain any provision in its wording relating to any administrative procedure.  
In the same way that the AEPD is not applying the aggravating and mitigating factors provided in Article 29 of the LRJSP, since the GDPR establishes its own, therefore, there is no legal gap nor subsidiary application of the same, nor is there room for the application of the section relating to medium competition for the same reasons.  
For all the above, this allegation is dismissed.  
2.3.- Conclusion: inadmissibility of separately sanctioning the alleged infringements of Articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR  

C/ Jorge Juan, 6

144/214  
In the opinion of ENDESA, the above demonstrates the error made by the Proposal for Resolution in attempting to sanction Endesa separately for the alleged violations of Articles 5.1.f) and 32 of the GDPR.  
Therefore, ENDESA believes that this Proposal for Resolution should be rectified and, in the event that, despite the arguments presented in this document, this Agency considers that there is a concurrent violation of security obligations, Endesa could only be sanctioned for the alleged non-compliance with Article 32 of the GDPR (if the specialty criterion contained primarily in the Guidelines is applied) or, at most, for Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR (if the general rule contained in the LRJSP is deemed applicable), without prejudice to the fact that Article 9.3 of the Constitution would require the application of the principle of specialty contained in the aforementioned Guidelines.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates what has already been stated in its response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement and the proposal for resolution of this sanctioning procedure, and thus the present allegation is dismissed.  
3. Violation of the principle of legality  
Regarding ENDESA's allegation in the written submissions to the Initiation Agreement, the AEPD responds that the principle of legality is not violated because the GDPR allows the supervisory authority to specify the amount of the sanction using a series of evaluative elements included in Article 83 GDPR, such as the modifying circumstances of liability in point 2 or the definition of types in points 3 and 4.  
ENDESA argues that the above does not resolve the issue raised, as the legality in this case is not violated by the structure of the GDPR, but by the lack of specification of the infringing activity contained both in the Initiation Agreement and in the Proposal for Resolution. In this sense, despite the extensive nature of the file, including prior requests, the AEPD has not specified what the specific measures that Endesa has failed to comply with would be that would allow: (i) to avoid or reduce the risk of the incident in question occurring, and (ii) to defend the adequacy of the measures deployed by Endesa to the GDPR.  
Therefore, the allegation expressed regarding the violation of the principle of legality in this aspect is reiterated, and in particular, what was stated in the conclusion of that document when it was said that “Consequently, in the application of the norm, the administration must be extremely careful when identifying the facts constituting the infringement and explaining the reason why those facts are encompassed within the type drafted in a non-exhaustive manner.”  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it has extensively detailed what the facts constituting the infringement have been and how they fit into the drafted type.  
ENDESA argued in its submissions to the initiation agreement of this sanctioning procedure that: “Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR establishes a general principle that does not have specific concreteness through clear positive or limiting acts that the obligated subject can know and comply with. On the contrary, it uses indeterminate legal concepts.”

145/214  
in a chained manner (“adequate security”, “appropriate measures”), which do not allow for a transparent understanding of the scope of obligations or prohibitions.”  
In the present case, as detailed in Legal Basis IV, on August 24, 2021, ENDESA detected an advertisement on the social network Facebook selling access to the application ***HERRAMIENTA.1 with access to customer information.  
On September 10, 2021, ENDESA confirmed the veracity of the advertisement and identified the credentials of the application user that were being used by the advertiser to sell access. That is to say, by September 2021, it had been verified that the confidentiality of the databases owned by ENDESA had been compromised.  
Subsequently, on October 19, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 detected that a user of ***EMPRESA.9 had attempted to carry out a fraudulent registration, which is why on October 27, 2021, a certified letter was sent to ***EMPRESA.9 communicating the definitive closure of the ENDESA supply contract sales campaign effective October 19, after having provisionally suspended that campaign on the same day.  
On October 20, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 sent a communication to ENDESA indicating the operations affected by the fraudulent practice of ***EMPRESA.9, identifying 137 contracts made and another 7 operations pending validation by ENDESA that had not been registered. Additionally, 172 orders that were pending validation by the back office of ***EMPRESA.9 were identified and blocked to prevent their processing.  
That is to say, since September 2021, ENDESA was already aware of the existence of a security breach, and since October 2021, it knew that the confidentiality and integrity of the data of 137 interested parties had been compromised, and that there were another 179 potential interested parties whose data was more than likely to have been compromised.  
For its part, on October 27, 2021, an email was sent to ENDESA attaching an email from the manager of ***EMPRESA.9 communicating to ***EMPRESA.1 the complaints filed with the Police of ***PAÍS.1 on October 19, 2021, against 23 commercial agents.  
On October 27, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 received an email from the manager of ***EMPRESA.9, which on the same day ***EMPRESA.1 forwarded to ENDESA, detailing the inquiries made regarding the fraudulent practice carried out by some of its agents and their modus operandi.  
With the details of this modus operandi, it was established that the integrity and confidentiality of the personal data of at least those clients who had been fraudulently registered had been affected, since to proceed with the registration it was necessary to access their data and modify it with false information.  
Also, in this email dated October 27, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 identified five of the users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 involved in the security breach.

of personal data (used by ***COMPANY.9) and screenshots were attached showing Facebook ads offering the rental and/or sale of access credentials to the application ***TOOL.1.  
On January 17, 2022, new ads were published on Facebook by user B.B.B., which affected the customer databases of ENDESA. Therefore, at least during the month of January 2022, the confidentiality of the data contained in the database owned by ENDESA continued to be inadequately guaranteed.  
On February 7, 2022, ENDESA sent a burofax to FACEBOOK SPAIN S.L. indicating that there were users publishing information that could constitute a crime and requested its removal, from which it can be inferred that the ads selling credentials to access ***TOOL.1 were still published, further compromising the confidentiality of the data contained in that system.
