ENDESA customers, as well as data from 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers of ENDESA.  
Furthermore, regarding the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.2, the confidentiality of the personal data of 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers of ENDESA was not guaranteed.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA did not adequately guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data in its possession.  
Regarding ENDESA's demand that this Agency specify what specific measures ENDESA has not complied with, the very approach of the GDPR prevents such detail from being provided.  
The proactive responsibility advocated by the GDPR involves the implementation of a compliance and management model for the GDPR that determines the widespread fulfillment of obligations regarding data protection. It includes the establishment, maintenance, updating, and control of data protection policies within an organization—understood as the set of guidelines that govern the actions of an organization, practices, procedures, and tools—ensuring privacy by design and by default, which guarantees compliance with the GDPR, prevents the materialization of risks, and allows it to demonstrate its compliance.  
It pivots on risk management. As established in Report 0064/2020 from the Legal Department of the AEPD, it shows the metamorphosis of a system that has transitioned from being reactive to becoming proactive, since "at the current moment, it must be taken into account that the GDPR has represented a paradigm shift in addressing the regulation of the right to personal data protection, which is now based on the principle of 'accountability' or 'proactive responsibility' as repeatedly pointed out by the AEPD (Report 17/2019, among many others) and is reflected in the Explanatory Memorandum of the LOPDGDD: 'the greatest novelty presented by Regulation (EU) 2016/679 is the evolution from a model fundamentally based on compliance control to one that rests on the principle of active responsibility, which requires a prior assessment by the data controller or processor of the risk that may arise from the processing of personal data in order to adopt the appropriate measures based on that assessment.'"  
It requires a conscious, committed, active, and diligent attitude. Consciousness implies the knowledge of one's organization by the data controller and how it is affected by data protection and the inherent risks of personal data processing; commitment involves the willingness to comply and to truly take responsibility for the implementation of data protection policies within the organization; the active attitude is related to proactivity, effectiveness, efficiency, and operability; and diligence is the care, zeal, and dedication put into compliance.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
148/214  
The party that best knows its activity is the one that carries out the processing of personal data in question (in this case, ENDESA) and who must detail and demonstrate the measures adopted, in accordance with what is established, particularly in Article 5.2 of the GDPR. The deficiencies observed in the measures adopted by Endesa have already been highlighted by this Agency throughout this resolution.  
For all the above, this allegation is dismissed.  
Second.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR  
The Proposal for Resolution, in maintaining the proposed sanction for the alleged infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR, focuses mainly on the argument, which ENDESA considers to be erroneous, that Endesa did not correctly qualify the incident by considering that it was not reportable after the first two analyses conducted.  
The AEPD argues that “it is simply sufficient for there to be a probability (whatever it may be) that there is a risk (whatever it may be)” for, in accordance with Article 33 of the GDPR, a data controller must notify the existence of a security breach to the AEPD.  
ENDESA is surprised that this is the interpretation of the AEPD in this proceeding when this same Agency, along with the first Guide for Managing Security Breach Notifications, published a tool intended to assist data controllers in their risk assessment, which, based on various factors (volume of affected data, type of data, and impact of the violation), assigned a risk level (from 2 to 100) and, based on that, provided a result regarding the necessity or not of notifying the security breach to the AEPD (and to the affected parties). In this regard, the tool only indicated the need to notify the breach to the AEPD in those cases where the risk level was: (i) from a quantitative perspective, greater than 20; or (ii) from a qualitative perspective, high based on the analyzed factors.  
That is to say, according to the analysis of this tool, it was not sufficient, as the AEPD now indicates, that “there is a probability (whatever it may be) that there is a risk (whatever it may be)” for the security breach to be reportable to the AEPD, but that such risk should be sufficiently appreciable and probable (in the previous example, greater than 20).  
ENDESA argues that in a similar sense, the current Guide for the notification of personal data breaches from the AEPD states literally that: “It is not mandatory to notify all personal data breaches, since the GDPR provides an exception to this obligation when, in accordance with the principle of proactive responsibility, the controller can guarantee that it is unlikely that the personal data breach entails a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.”  
ENDESA points out these last two words, “unlikely” and “risk,” as it is relevant to bring up the footnotes associated with them in the aforementioned Guide from the AEPD. In this regard, both footnotes associated with the previous words...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
149/214  
In the Guide, reference is made to the Guidelines on the notification of personal data breach in accordance with Regulation 2016/679 published by the Article 29 Working Party (now the EDPB), in which it is stated that:  
i  
“When the breach relates to personal data revealing racial or ethnic origin, political opinions, religious or philosophical beliefs, trade union membership, or that includes genetic data, data concerning health or data concerning a person's sex life, or criminal convictions and offenses or related security measures, it will be considered likely that such damage and harm will occur”; and  
ii  
“…immediately after becoming aware of a breach, it is of vital importance that the data controller not only seeks to contain the incident but also assesses the risk that may arise from it.”  
ENDESA claims that, as can be observed from the first mention, if in those cases where the security breach impacts special categories of data, it is considered likely that damage and harm will occur to the data subjects, “a sensu contrario” it must be interpreted that, in the remaining cases (where special categories of data are not affected – as is the case in this file) this will not be the case by default, so the probability that the breach constitutes a risk to the data subjects must be calculated and assessed. Otherwise, the Guidelines of the Article 29 Working Party would not have made this distinction.  
ENDESA indicates that, in the case at hand, it determined, in its first two risk analyses of the incident, that it was not likely that it posed a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected natural persons, among other aspects because no special category of data had been affected.  
ENDESA explains that, returning to footnote number (ii) mentioned above, the Article 29 Working Party indicates that it is the responsibility of the data controller, in addition to containing the incident, to assess the risk that the security breach poses to the affected data subjects. That is to say, the data controller (in this case, Endesa), prior to notifying the security breach to the AEPD, must assess the risk that it poses to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects and, based on that assessment and the risk, decide whether or not to make such notification. Therefore, it would directly contravene the guidelines of the Article 29 Working Party, and the literal wording of Article 33.1 of the GDPR, as indicated by the AEPD in its Proposal for Resolution; that is, that notification to the supervisory authority must be made whenever “there is a probability (of any kind) that there is a risk (of any kind).”  
Furthermore, in this regard, ENDESA brings to attention the Guidelines 1/2021 on examples of notification of personal data breaches issued by the EDPB, to which the AEPD itself refers in its Proposal for Resolution to justify its conclusion. In these Guidelines, in several of its examples (more specifically in cases numbers 9, 13, and 15) where the Committee concludes that it would not be necessary to make a notification to the supervisory authority, it is indicated that, although there is a risk, it is low. That is to say, the Committee itself does not share the interpretation made by the AEPD in the Proposal for Resolution.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
150/214  
solution by virtue of which the determination of any risk, “whatever it may be,” would lead to the conclusion that it is necessary to notify the security breach to the supervisory authority.  
More specifically, one of the examples indicates: “In the specific case described, the risk is low, as special categories of personal data were not involved…” (case No. 13) and in another it is stated that “it was unlikely that this data security breach would pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, so no notification to the SA or to the affected data subjects was necessary” (case No. 15). ENDESA claims that, as can be observed, contrary to what the AEPD indicates in its Proposal for Resolution, neither any risk (“whatever it may be”), nor any probability (“whatever it may be”), turns, in the eyes of the EDPB, a security breach into one that must be notified in accordance with Article 33 of the GDPR.  
That is to say, in accordance with Guidelines 1/2021, in those cases where it is determined that the risk is low (for example, because it does not affect special categories of data) or that the probability of the breach posing a risk is remote (“unlikely”), a notification to the supervisory authority would also not be necessary. This is specifically the case at hand, in which Endesa, after conducting the corresponding risk and probability analyses, determined, on two occasions, that both the risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects was low, and the probability of that risk materializing was remote.  
And that, when the circumstances of the case changed, Endesa conducted a third analysis whose result indicated that at that moment it was indeed necessary to notify the security breach to the AEPD. It is worth highlighting that, if Endesa's intention from the very beginning had been to conceal this incident from the AEPD, it would not have carried out the notification of the breach once it considered it to be notifiable. And it did not do so until then because it was convinced, after conducting the appropriate analyses, that, up to that moment: (i) the risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects was low; and (ii) the probability of that risk materializing was remote.  
Therefore, ENDESA claims that it is not true, as indicated in the Proposal for Resolution, that it did not notify the incident at the first moment it became aware of the announcements published on Facebook because it was conducting a “detailed examination of the situation.” On the contrary, Endesa conducted that examination of the situation as soon as it became aware of it, but determined, following the guidelines of the AEPD and the EDPB in this regard, that the notification threshold set out in Article 33 of the GDPR was not reached.  
Notwithstanding the above, ENDESA is surprised that the AEPD, in its Proposal for Resolution, beyond indicating that it disagrees with the assessment of the non-necessity of notification of the breach made by Endesa in its first two reports [since, contrary to what is indicated in its own guidelines and in the guidelines issued by the EDPB, the AEPD understands in this case that notification to the supervisory authority must be made whenever “there is a probability (whatever it may be) that there is a risk (whatever it may be)”], does not substantiate the presence of intent in Endesa's actions. That is to say, given that the first two reports prepared by Endesa analyzing the incident were drafted (in ENDESA's opinion) based on an interpretation  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
151/214  
reasonable interpretation of the applicable data protection regulations and the interpretative guidelines, which demonstrates (in its opinion) due diligence, Endesa understands that the imposition of a sanction for non-compliance with Article 33 of the GDPR (especially when a sanction of such magnitude is proposed) must be preceded by a rigorous and complete legal foundation regarding the presence of intent in Endesa's actions, which is not provided in the Proposal for Resolution.  
In this regard, this Agency acknowledges that the wording of the phrase in the proposal for resolution, contested by ENDESA, stating that “It is simply enough that there is a probability (whatever it may be) that there is a risk (whatever it may be). It is also not necessary for that risk to have materialized for such an incident to be reportable; it is enough that it is probable,” may not have been the most appropriate, or rather, it could require further development in its explanation.  
Of course, not all violations of the security of personal data need to be reported to the corresponding supervisory authority. However, it is true that Article 33 of the GDPR contains a sort of general rule according to which all violations of the security of personal data must be reported to the supervisory authority, unless “…it is unlikely that such a security breach would pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons.”  
From what is stipulated in the GDPR and from everything explained in the Guide for the Notification of Personal Data Breaches by the AEPD (both in its first version and in its current version), as well as in the aforementioned Guidelines 1/2021 on examples of notification of personal data security breaches, it follows that (as ENDESA states) “it is the responsibility of the data controller, in addition to containing the incident, to assess the risk that the security breach poses to the affected data subjects' rights and freedoms. That is, the data controller (in this case, Endesa), prior to making the notification of the security breach to the AEPD, must assess the risk that this poses to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects and, based on that assessment and the risk, decide whether or not to make such notification.” But this does not exclude the Agency's ability to subsequently evaluate the compliance with the GDPR of the decisions made by the data controller regarding general data protection.  
From all the documentation in the present sanctioning procedure, this Agency has concluded that ENDESA has not correctly classified the violation of the security of personal data as reportable.  
ENDESA cites the first Guide for the Management of Security Breach Notifications, which included a tool to help assess the risk of incidents and the need to notify them to the AEPD and affected individuals.  
First of all, this Agency wishes to point out that the conclusions that the data controller may reach by using this Guide and the tool in question are nothing more than a guide, precisely, a decision-making aid, but the decision on whether a security breach of personal data should be reported to the AEPD belongs solely and exclusively to the data controller, and its use in no case represents the Agency's pronouncement on the application of Article 33 of the GDPR regarding a specific incident.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
152/214  
Secondly, the tool in question indicated that an incident should be reported to the AEPD when the level of risk and its probability were so low that it could be disregarded. To this end, it evaluated a series of factors and assigned a risk score (which had to be greater than 20 to be considered reportable) and also analyzed different factors (if more than one coincided, it had to be reported).  
As stated in the proven facts of this procedure, on September 13, 2021, according to Document number 10 accompanying ENDESA's submission of July 26, 2022, the first assessment of the personal data security breach was carried out, where the information obtained up to that moment was recorded in a document signed on September 14, 2021.  
In said document, it is indicated that the main data contained in ***PLATAFORMA.1 are: name, surname, and address of the client, NIF, CUPS Number, contracted products, and that the estimated number of affected records would be between 100 and 1,000.  
Based on the data contained in this document, ENDESA carried out a risk assessment following the tool provided by this Agency, in which it assigned the following score to the incident:  
- Volume: between 100 and 1,000 records (2). [Not highlighted.]  
- Non-sensitive data (x1) [Not highlighted.]  
- Impact: External (6). [Highlighted]  
And a risk of 3 x (1 x 6) was assigned: 12  
Therefore, due to being a value lower than 20 and not coinciding with more than one highlighted factor (in another color), ENDESA considered that the incident was not reportable.  
However, ENDESA itself has indicated in that same document that the data affected by the personal data security breach included the name, surname, address, among others, along with their NIF. Data that unequivocally identifies the interested parties.  
On this point, it should be noted that, Recitals 51 and 75 of the GDPR distinguish a group of personal data that by their nature are particularly "sensitive" due to the significant risk that their processing may entail for fundamental rights and freedoms. Their common denominator is the risk they pose to fundamental rights and freedoms, as their processing can lead to physical, material, or immaterial harm and detriment.  
Included in this group or category are the specially protected data regulated by Article 9 of the GDPR - Recital 51 of the GDPR - and, moreover, many others that are not mentioned in that provision. Recital 75 mentions in detail the personal data whose processing may entail variable degrees of severity and probability of risk for the rights and freedoms of natural persons as a consequence of potentially causing physical, material, or immaterial harm and detriment. Among them, it refers to those whose processing "may lead to discrimination issues,  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
153/214  
identity theft or fraud, financial losses, damage to reputation, loss of confidentiality of data subject to professional secrecy, unauthorized reversal of pseudonymization, or any other significant economic or social harm;”  
The numerical identifier of the DNI along with the corresponding verification character for the tax identification number unequivocally identifies a natural person. This quality makes it a particularly sensitive data point because, to the extent that its processing is not accompanied by the necessary technical and organizational measures to ensure that the person identifying themselves with it is indeed its holder, a third party can easily impersonate the identity of a natural person, or, in other words, can commit identity fraud, with the risks this entails for the privacy, honor, and assets of the impersonated individual.  
The breach of the confidentiality principle for which ENDESA is responsible has involved, among other data, the NIF of the interested parties, which allows for their unique identification. For this reason, this Agency has considered in previous sanctioning procedures (for example, PS/00012/2022) that the NIF is a personal data point that is considered comparable to those of special categories, deserving of special protection due to the greater risk they pose to the rights and freedoms of the interested parties. All of this without forgetting that along with the NIF, the personal data of names, surnames, addresses, and bank accounts, among others, of the affected individuals were also compromised by the security breach, from which at least a risk of identity theft or fraud can be inferred.  
Therefore, that initial assessment of the incident was not well conducted, as the risk score should have been higher, and a double value should have been assigned to the type of affected data due to their sensitive nature (the numerical value would have been 24, exceeding the threshold of 20 necessary to consider the incident as reportable). Additionally, these would have been considered significant factors, thus qualitatively also exceeding the threshold necessary to be reportable. Therefore, this Agency reiterates that ENDESA should have notified this Agency of the breach of personal data security much earlier than it did.  
ENDESA cites the current AEPD Guide for the notification of personal data breaches, which indicates that the exception to this notification obligation to the supervisory authority occurs when the controller can guarantee that it is unlikely that the personal data breach poses a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons. Regarding the words “unlikely” and “risk,” it references the Guidelines 1/2021 on examples of notification of personal data security breaches, concluding that when personal data security breaches do not impact special categories of data, “a sensu contrario,” it would not be necessary to notify them by default.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to emphasize that even if the personal data security breach did not affect special categories of data, this does not automatically exempt the obligation to notify the incident to the corresponding supervisory authority; rather, the data controller must, in any case, evaluate the  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
154/214  
risks to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects as well as the likelihood of their occurrence, determining the possible impact of the incident in question.  
In any case, as detailed above, in the present case, both due to the volume of the personal data compromised and the category of such data (in particular, given that the confidentiality of names, surnames, addresses, ID numbers, and even bank account numbers has been compromised, with the consequent risks this could pose to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals), this Agency reiterates that the risk was greater than that assessed by ENDESA and that the notification of the incident should have been made earlier than it was.  
ENDESA also cites some examples (numbers 9, 13, and 15, in particular) from the aforementioned Guidelines 1/2021, to conclude that in these examples it is considered unnecessary to notify the supervisory authority of the breach of personal data security. However, the cited examples would not be extrapolable to the present case.  
Case number 9 refers to a situation of accidental transmission of data to a trusted third party, and the differences with the present case are more than evident: in the example from the Guidelines, it is an unintentional error, unlike what occurs in the present case (which involved an intentional action); the incident only affected the confidentiality of the data, whereas in the present case both the confidentiality and integrity of the data were affected; in the example, only a limited number of individuals were affected, while in the present case, the individuals affected by the incident numbered in the millions; and the person who had access to the data was subject to a duty of professional secrecy (which did not occur in the present case either).  
Case number 13 from the cited Guidelines refers to a postal error, which also did not occur in the present case (as it involved access to ENDESA's computer systems). And in the example, special categories of data were not affected (which can be understood to have occurred in the present case, as detailed above).  
Case number 15 from the cited Guidelines refers to personal data sent by mail by mistake, which also did not occur in the present case. Although both cases involve particularly protected data, it is true that in the example the risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals was considered relatively low, unlike what occurs in the present case. Furthermore, the amount of compromised data and the number of affected individuals was very low (unlike what occurred in the present case).  
Finally, this Agency considers that the example from the cited Guidelines that could be considered most similar to what occurred in the breach of personal data security subject to this sanctioning procedure is possibly case number 8, referring to data exfiltration by an employee, since it was employees of companies working with ENDESA who put the accesses to the systems in question up for sale. While some differences are observed (in the example it is considered that normally only confidentiality is compromised and that special categories of personal data were not compromised), in the same example the Guidelines refer to the fact that “misuse is not excluded.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
155/214  
additional and more serious than the stolen data, depending on the purpose of the processing established by the former employee.” And it concludes: “In summary, since the violation in question will not pose a high risk to the rights and freedoms of individuals, a notification to the supervisory authority will suffice. However, informing the affected parties could also be beneficial for the data controller, as it might be better for them to learn about the data breach from the company rather than from the former employee trying to contact them.” That is to say, even with the noted differences, in this case, the same Guidelines consider that the breach of personal data security should be reported to the supervisory authority.  
Finally, ENDESA states that it did not intend to conceal the incident from the AEPD and that the presence of intent in ENDESA's actions regarding this matter has not been properly substantiated.  
On this issue, this Agency wishes to point out that it has never considered that ENDESA intended to conceal the breach of personal data security or that there was any intent in its actions. Rather, it has considered that when assessing the risk to the rights and freedoms of those affected by the incident, it was not as diligent as it should have been, which is why the assessment was not adequate and it did not make the notification referred to in Article 33 of the GDPR when it should have done so, thus it is considered that ENDESA has infringed said Article 33 of the GDPR.  
  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  
Third.- On the inappropriateness of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR  
ENDESA is interested in clarifying that, contrary to what the Proposal for Resolution indicates, it has not “forgotten” that the obligation of Article 34 of the GDPR is not “activated” when damage has occurred but when it is determined that the breach of security may entail a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties.  
In fact, it argues that this is what ENDESA analyzes on the three occasions when it conducts the corresponding risk analysis that has already been presented to the AEPD. At no point does ENDESA wait, as the AEPD seems to imply, for damage to materialize; rather, it conducts the corresponding analysis in accordance with the GDPR and the published Guidelines on the matter and determines that the risk that the breach may pose to the affected parties is not high.  
And it is only afterwards, and not as part of the analysis already conducted at the time by ENDESA, that it can be seen that said analysis was correct and that the breach did not actually pose a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties. Therefore, ENDESA does not seek to justify the lack of notification to the affected parties under the supposed excuse that they did not suffer damage (the risk analysis was conducted long before knowing whether the affected parties would suffer damage, and ENDESA would continue - and continues - to defend, regardless of whether the outcome is damaging or not, the rigor with...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
156/214  
the one who conducted said analysis and the result thereof), but rather to ascertain that the result of the analysis carried out by Endesa (the incident did not pose a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects) turned out to be more in line with the reality of the consequences than that conducted by the AEPD.  
Notwithstanding the above and that ENDESA disagrees with the result yielded by the risk analysis conducted by the AEPD (the violation entails a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects) and, therefore, with the requirement issued by the AEPD regarding the need to notify the breach to the affected data subjects (especially since, at the date of receipt of said requirement, Endesa had already taken further measures to ensure that there was no longer a probability of the risk identified by the AEPD materializing), Endesa, in order to show its utmost cooperation with this Agency and in an exercise of transparency that is clearly unnecessary from a regulatory standpoint (in its opinion), decided to comply with the AEPD's requirement and provide the requested information to the affected data subjects.  
ENDESA submits, as Document number 2, a statement from ***DEPARTMENT.7 detailing the proactive actions taken by Endesa to personally address the data subjects who may have been affected by the incident, as well as examples of such tasks.  
Regarding the manner in which Endesa notified the data subjects, the AEPD, in the Proposal for Resolution, indicates that it does not assess the appropriateness of using a layered model. ENDESA assumes, therefore, that, as could not be otherwise since this possibility is contemplated in the AEPD's infographic on how to carry out the corresponding notifications of personal data breaches to the data subjects, Endesa cannot be reproached for not providing the data subjects with all the information required by Article 34 of the GDPR.  
Endesa claims that it followed this layered information model to achieve more effective communication with the affected data subjects, taking into account the characteristics of the security breach (since not all data subjects could suffer the same consequences nor were they affected in the same way, the possible risk mitigation measures would vary depending on the specific case, making it more effective for such measures to be communicated to the data subjects in a personalized manner through ***DEPARTMENT.4).  
Taking the above into account, ENDESA is surprised by the assertion made by the AEPD in its Proposal for Resolution, as it contradicts: (i) the fact that the AEPD does not assess the appropriateness of using a layered model (without assessment, there can be no reproach); and (ii) the AEPD's own infographic on how to carry out notifications of personal data breaches to the data subjects.  
Finally, the Proposal for Resolution reiterates that the information provided to the data subjects in the notification made by Endesa is not truthful.  
1 Likewise, in the communication sent to the affected parties, ENDESA did not include a description of the possible consequences that the breach of personal data security could cause them” – Page 144 of the Proposal for Resolution

sedeagpd.gob.es
157/214  
First, because, according to the Proposal for Resolution, the notification indicates that the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data of the affected parties has not been compromised. The AEPD forgets that this notification was made at the end of March 2022, when Endesa: (i) had already proceeded to disable the compromised users; (ii) had already deployed multifactor authentication on the platforms ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and ***HERRAMIENTA.1; and (iii) was already in contact with most of the affected parties, providing them with personalized assistance to, as indicated in the notification, "prevent a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms" of Endesa's customers.  
It is for this reason that Endesa indicates that the confidentiality and integrity of the data has not been compromised (Endesa's intention was to indicate that, since it had implemented appropriate measures to avoid the risk, at that time the breach of confidentiality and the integrity of the data had already been remedied).  
Secondly, the Proposal for Resolution states that "it is also not true that the measures adopted prevented a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms of the affected parties," since the compromised data could be used for other purposes. Endesa can only express its disagreement with this assertion since, as has been demonstrated subsequently, the measures implemented by Endesa were completely effective in preventing any type of harm to the affected parties. If this had not been the case, Endesa, or even the AEPD itself, would have received some complaint for these reasons (which has not been the case).  
ENDESA argues that it is for this reason, and because, at the end of March 2022 (when the notification was made), Endesa: (i) was already in contact with most of the affected parties, providing them with personalized assistance and addressing any consequences that the breach may have caused; (ii) had already blocked any access to the platforms ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 by the compromised users and had implemented multifactor authentication measures; and (iii) already knew that the intentions of the individuals who had used the compromised users were not, as the AEPD indicates, identity theft or obtaining more data through social engineering, which Endesa determined (and informed the affected parties) that the measures adopted prevented any future risk.  
In fact, ENDESA indicates that the ***HERRAMIENTA.1 platform itself, due to the way it was configured prior to the data security breach, prevents the execution of mass downloads of personal data (queries are made on an individualized basis, that is, to access the data of a particular person, it is necessary to first enter a supply point code), so the risk identified in the Proposal for Resolution regarding the possibility that the data could be used for other purposes (e.g., obtaining more data through social engineering) was extremely low and, therefore, negligible in terms of making the notification to the affected parties.  
ENDESA considers that having informed otherwise, when through other channels the Endesa teams were already providing personalized attention to the affected parties and addressing any consequences they may have suffered,

sedeagpd.gob.es

would have led to them receiving contradictory messages that, far from being informative and useful, would have created a false sense of danger that, by the end of March 2022, no longer existed (as has been fully proven a posteriori, in their opinion).  
The AEPD considers, in ENDESA's view erroneously and completely disproportionate, that the assertion included in the communication to the affected parties regarding the confidentiality and integrity of the data not being compromised should be sanctioned with 800,000 euros when, it should be noted, the modification of the wording in the manner proposed by the AEPD would not have changed the purpose intended with this notification (which is none other than allowing the interested parties to take measures to avoid the risk), since the risk, as has been previously stated, was already fully mitigated and/or was being addressed by the specialized teams of Endesa at the time of making said notification.  
Additionally, and as was the case regarding the proposed sanction for the alleged violation of Article 33 of the GDPR, the AEPD, in its Proposal for Resolution, again justifies the imposition of this sanction (for alleged non-compliance with Article 34 of the GDPR) on purely subjective aspects, without rigorously motivating, in their opinion, why they consider that Endesa's actions (which, in their view, were carried out with the utmost diligence, making a reasonable interpretation of the applicable regulations and guidelines) deserve such a disproportionate reproach.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the analysis carried out by ENDESA when determining that the personal data security breach did not need to be communicated to the affected parties was not adequate, which is why the Director of this Agency ordered ENDESA to carry out the communication referred to in Article 34 of the GDPR.  
Regarding ENDESA's assertion that no high risk for the rights and freedoms of individuals was identified, this Agency reiterates that not all risks are related to fraudulent contracts for ENDESA products, but that the risks could be of another nature, since the affected parties could be, for example, victims of phishing or social engineering, another cyberattack, or a change of supply company, or be victims of identity theft and fraud, all of which ENDESA would not be aware of. The fact that this data had been compromised means that its holders lost control over it and that it could be used unlawfully, not only at that moment but in the future.  
Regarding Document number 2 submitted by ENDESA with the allegations to the proposal for resolution of this procedure, the actions of the company referred to are solely concerning the fraudulent contracts carried out by ***COMPANY.1. In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the scope of the personal data security breach exceeded what ***COMPANY.1 could communicate, as more users were compromised than those reported by these providers, and the data contained in ***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2 were exposed to more individuals, not only to those working in these companies.

Regarding the manner in which ENDESA communicated the incident to the interested parties (layered information), this Agency has not made any reproach since it is not a matter of form that is considered infringed by ENDESA, but rather this Agency understands that the content of said communication does not comply with the requirements set forth in Article 34 of the GDPR.  
In particular, concerning the possible consequences of the incident, if ENDESA's aim was for the affected individuals to contact the DPO to explain the consequences in each specific case, this should have been made abundantly clear. To suggest that the phrase “You can consult our privacy policy at www.endesa.com/es/proteccion-datos-endesa, where you will find information regarding the processing of your personal data. If you prefer, you can contact our Data Protection Officer directly by sending a communication to ***EMAIL.1 to learn about the details of the measures taken or to obtain more information” should be interpreted as ENDESA wishing for the affected individuals to contact the DPO to understand the possible consequences that the incident could have for them, is an argument that this Agency does not share at all and considers impossible to defend.  
This Agency also wishes to point out that the consideration that ENDESA has infringed Article 34 of the GDPR does not contradict in any way the possibility of using a layered model to provide the information required by said article nor does it contradict the infographic of the AEPD itself. It is emphasized that Article 34 of the GDPR is not considered infringed due to a defect of form, but rather that the content of such communication has not fulfilled the requirements set by the regulations.  
Regarding the assertion that the information provided in such communication was truthful because measures had already been adopted by March 2022 that remedied the violation of the security of the compromised personal data, this Agency does not share such an interpretation. Even if it were considered true and the risks to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals had been mitigated, it is nonetheless true that the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data had indeed been compromised, and this should have been stated in the communication directed to the interested parties.  
This Agency also does not share ENDESA's opinion that the compromised data could not be used for other purposes, as previously explained. In this regard, this Agency reiterates that ENDESA (and this Agency) would not have been aware of the possible incidents that could have arisen from the incident in question, as the risks are not solely the potential fraudulent contracting of ENDESA products.  
The fact that the platform ***HERRAMIENTA.1 could not perform mass downloads of personal data does not prevent the consideration that the data obtained could have been used for other purposes, especially considering that ENDESA itself was unable to know how many records had been obtained during the months that the incident lasted. Therefore, this Agency in no way considers such risk as “negligible” regarding the communication of the incident to the interested parties.

28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
160/214  
This Agency also disagrees with the assertion that the opposite would have led to receiving contradictory messages and creating a false sense of danger. On the contrary, this Agency considers it blameworthy that ENDESA's message created a false sense of absence of risks, which it believes was not real, for the reasons previously explained.  
Regarding the claim that the amount of the fine for the infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR (800,000 euros) is disproportionate because the risk was completely mitigated by ENDESA, this Agency reiterates that this was not the case, as ENDESA focuses solely and exclusively on the risks of fraudulent contracting of ENDESA products and not on other risks. The risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals still exists because they have lost control of their data.  
Finally, this Agency wishes to point out that the imposition of this sanction is in no way justified by purely subjective aspects or without explaining why ENDESA has not acted diligently. On the contrary, the reasons why ENDESA should have made the communication required by Article 34 of the GDPR have been detailed, which was not properly carried out by ENDESA, violating the provisions of the applicable regulations.  
Furthermore, this Agency reiterates what was stated in response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement of the current sanctioning procedure.  
For all the above reasons, the present allegation is dismissed.  
Fourth.- On the impropriety of the sanction imposed for the alleged infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR  
ENDESA argues that, regarding the sanction imposed for the alleged violation of Article 44 of the GDPR, there are various procedural and substantive defects that, if maintained in the resolution to be issued, would be grounds for absolute nullity of the same.  
4.1 ENDESA indicates that, in procedural terms, the Agency has begun with a direct accusation in the Initiation Agreement of the current procedure, without any basis whatsoever and without any factual support. The Initiation Agreement includes a sanction of, no more and no less than, two million euros (2,000,000€), for failing to comply with the obligations established in the GDPR for international data transfers. The Agreement further aggravated this infringement due to alleged serious negligence, given that "more than four years have passed since the GDPR became applicable, yet ENDESA has not adapted the international transfers of its personal data to what is required (...)".  
ENDESA finds this approach of the Initiation Agreement and the way in which this sanction has continued to be maintained in the Proposal for Resolution to be seriously disconcerting, since:  
a) The Agency seems to refer to ENDESA's state of compliance regarding international transfers as of the initiation of the procedure (when it refers to...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
161/214  
referring to the more than four years since the mandatory compliance with the GDPR), instead of referring to the date of the facts subject to the procedure.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that, in this case, Endesa did not comply with the provisions of Decision 2021/914 despite the extension granted therein and continued to carry out international transfers to ***COMPANY.15 without assessing whether the internal regulations of ***COUNTRY.2 allowed for the guarantee of the high level of protection that the GDPR confers to data subjects, which indicates a greater intensity in the lack of diligence, since after the CJEU ruling of 16/07/2020, Endesa was aware of the limitations that international transfers to a third country whose internal regulations might not guarantee an adequate level of protection could pose for the rights and freedoms of individuals, a circumstance that is aggravated by the temporal persistence of the non-compliance, reasons that allow for the application of the aggravating factor of culpability.  
Therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
b) The Agency, despite having been aware of the existence of service providers outside the European Union during the exhaustive prior investigation it conducted, at no time requested from Endesa any justification or information regarding the mechanisms applied by Endesa to carry out those international data transfers. However, it includes a sanction for this reason in an absolutely unprecedented amount.  
Regarding this lack of prior inquiry, the Proposal for Resolution states that prior actions are not mandatory, according to Article 67 of the LOPD-gdd. ENDESA agrees with this assertion, but what is also true, and it understands leaves no doubt, is that a sanction cannot be imposed without having conducted a minimal investigation of the supposedly attributed fact, or without having the slightest indication of the existence of a fact constituting an infringement, or at least, without having made a mere inquiry about the existence of the fact for which one wishes to impose a sanction. The attribution of a non-existent and unverified fact, along with the enormous amount of the fine associated with such a non-existent fact, is what is truly surprising.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that, as determined by Article 67.1 of the LOPDGDD: “Before the adoption of the agreement to initiate proceedings, and once the complaint has been admitted for processing, if any, the Spanish Agency for Data Protection may carry out prior investigative actions…” Therefore, the carrying out of these investigative actions is not mandatory.  
However, it is not true that no actions were taken to verify whether there were guarantees supporting the international transfers and, if applicable, to determine the possible existence of an infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR. Thus, it could be established that neither in the contracts with its suppliers provided by Endesa nor in the General Conditions of Contracting published on its website: https://globalprocurement.enel.com/es/documentos/condiciones-generales-contratacion was there any reference to the existence of adequate guarantees, so when the initiation of the sanctioning procedure was agreed upon, there were indeed indications of a possible infringement.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
162/214  
Therefore, the present allegation is dismissed.  
c) ENDESA argues that, considering that the security breach was notified on February 10, 2022, it is not until June 26, 2023, during the evidentiary phase of the file - that is, more than sixteen months later and only because ENDESA has pointed out the incorrectness of the accusation - that the AEPD requests, for the first time, information regarding the international transfers, which was provided by ENDESA in a timely manner, to demonstrate the regularity of the mechanisms employed.  
ENDESA also points out that the Agency not only requested information on how the transfers were carried out at the time of the events but also currently. Therefore, ENDESA has the impression that attempts are being made to seek arguments to maintain an accusation initially made without any factual support, redirecting it towards another assumption that has nothing to do with the facts subject to the procedure. Furthermore, the requested information refers to requirements that not only do not exist or did not exist in any imperative norm but that, as will be argued later, were not clearly identified on the dates when the events occurred (in fact, even as of the current date, ENDESA considers it extremely doubtful that it can be argued that an Evaluation of an International Transfer is more or less correct using one methodology or another).  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that the proposed resolution does not modify the accusation, which remains the infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR due to the lack of guarantees for the corresponding international transfers. The contractual clauses signed between the parties may not be binding for the authorities of ***COUNTRY.2 and therefore may not be sufficient to guarantee adequate protection; hence, Clause 14 of the Implementing Decision includes the need to ensure, through an appropriate assessment of the legislation of the country in question, a level of protection equivalent to that guaranteed within the Union by the GDPR. The lack of evaluation regarding the aspects contemplated in this clause means that the guarantee that it intends to offer cannot be considered provided and, therefore, it cannot be guaranteed that the transfer is subject to adequate guarantees in the sense of Article 46.1.c) of the GDPR.  
This is reflected in Recital (19) of the Implementing Decision: “The transfer and processing of personal data under standard contractual clauses must not take place if the law and practices of the third country of destination prevent the data importer from complying with those clauses (...). The parties must ensure that, at the time of submitting to the standard contractual clauses, they have no reason to believe that the law and practices applicable to the data importer do not comply with these requirements,” which is guaranteed through the assessment referred to in letter b) of Clause 14.  
As can also be inferred from the JUDGMENT OF THE COURT OF JUSTICE (Grand Chamber) of July 16, 2020.  
“102 The referring court also wishes to know what elements must be taken into consideration to determine the adequacy of the level of protection in the context of a transfer of personal data to a third country based on the standard data protection clauses adopted pursuant to Article 46(2)(c) of the GDPR.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
163/214  
103    In this regard, although that provision does not enumerate the different elements to be taken into account to assess the adequacy of the level of protection that must be respected in the context of a transfer of such characteristics, Article 46, paragraph 1, of the aforementioned Regulation specifies that the interested parties must enjoy adequate guarantees and have enforceable rights and effective legal remedies.  
104    The evaluation required for this purpose in the context of a transfer of such characteristics must, in particular, take into account both the contractual stipulations agreed upon between the controller or the processor established in the Union and the recipient of the transfer established in the third country in question, as well as, regarding any potential access by public authorities of that third country to the transferred personal data, the relevant elements of the legal system of that country. As for this latter aspect, the elements to be considered in the context of Article 46 of the aforementioned Regulation correspond to those mentioned, in a non-exhaustive manner, in Article 45, paragraph 2, of the same.  
105    Therefore, it is appropriate to respond to the second, third, and sixth preliminary questions that Article 46, paragraphs 1 and 2, letter c), of the GDPR must be interpreted as meaning that the adequate guarantees, enforceable rights, and effective legal remedies required by those provisions must ensure that the rights of individuals whose personal data is transferred to a third country on the basis of standard data protection clauses enjoy a level of protection that is substantially equivalent to that guaranteed within the Union by the aforementioned Regulation, interpreted in light of the Charter. For this purpose, the evaluation of the level of protection guaranteed in the context of a transfer of such characteristics must, in particular, take into account both the contractual stipulations agreed upon between the controller or the processor established in the Union and the recipient of the transfer established in the third country in question, as well as, regarding any potential access by public authorities of that third country to the personal data transferred in this manner, the relevant elements of the legal system of that country and, in particular, those mentioned in Article 45, paragraph 2, of the aforementioned Regulation.  
In summary, Commission Implementing Decision 2021/914 of June 4, 2021, addresses the need to conduct an evaluation of the relevant elements of the legal system of the country in its clause 14, so it cannot be asserted that in the absence of an adequate evaluation of the law of the destination country, the guarantee contemplated by the Implementing Decision has been adopted.  
Therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
d) In the Proposal for Resolution, the Agency continues to impose a sanction, now limited to the alleged insufficiency of the Transfer Impact Assessment mechanism (known as “TIA,” the English acronym for “Transfer Impact Assessment”), with the same amount previously imposed in the Initiation Agreement for the...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6

absolute absence of legal coverage for the transfers. ENDESA alleges that, despite the information provided, a fine is imposed in an amount that is likely the highest imposed in the entire European Union for insufficient TIA in international transfers (although it is also possibly the only sanction imposed in the entire European Union for this reason). That is to say, despite the change in the charge (it is no longer the absence of mechanisms, but the insufficiency of the same), the amount of the sanction remains unchanged.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that there is no modification in the charge. However, the amount of the sanction has been modified, as will be explained later.  
e) Furthermore, ENDESA alleges, also in relation to proportionality, that the fact that the Agency considers that the transfer mechanism employed is not sufficiently executed (insufficiency of the TIA) should nullify the modifying cause of liability consisting of assessing serious negligence for not applying adequate mechanisms for the transfer. In this sense, the Agency applied this aggravation because it indicated that, having passed four years since the obligation of the GDPR, the transfers had not been adapted to the new regulations. By now appreciating that the mechanism employed does exist, although it is not considered entirely correct, it is clearly evident that there was no negligence of any kind. The aggravating factor would disappear and, however, the amount of the sanction remains.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates the lack of diligence observed in ENDESA's actions, which did not comply with the provisions of Decision 2021/914 regarding the extension granted therein and continued to carry out international transfers to ***COMPANY.15. While it is noted that the transfer made to ***COUNTRY.1 ended before the deadline set in the execution decision, a reduction of the sanction is warranted for this reason.  
f) In addition, the Proposal for Resolution states that Endesa “has made international transfers to ***COUNTRY.1 and ***COUNTRY.2, and continues to make international transfers to ***COUNTRY.2 (…)”. Notwithstanding the fact that ENDESA has not denied this at any time, and that such transfers are made in strict compliance with the regulations (as it states), as pointed out during the evidentiary phase, the analysis of the international transfers made by Endesa, both on the dates of the events subject to the sanctioning procedure and, especially, on the current date, ENDESA understands cannot be subject to specific analysis within the framework of the present sanctioning procedure. The Agency would be acting to accumulate various sanctioning procedures without complying with the established rules in the legal system for the exercise of the sanctioning power of the administration.  
In particular, it alleges that without opening a specific process regarding specific facts (the initially charged facts, the non-existence of mechanisms regulating the transfers, were non-existent) and, additionally, opening a kind of “separate piece” of investigation on the matter in the midst of the evidentiary phase of the sanctioning file initiated in relation to a personal data security breach, placing Endesa in a position of procedural vulnerability and violating its rights.

constitutional right to defense, in its aspect of access to the accusation from the moment it occurs and giving the possibility to argue about the alleged facts (the facts initially contained in the Initiation Agreement were, in their opinion, non-existent, so the new approach adopted in the Proposal for Resolution could never be subject to allegations by Endesa).  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it is stated in the initiation agreement that neither in the contracts provided with their suppliers nor in the General Contracting Conditions published on their website: https://globalprocurement.enel.com/es/documentos/condiciones-generales-contratacion was there any reference to the existence of the same. Therefore, the right to defense has not been violated. ENDESA has been able to formulate allegations and provide whatever it deemed appropriate regarding its rights both to the initiation agreement and to the proposal for resolution.

g) ENDESA alleges that a sanction is imposed for a formal requirement that is not included in the GDPR, nor in the LOPD-gdd, nor in any other imperative norm of the legal system of the European Union (“EU”). Specifically, the aforementioned Transfer Impact Assessment mechanism, which is, in reality, nothing more than a mechanism or methodology of analysis derived from the Judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union (“CJEU”) in the Schrems II case, but which is not even established in that judgment in detail, but is progressively developed from the year 2020 in various interpretative guidelines from different bodies, such as the EDPB, which as late as June 2021 published its Recommendations 1/2021 on this matter. ENDESA indicates that the EDPB guidelines do not constitute imperative law, but are configured as interpretative guidelines of the regulation that should be considered as best practices or compliance formulas.  
Notwithstanding this, Endesa indicates that it duly fulfilled its obligations of due diligence and proactive responsibility, attending to the requirements of the aforementioned Judgment and the Recommendations of the EDPB, going so far as to apply the most rigorous compliance formulas existing among experts in the matter. Formulas that, however, the Agency considers to be insufficient.  
Undoubtedly, in its opinion, the sanction of €2,000,000 for insufficiency of the TIA, if maintained, will be discussed among privacy experts throughout the EU and the rest of the world as a milestone, a novelty, and a first case of sanction for this reason, and will be criticized both for the strictness of its interpretation of the Schrems II Judgment and for the amount of the imposed sanction.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that compliance with clause 14 of the Implementing Decision is not a formal requirement but a necessary requirement for international transfers to be carried out with the necessary guarantees for the protection of the fundamental rights of the data subjects. Nevertheless, in any case, the amount of the sanction has been reduced.  
4.2. In addition to all the above, and already in the material scope, ENDESA categorically denies having failed to comply with the obligations established by the GDPR in relation to international transfers, for the following reasons:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
166/214  
a) Firstly, because, contrary to what the Agency indicated in the Initiation Agreement, Endesa had the legally required mechanisms to carry out international data transfers outside the EU. Specifically, it had signed the standard contractual clauses of the EU that were valid at the time they were used, namely, the model approved by the European Commission decisions 2001/497/EC, 2004/915/EC, and 2010/87/EU, which were perfectly valid until December 27, 2022, nothing more and nothing less.  
The Resolution Proposal extensively develops the requirements set forth by the “Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914 of June 4, 2021, regarding standard contractual clauses for the transfer of personal data to third countries in accordance with Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council” (hereinafter, the “Decision”). Endesa argues that the content of this Decision has nothing to do with the object of the present procedure, since that Decision approved models of standard contractual clauses that could coexist with the previous ones, which were perfectly valid until December 27, 2022.  
In this regard, it should be noted that this Agency has taken into account that contracts entered into before September 27, 2021, under Decision 2001/497/EC or Decision 2020/87/EU could remain in force until December 27, 2022. However, from that date onwards, Endesa has not complied with the requirement set forth in clause 14 of the aforementioned Implementing Decision, which is why this Agency considers that Endesa has infringed Article 44 of the GDPR, and therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
b) Secondly, Endesa claims that the reinforcement mechanisms for the Standard Contractual Clauses were analysis protocols derived from the ruling of the CJEU in the Schrems II case on July 16, 2020. However, the specification of these protocols was neither easy, nor quick, nor unequivocal, nor has it been incorporated into any type of binding regulation in the European Union.  
Endesa indicates that the EDPB, after publishing an initial document titled “Frequently Asked Questions on the Judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union in Case C-311/18,” took until June 18, 2021, to publish its document “Recommendations 01/2020 on measures that complement transfer instruments to ensure compliance with the level of protection of personal data in the EU” (the “Recommendations”). The Recommendations are even subsequent to the Decision of the European Commission.  
Delving into detail, Endesa explains that the Decision is based on the need for the signing parties (data exporter and importer) to ensure that they have carried out a series of analyses that, as the Agency cites in its Resolution Proposal, are incorporated into the new standard contractual clauses (which are not the ones Endesa had at the time of the events) through declarations from the parties.  
For its part, the Recommendations establish a framework and a methodology for evaluation and analysis that may allow for the conclusion that the specific transfer does not pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects, taking into account all relevant elements, such as the legislation.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
167/214  
the destination country, the type of data transferred, or the profile of the recipient, among others.  
ENDESA claims that it has provided sufficient documentation to the file to demonstrate, firstly, that it had a legally valid mechanism for the transfer (the standard contractual clauses of the Commission still valid at that time) and, secondly, that it had exercised due diligence by conducting a Transfer Impact Assessment, taking into account the elements mentioned in the Schrems II ruling.  
ENDESA believes that the Proposal for Resolution does not justify the imposition of the sanction in this area. Not only because Endesa (in its opinion) has indeed demonstrated compliance with legal obligations and best practices in the matter, both for the case of ***COUNTRY.1 and for the case of ***COUNTRY.2 (despite the difference that the AEPD seems to see, in both cases a Transfer Impact Assessment has been carried out and standard contractual clauses were in place).  
But because ENDESA understands that if the AEPD considers that the data transfers to ***COUNTRY.1 and ***COUNTRY.2 violate the GDPR, it must specify the exact reasons for such consideration.  
And, if it is to assert that Endesa's criteria are incorrect and the legislation of both countries does not meet a sufficient level of security, it must indicate in what aspects the Agency believes that those legal systems incur such deficiencies. This is especially relevant in the case of these two countries, ***COUNTRY.1 and ***COUNTRY.2, for which the AEPD had expressly authorized such processing operation to Endesa under the legal framework prior to the GDPR.  
In ENDESA's opinion, it is very difficult to understand and justify how, in this case, a data transfer expressly authorized by the Agency could cease to be correct when the legislation of the destination country remains the same (or, even, may have improved over the years) and Endesa has also added the analyses and methods required by the new European legislation in this regard.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that clause 14 of the aforementioned Implementing Decision does contain a precise statement regarding the need to take into account, to provide the guarantee, whether the law and practices of the destination country respect the rights and freedoms of the data subjects, and in particular indicates that the following aspects should be considered:  
i) the specific circumstances of the transfer, such as the length of the processing chain, the number of parties involved, and the transmission channels used; the anticipated subsequent transfers; the type of recipient; the purpose of the processing; the categories and format of the personal data transferred; the economic sector in which the transfer takes place; the location of the storage of the transferred data;  
ii) the law and practices of the third country of destination—especially those requiring the communication of data to public authorities or authorizing access by such authorities—that are relevant given the specific circumstances of the transfer, as well as the applicable limitations and guarantees;  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
168/214  
iii) the relevant contractual, technical, or organizational guarantees provided to complement the guarantees set forth in this terms and conditions document, particularly including the measures applied during the transfer and processing of personal data in the destination country.  
However, the documentation provided by ENDESA does not assess the law and practices of ***COUNTRY.2 regarding the requirement to communicate personal data to the authorities of the country.  
ENDESA understands that this is no different from the exercise carried out by the CJEU in the Schrems II ruling, where it detailed one by one the reasons, cases, situations, and specific examples for which the European Court considered that the legal framework of the United States did not meet the sufficient guarantees that must be required from the perspective of the EU regarding data security. That detailed and substantiated breakdown allowed the CJEU to declare the invalidity of the Privacy Shield agreement. However, ENDESA claims that the Agency, in this case, determines that it has violated the GDPR because, despite the existence of a valid transfer mechanism (standard contractual clauses) and a privacy impact assessment, the data transfer poses a risk to the rights and freedoms of individuals. The vagueness of this accusation can only be grounds for absolute nullity (in ENDESA's opinion), as a consequence of the lack of specificity in ENDESA's actions.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that it has not made any pronouncement on whether the law of ***COUNTRY.2 offers an adequate level of protection or not, a matter that must be assessed and documented by ENDESA so that the guarantee contained in clause 14 of the Adequacy Decision can be considered provided, and to base the transfer on the standard contractual clauses contained in Commission Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914 of June 4, 2021.  
For all the above reasons, this allegation is dismissed.  
c) ENDESA claims that the level of legal uncertainty generated by the Schrems II ruling regarding the manner of conducting international data transfers has been a constant since its publication, to the point that, even currently, there is no absolute consensus on how to conduct the necessary analyses prior to making a data transfer outside the EU, beyond the "soft law" norms such as the aforementioned Recommendations of the EDPB. The vast majority of European companies as of now, September 2023, still do not know what they need to comply with or how, and those that have made some attempts to adapt to what is supposed to be required by the Schrems II ruling are only conducting tentative work that has not been validated by any standard. Obviously, this situation affects the element of culpability as the essence of sanctioning administrative law.  
In that environment, ENDESA understands that imposing a fine of €2,000,000 for the alleged insufficiency of the TIA is presented as an absolutely inappropriate penalty, in addition to being disproportionate and extravagant. ENDESA asserts that the legal uncertainty arising from the indefiniteness of the regulatory framework cannot harm economic operators, especially when they demonstrate having deployed all due diligence.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
169/214  
due diligence to address extremely complex requirements from the most specialized legal perspective in a matter such as personal data protection regulations. And, in parallel, without the Agency pointing out a single instance in which the legislation of the destination country, as well as the specific transfer, could pose a risk to the rights of the data subjects, having previously authorized exactly the same transfer, as stated.  
In this regard, this Agency wishes to point out that, regarding the culpability of ENDESA, it has already been mentioned that the Implementing Decision 2021/914 provided that contracts entered into before September 27, 2021, based on previous Decisions, offered adequate guarantees until December 27, 2022. Therefore, Endesa was aware that from that date onwards, its contract had to incorporate the new contractual clauses that require an assessment of whether the internal regulations of ***COUNTRY.2 guarantee the continuity of the level of protection.
