Fifth.- Regarding the grading of the sanction imposed on Endesa and the relative lack of proportionality of the Proposal for Resolution  
In the Proposal for Resolution, the AEPD, in response to the allegation made by Endesa regarding the relative lack of proportionality of the proposed sanctions to Endesa compared to those proposed in other proceedings (which, in ENDESA's opinion, are much more serious), presents certain reasons that ENDESA considers entirely insufficient to dismiss the allegation made by Endesa.  
And it argues that, although the AEPD indicates that each case is different and must be analyzed according to its own circumstances, there are a series of objective and comparable facts (since they are included in the AEPD's own sanctioning resolution) that leave no room for doubt (in its opinion) in the sense indicated by Endesa in its allegations; that is to say, that the sanctions proposed in the context of this proceeding are entirely disproportionate.  
In this regard, it details, for example:  
a. In relation to the AEPD's resolution regarding procedure E/05937/2021, which concluded with the imposition of a warning sanction even when the notification of the data security breach to the AEPD was reported, according to what the AEPD indicated in its resolution, "after a period exceeding 72 hours from the time it became aware that it had occurred," the AEPD indicates in the Proposal for Resolution that Article 33 of the GDPR was not considered infringed since "the data controller duly indicated the reasons for the delay in notifying the supervisory authority."  
ENDESA explains that the reason for such a delay in procedure E/05937/2021 was that the notification was delayed "due to the investigations carried out to clarify whether there was data access and/or fraudulent activity, which required multiple analyses by the security teams." ENDESA finds it surprising that, in this case, contrary to what the AEPD indicates in the Proposal for Resolution, the AEPD did not consider that "the notification of the breach should not be made after  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
170/214  
that the data controller has conducted a detailed examination of the situation, but only when the controller detects that the breach has occurred, and this analysis may be carried out in parallel with the notification.”  
At this point, ENDESA understands that it can be observed how the AEPD treats two circumstances, which it considers identical (delay in the notification of the breach), in different ways, demonstrating a clear disproportionality (in its opinion).  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the facts subject to both proceedings are not comparable, as detailed in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement of the present procedure.  
For its part, ENDESA itself indicated on page 24 of its allegations to the resolution proposal of the present procedure that “it is not true, as stated in the Resolution Proposal, that Endesa did not notify the incident at the first moment it became aware of the announcements published on Facebook because it was conducting a ‘detailed examination of the situation.’ On the contrary, Endesa conducted this examination of the situation as soon as it became aware of it, but determined, following the guidelines of the AEPD and the CEPD in this regard, that the notification threshold established in Article 33 of the GDPR was not reached.”  
The reasons for ENDESA's delay in notifying the supervisory authority were due to its failure to properly qualify the incident as notifiable, which is why this Agency considers that it violated Article 33 of the GDPR.  
Therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
b. Regarding the AEPD's resolution related to procedure PS/00118/2020, which concluded with the imposition of a warning sanction even when: (i) the notification of the data security breach to the AEPD was made two months after the investigated entity became aware of it; (ii) the investigated entity in this case did not provide the AEPD with the risk analysis of the processing for which it is responsible (while Endesa did); and (iii) the investigated entity stated that “the machine affected by the security incidents was technically obsolete,” and therefore did not have adequate security measures for the risk posed by the processing, the AEPD indicates in the Resolution Proposal that “the degree of reproachability of the actions of the data controller allowed for the substitution of the imposition of a fine with a warning.”  
ENDESA finds it surprising how, in a case where the data controller: (i) notifies a security breach late (with a 2-month delay) without any apparent reason (in its opinion), when in the case at hand, Endesa has justified how it carried out risk analyses of the breach on three occasions, determining in the last one that it was likely to pose a risk to the rights and freedoms of the data subjects; and (ii) does not have, in its opinion, the minimum required security measures (conducting risk analyses and maintaining updated systems), when Endesa conducted the corresponding risk analysis that it provided to the AEPD and had security measures that, in ENDESA's opinion, were adequate to the risk, the AEPD understands that its conduct...  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
171/214  
conduct is not deserving of reproach, whereas, in the present case, it not only intends the opposite but proposes to impose the harshest sanctions to date.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates the differences noted in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement of the present procedure: among others, the incident lasted only three days and affected a maximum of 75,000 interested parties, compared to the incident involving ENDESA, which lasted months and affected millions of interested parties, and it was considered that the degree of reproachability of the actions of the data controller allowed for the substitution of a fine with a warning, which is not the case in the present sanctioning procedure against ENDESA. Furthermore, this Agency reiterates that the measures adopted by ENDESA prior to the incident were not appropriate given the risk.  
Recital 148 of the GDPR allows for the substitution of the imposition of a fine with a warning, taking into account "the nature, gravity and duration of the infringement, its intentional character, the measures taken to mitigate the damages suffered, the degree of responsibility or any relevant prior infringement, the manner in which the supervisory authority became aware of the infringement, compliance with measures ordered against the controller or processor, adherence to codes of conduct, and any other aggravating or mitigating circumstances."  
In the present case, this Agency has assessed such circumstances for each of the infringements (all of which are detailed in the Legal Grounds VI, IX, XII, XV, XVIII, respectively) and has considered that what corresponds in the present infringements is not to substitute the fine sanction with a warning.  
Therefore, the present allegation is dismissed.  
c. Regarding the resolution of the AEPD related to procedure PS/00254/2019, which concluded with the imposition of a warning sanction even when the AEPD considered that, from the investigations carried out, there was a lack of diligence on the part of the controller in implementing relevant security measures, even indicating that "the absence of consideration of the risk that unauthorized access by third parties to data of subscribers related to a political party may pose, and its subsequent public dissemination, aggravates the culpable and sanctionable reproach of the conduct."  
ENDESA is again surprised at how the AEPD, in a case where (in its opinion) the AEPD clearly indicates that the data controller did not take into account the risk that unauthorized access to data of subscribers related to a political party may pose, and its subsequent public dissemination, and that, therefore, this "aggravates the culpable and sanctionable reproach of the conduct," states in the Proposal for Resolution that "the circumstances of the breach differ from the severely negligent conduct of ENDESA."  
The AEPD understands that the actions of ENDESA, which in the context of this procedure and, among others: (i) had analyzed the risk posed to the interested parties by the compromised processing; (ii) had implemented security measures deemed adequate (in its opinion) to the risk in accordance with industry standards; (iii) has conducted up to 3 risk analyses regarding the impact that the incident may have...

www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
172/214  
regarding the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects; (iv) has implemented, after the breach, security measures to try to prevent a recurrence of the security violation and has attended to the affected data subjects in such a way that the identified potential risk has not materialized; and (v) has responded to 5 information requests issued by this AEPD, deserves a greater reproach (it insists, the greatest reproach to date regarding security violations) than an entity that had not even considered the risk that access to special categories of data could entail.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the security measures adopted by ENDESA prior to the incident were not appropriate based on the risk, that although it prepared three reports on the breach, it did not correctly classify the incident as reportable, that at best what has been mitigated is only the risk of fraudulent contracting of ENDESA products, and that responding to the information requests from this Agency is a legal obligation established by the applicable regulations, the violation of which may be subject to a specific sanctioning procedure.  
Regarding the paragraph of the resolution of PS/00254/2019 cited by ENDESA, it only referred to the fact that the entity had not considered that the contact data for sending a newsletter related to a political party could be considered special categories of protected data, not due to the data exposed per se but due to its possible combination with other sources.  
In any case, it is no less true that in such resolution it was deemed appropriate to replace the monetary fine with a warning, taking into account, among other factors, the diligence carried out by the entity regarding the prompt communication of the security breach to this Spanish Agency for Data Protection, which has not occurred in the present case.  
Therefore, this allegation is dismissed.  
Considering all of the above, ENDESA believes that the disparity of criteria that the AEPD applies when analyzing the security measures implemented by the entities investigated by the AEPD, as well as the consequences that arise in terms of administrative sanctions from the alleged violations contained in the Proposal for Resolution, is evident, and therefore reiterates the lack of relative (and absolute) proportionality of the Proposal for Resolution.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates everything previously stated, and therefore this allegation is dismissed.  
Finally, ENDESA reiterates, as it did in the context of the allegations to the Initiation Agreement, its disagreement with the application of the circumstances that the AEPD considers aggravating of ENDESA's conduct. Without prejudice to ENDESA's belief that its actions in the context of this file have been fully diligent and, therefore, should not be subject to any reproach (much less to a reproach as disproportionate as that proposed in the Proposal for Resolution), in the event that the AEPD continues to believe otherwise, ENDESA understands that:

(i) A "serious lack of diligence" on the part of Endesa should not be considered an aggravating circumstance. As stated in the previous allegations, ENDESA believes that all actions taken by Endesa within the framework of this file were carried out: (a) following a reasonable interpretation of the applicable data protection regulations and the interpretative guidelines published by the AEPD and the CEPD; and (b) with the utmost diligence possible at a time of maximum concern within the company regarding what had occurred.  
In this regard, given that it has been demonstrated, in accordance with the indications in the previous allegations, ENDESA argues that:  
(a) the report prepared by ***EMPRESA.1 is completely biased and subjective, and there is a well-founded suspicion that it was prepared in bad faith long after the occurrence of the events, and therefore it should not be taken into account within the framework of this file;  
(b) Endesa did not consciously reach out to Facebook Ireland as it did not want to accept Irish jurisdiction for the purposes of any potential subsequent judicial or administrative claim (not for data protection purposes), and doing so had no practical utility in terms of protecting the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects (the credentials of ***HERRAMIENTA.2 and ***HERRAMIENTA.1 compromised, except for one, had already been disabled);  
(c) Endesa had implemented (in its opinion) adequate security measures in its systems commensurate with the risk (as evidenced by the risk analyses provided to the AEPD, the forensic report submitted to the AEPD, and the fact that, for years, no similar incident occurred);  
(d) Endesa deployed, once it became aware of the incident and with the utmost diligence possible, appropriate measures (in its opinion) to disable the compromised accounts as quickly as possible (as indicated in the Proposal for Resolution, several months never elapsed between when Endesa became aware of the account compromise and their disabling);  
(e) When conducting the initial analyses regarding the need to notify the AEPD of the security breach, Endesa made a reasonable interpretation (in its opinion) based on the regulations and guidelines published on the matter and proceeded to notify the AEPD of the breach within 24 hours of becoming aware that it could pose a (real) risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected data subjects. It is, in every way, unsustainable for ENDESA to maintain that "every incident" must be reported to the AEPD;  
(f) When notifying the incident to the affected data subjects, Endesa followed (in its opinion) the indications in the guidelines and infographics of the AEPD, informing the data subjects of those aspects that, given the time elapsed since the breach occurred, were most useful to them; and

(g) Endesa has complied and continues to comply (in its opinion), in a completely scrupulous manner, with the obligation to implement adequate guarantees for the execution of international transfers.  
ENDESA considers that the application of the aggravating factor of serious lack of diligence by the AEPD is totally unfounded and disproportionate, and does not reflect the reality of the actions carried out by Endesa.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates what was stated in response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement and the proposed resolution of this sanctioning procedure:  
(a) The determination of the commission of infringements by ENDESA, as well as its serious negligence, has been evidenced throughout this procedure not by the report of ***COMPANY.1, but by all the documentation in the file, and no content from the aforementioned report is cited in the Legal Grounds to substantiate either the infringements or the grading of the amount of the sanctions, beyond the responses to the allegations presented by ENDESA in this regard;  
(b) ENDESA took more than a month to disable the credentials and although only one credential remained enabled, it was sufficient to access the personal data held in ENDESA's systems. Furthermore, this Agency reiterates that it is only competent to assess whether ENDESA complied with personal data protection regulations and not to evaluate issues related to intellectual property or other areas. In this sense, in the present case, a series of advertisements had been published offering access to ENDESA's systems, which allowed access to personal data owned by ENDESA, which it processed in its capacity as data controller. As such, it should have adopted a series of measures, among others, to ensure that such advertisements were removed. If it was necessary to contact Facebook Ireland for this, then that is what should have been done. Finally, ENDESA also did not have certainty that the compromised users were only those of which it was aware, making it necessary to seek assistance from Facebook to remove the advertisements in question;  
(c) This Agency reiterates that ENDESA did not have appropriate security measures in place prior to the incident, based on the risk (and the forensic report does not indicate anything in this regard, but rather analyzes the measures implemented as of the date of the report). The fact that no similar incident had occurred of which ENDESA was aware does not imply in any way that the security measures were appropriate, but simply that ENDESA was not aware of any similar incident. It also did not adopt adequate measures during the security incident to effectively mitigate the risk, and this is without prejudice to the fact that it adopted a series of measures, such as resetting user passwords, monitoring publications on Facebook, attempting to remove the aforementioned advertisements, disabling the possibility of maintaining simultaneous sessions, and increasing the traceability of access to ***TOOL.1;  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
175/214  
(d) ENDESA became aware on August 24, 2021, that there were advertisements published on Facebook selling access credentials to its systems, and in its first report of the personal data breach, dated September 14, 2021, the validity of the accesses had already been verified. In any case, on October 27, 2021, ENDESA received an email from ***COMPANY.1 indicating that there were five compromised users, and it was not until November 29, 2021, that they were disabled. That is to say, the accounts were not disabled until more than a month had passed.  
(e) This Agency does not assert that “every incident” must be reported to the supervisory authority. However, in this case, this Agency considers that ENDESA has not made an adequate interpretation of whether the breach of personal data security was reportable due to involving a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, as detailed extensively in the Legal Grounds of this resolution.  
(f) ENDESA has not duly provided the data subjects with the information required by Article 34 of the GDPR, as detailed extensively in the Legal Grounds of this resolution.  
(g) ENDESA has complied and continues to comply (in its opinion), in a completely scrupulous manner, with the obligation to implement adequate safeguards for the execution of international transfers; however, ENDESA has not duly complied with the obligations established by Article 44 and subsequent articles of the GDPR, as detailed extensively in the Legal Grounds of this resolution.  
ENDESA considers that the application of the aggravating factor of serious lack of diligence by the AEPD is completely inappropriate and disproportionate, and does not reflect the reality of the actions carried out by ENDESA.  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates everything detailed in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement and proposed resolution of this procedure.  
And for all the above reasons, it considers that ENDESA's actions have been severely negligent, and therefore dismisses this allegation.  
(ii) ENDESA argues that the “linkage of the infringer's activity with the processing of personal data” should not be considered as an aggravating circumstance. As already indicated in the allegations to the Initiation Agreement, the activity of marketing electricity necessarily implies that ENDESA is accustomed to “the processing of personal data,” but that this should constitute an aggravation when the majority of companies worldwide process data of customers and third parties and engage in an activity that involves being in constant contact with them is difficult to perceive, since, if this were the case, this aggravating circumstance (which should be exceptional) would be applied to the vast majority of cases analyzed by the AEPD, completely distorting the nature of said aggravating factor (as is currently happening due to the excessive application, in ENDESA's view, that the AEPD is carrying out of this aggravating circumstance).  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
176/214  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that the Spanish legislator has included in the LO-PDGDD such circumstance when determining the imposition of a sanction, and that this is what this Agency does, taking such circumstance into consideration.  
And that it is not the same for the purposes of deciding on the imposition of an administrative fine to consider an infringement committed by a natural person or a small company unaccustomed to processing personal data, than to consider that of a large company like ENDESA, accustomed to processing personal data of millions of customers and non-customers, with a long history in this regard. Of course, it is considered that the infringement is more serious for the purposes of imposing a fine if the data controller is among the latter, as is the case with ENDESA. This has also been pointed out by the Audiencia Nacional in its SAN 65/2017, of February 7, 2017, when it stated the doctrine of the Supreme Court, "in assessing the degree of diligence, the professionalism or lack thereof of the subject must be especially weighed, and there is no doubt that, in the case examined, when the activity of the appellant involves constant and abundant handling of personal data, it must be insisted upon the rigor and exquisite care to comply with the legal provisions in this regard."  
Additionally, and for the sake of brevity, ENDESA reiterates what has already been indicated in its written allegations to the Initiation Agreement regarding the lack of application, in the Proposal for Resolution, of mitigating factors concerning ENDESA's conduct, such as: (i) the measures taken by ENDESA to mitigate the damages suffered by the affected parties (as evidenced, ENDESA, prior to the initiation of this file, had already adopted the appropriate technical and organizational protective measures, in its opinion, to ensure that no risk materialized for the rights and freedoms of the affected parties and had provided individual and personalized assistance for this purpose); (ii) cooperation with the supervisory authority (ENDESA has responded, in a timely manner, to 6 requests issued by the AEPD); and (iii) the lack of benefits obtained (ENDESA has been clearly harmed by the security breach, as it has seen how third parties outside the organization accessed its systems and the data contained therein for an illegitimate purpose).  
In this regard, this Agency reiterates that (i) having reviewed the contracts made by ***EMPRESA.9 to prevent new fraudulent registrations cannot be considered a mitigating factor, when it is an obligation imposed by the regulations and that having adopted appropriate measures to the risk afterwards has already been assessed as a mitigating factor for the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR; (ii) responding to each of the information requests sent by this Agency is not considered a mitigating factor since it is an obligation contemplated in the applicable regulations, the infringement of which is subject to a subsequent sanctioning procedure (Article 52 of the GDPR and Article 72.1.ñ) of the LOPDGDD); and (iii) the lack of benefits obtained by ENDESA cannot be considered a mitigating factor, according to the Judgment of the Audiencia Nacional, of 05/05/2021, rec. 1437/2020, which states: "On the other hand, it should be appreciated as a mitigating factor the non-commission of a previous infringement. Well, Article 83.2 of the GDPR establishes that the following circumstance must be taken into account for the imposition of the administrative fine, among others: 'e) any previous infringement committed by the data controller or the processor.' It is an aggravating circumstance that the prerequisite does not concur."  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
177/214  
for its application implies that it cannot be taken into consideration, but it does not imply or allow, as the claimant intends, its application as a mitigating factor.”  
Furthermore, this Agency refers to what has already been indicated in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement of the present sanctioning procedure.  
For all the above, the present allegation is dismissed.  
IV  
Integrity and confidentiality  
Article 5.1.f) “Principles relating to processing” of the GDPR establishes:  
“1. Personal data shall be:  
(...)  
f) processed in a manner that ensures an adequate level of security of personal data, including protection against unauthorized or unlawful processing and against accidental loss, destruction or damage, by applying appropriate technical or organizational measures (‘integrity and confidentiality’).”  
In the present case, ENDESA had signed a contract with ***EMPRESA.1 on June 1, 2020, to market ENDESA's products and services through various commercial actions. To this end, on July 28, 2021, ENDESA issued an authorization to ***EMPRESA.1 for subcontracting in favor of ***EMPRESA.9 to handle the telemarketing of clients on behalf of ENDESA, and the start date for the subcontracted work was established as August 1, 2021.  
On August 24, 2021, ENDESA detected an advertisement on the social network Facebook selling a user of the application ***HERRAMIENTA.1 with access to customer information. And on September 10, 2021, ENDESA verified the truthfulness of the advertisement and identified the credentials of the application user that were being used by the advertiser to sell access. That is, by September 2021, it had been verified that the confidentiality of the databases owned by ENDESA had been compromised.  
Subsequently, on October 19, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 detected that a user of ***EMPRESA.9 had attempted to carry out a fraudulent registration, which is why on October 27, 2021, it sent a certified letter to ***EMPRESA.9 communicating the definitive closure of the sales campaign for ENDESA's supply contract effective October 19, after having suspended such campaign provisionally on the same day.  
On October 20, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 sent a communication to ENDESA indicating the operations affected by the fraudulent practice of ***EMPRESA.9, identifying 137 contracts made and another 7 operations pending validation by ENDESA that had not been registered. (…). Also, 172 orders that were pending validation by the back office of ***EMPRESA.9 were identified and blocked so that their contracting would not be processed.  
That is to say, since September 2021, ENDESA was already aware of the existence of a violation of personal data security and since October 2021 that the confidentiality and integrity of the data of 137 interested parties had been compromised and that there were another 179 possible interested parties whose data were more than likely to have been compromised.  
For its part, on October 27, 2021, an email was sent to ENDESA in which an email from the manager of ***EMPRESA.9 was attached, communicating to ***EMPRESA.1 the complaints filed with the Police of ***PAÍS.1 on October 19, 2021, against 23 sales agents who had “been given access to our database to carry out sales, illegally using the databases and the information presented in them for profit from another purpose,” (…)  
On October 27, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 received an email from the manager of ***EMPRESA.9, which that same day ***EMPRESA.1 forwarded to ENDESA, detailing the inquiries made regarding the fraudulent practice carried out by some of its agents and their modus operandi since some of the salespeople had decided to cooperate after seeing the complaint filed against them with the Police.  
In this email, the three ways of operating, according to the management of ***EMPRESA.9, of the salespeople were detailed:  
1. They contacted the client and explained the product; if the client agreed and was aware of the contracting, they proceeded with the closure. However, the client raised the objection indicating that they could not respond to the SMS for various reasons (blocked for SMS, did not have a mobile phone, did not have battery, about to travel, etc.). What the salesperson did was obtain a number from another client who was with ENDESA and asked this latter to respond to the SMS.  
2. Through the internet, the salespeople contracted the program ***PLATAFORMA.1 (***HERRAMIENTA.1). As can be seen in images 4.1, 4.2, 4.3, and 4.4 attached in Document 2 accompanying this writing, different people “marketed” access to the program of ***PLATAFORMA.1 of Endesa. What the salespeople did was use this tool to extract data from Endesa clients, so the recordings were made with other people.  
3. Another case involved clients who at some point did not want to pass texts because they were too long and got tired of receiving several calls. What they did in this case was simulate sales with other people, and the message was also responded to by others.”  
With the detail of this modus operandi, it is evidenced that the integrity and confidentiality of the personal data of at least those clients who had been fraudulently registered had been compromised, since
179/214  
that in order to proceed with the registration it was necessary to access and modify their data with false ones.  
Also, in this email dated October 27, 2021, ***COMPANY.1 identified five users of ***TOOL.1 involved in the violation of personal data security (used by ***COMPANY.9) and attached screenshots of Facebook ads offering the rental and/or sale of access credentials to the ***TOOL.1 application.  
As a result of this situation, ENDESA verified that from July 1, 2021, the sales volume of ENDESA experienced an unusual and unjustified increase (specifically, sales increased by more than 500%), which could be representative of the irregular actions of the personnel hired by ***COMPANY.9.  
On January 17, 2022, new ads were published on Facebook by the user B.B.B., which affected the customer energy databases of ENDESA. Therefore, at least during the month of January 2022, the confidentiality of the data contained in the database owned by ENDESA continued to be inadequately guaranteed.  
On February 7, 2022, ENDESA sent a burofax to FACEBOOK SPAIN S.L. indicating that there were users publishing information that could constitute a crime and requested its removal, from which it is inferred that the ads selling credentials to access ***TOOL.1 were still published, compromising the confidentiality of the data contained in that system.  
On February 8, 2022, after the appropriate investigations, ENDESA became aware of certain coincidences between the data appearing in the published databases and those that could be included in ENDESA's commercial system, ***TOOL.2.  
ENDESA conducted a new assessment of the incident and on February 9, 2022, verified the veracity of the latest ads and the identity of the two advertisers, both former employees of ENDESA's suppliers with access to ***PLATFORM.1 (***TOOL.1). A total of nine compromised users were detected between September 2021 and January 2022, who were being used by the Facebook advertisers to sell access to ***PLATFORM.1 (***TOOL.1). Additionally, it was verified that six of them had access to ***TOOL.2. The validity of the credentials was also confirmed, in all cases assigned to ENDESA suppliers for customer acquisition and service tasks.  
That is to say, it was confirmed that the confidentiality of the data contained in ***TOOL.1 and ***TOOL.2 had not been sufficiently guaranteed since they were made available to unauthorized third parties, which also put the integrity of the data at risk by enabling numerous fraudulent registrations.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
180/214  
On June 27, 2022, the AEPD requested justification from ENDESA regarding the number of individuals affected by the violation of personal data security, and the analysis provided on July 26, 2022, is included below:  
“In the analysis carried out for the correct identification of the customers whose data had apparently been unlawfully taken from the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and, therefore, could potentially have been processed without their consent, the following actions were undertaken:  
(i) First, once the provider ***EMPRESA.1 informed ENDESA of the irregular practice committed by personnel from ***EMPRESA.9 and the appropriate verifications were conducted, it was confirmed that this practice did not extend to other providers.  
(ii) Second, once the possible origin of the allegedly irregular contracts was identified, the temporal scope of their actions was delineated, verifying that from July 1, 2021, the sales volume of ENDESA experienced an unusual and unjustified increase (specifically, sales increased by more than 500%), which could be indicative of the irregular actions of the personnel contracted by ***EMPRESA.9. On October 15, 2021, the activity of this subcontractor was terminated – as a result of the termination of the contract with ***EMPRESA.9 – thus limiting the temporal scope of the analysis of the contracts to the period between July 1 and October 15, 2015 (this year is considered a typographical error and is interpreted as 2021).  
(iii) Finally, a quality control and audit of the contracts carried out by ***EMPRESA.9 within the established temporal scope was conducted. In this analysis, cases were excluded where the customers themselves, on whose behalf a contract was made involving ***EMPRESA.9 – within the defined timeframe – had contacted ENDESA to carry out actions related to their contracts (for example, commercial inquiries), which indicated that the customers were aware of their contracts and did not express any objections.  
The number of potential individuals affected by the resulting incident was: 760 customers.  
That is to say, ENDESA acknowledges that the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data of 760 customers were compromised, but only regarding the contracts in which ***EMPRESA.9 was involved between July 1 and October 15, 2021, in which the customers themselves were allegedly aware of their contracts and did not raise any objections in this regard.  
However, ENDESA has indicated that the fact that two or more individuals could access ***HERRAMIENTA.1 simultaneously with the same user, a multi-session option enabled when the personal data security breach occurred, and that, in relation to the compromised users and the period in which this incident occurred...”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
181/214  
developed, the lack of a record (logs) of the use of the tools ***HERRAMIENTA.1 or ***HERRAMIENTA.2 prevents it from knowing the number of records of the applications that could have been actually accessed, at least since it became aware in August 2021 of the publication of the announcement on Facebook.  
In this regard, ENDESA itself has acknowledged that the approximate volume of supply points that can potentially be consulted from the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.1 is 30.6 million supply points and 8.6 million gas supply points, from which technical data could be known – such as power, voltage, estimated consumption. And that in the case of customers with a valid contract, the approximate volume that a user could consult was 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers, from which the name and surname of the holder of the supply point, National Identity Document number, contract number, CUPS, annual billing amount, debt indicator, information on prices, technical data related to the supply point, consumption data, and usage graph could be consulted.  
Regarding the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.2, ENDESA has acknowledged that “identified committed users could access data related to customers with a valid contract with ENDESA, with the approximate volume during the period from July 1, 2021, to October 15, 2021, being 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers. As for the type of data that could be viewed, they are mainly: name and surname of the holder of the supply point, postal address, National Identity Document number, CUPS, phone number, email, contracted products, invoices, bank account number, credit quality, debts with the company.”  
That is to say, the potential affected parties by the violation of the security of the personal data in question, regarding the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.1, were 30.6 million supply points and 8.6 million gas supply points of users who are not customers of ENDESA, as well as data from 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers of ENDESA.  
Likewise, regarding the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.2, the confidentiality of the personal data of 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers of ENDESA was not guaranteed.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA did not properly guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data in its ownership.  
Therefore, it is considered that the known facts constitute an infringement, attributable to ENDESA, for violation of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR.  
V  
Classification and qualification of the infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR  
It is considered that ENDESA did not properly guarantee the confidentiality and integrity of the personal data in its ownership.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

182/214  
The known facts constitute an infringement, attributable to ENDESA, classified under Article 83.5 of the GDPR, which stipulates the following:  
“Violations of the following provisions shall be sanctioned, in accordance with paragraph 2, with administrative fines of up to 20,000,000 EUR or, in the case of a company, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 4% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
a) the basic principles for processing, including the conditions for consent under Articles 5, 6, 7, and 9; (…)”  
For the purposes of the statute of limitations for infringements, the infringement attributed prescribes after three years, in accordance with Article 72.1.a) of the LOPDGDD, which qualifies the following conduct as very serious:  
“a) The processing of personal data in violation of the principles and guarantees established in Article 5 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679”  
VI  
Sanction for the infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR  
This infringement may be sanctioned with a fine of up to €20,000,000 or, in the case of a company, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 4% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount, in accordance with Article 83.5 of the GDPR.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to graduate the sanction to be imposed in accordance with the following criteria established by Article 83.2 of the GDPR:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The nature, gravity, and duration of the infringement, taking into account the nature, scope, or purpose of the processing operation in question as well as the number of affected parties and the level of damage and harm they have suffered (paragraph a): for failing to properly guarantee, at least between July 1, 2021, and October 15, 2021, the integrity of the data of at least 760 affected parties who became victims of fraudulent contracts and for failing to guarantee the confidentiality of the data of 30.6 million electricity supply points and 8.6 million gas supply points of non-ENDESA customers, as well as for failing to guarantee the confidentiality of identification data, address, phone number, email, bank account number, among others, of 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.2 million gas customers of ENDESA between August 2021 and February 2022.  
- Negligence in the infringement (paragraph b): Although in August 2021 ENDESA was already aware that it had published a truthful announcement  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
183/214  
by selling the possibility of accessing its database, ENDESA took more than a month to reset or delete the compromised users reported by ***EMPRESA.1 and took months to delete the compromised user ***USUARIO.1 (it did not even carry out an immediate preventive reset of all users nor were new users created in order to avoid having to use the same user with multiple sessions initiated), which allowed access to personal data stored in ENDESA's systems for months and enabled fraudulent user registrations. It has also been severely negligent in requesting Facebook to remove the ads in question, as it did not address FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED, as it was indicated that this was the correct channel. All of this allowed the confidentiality of the data to be compromised, at least until February 2022, and also allowed the integrity of such data to be compromised between July 1, 2021, and October 15, 2021, at a minimum.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA was severely negligent in ensuring the confidentiality and integrity of the data it owns.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to graduate the sanction to be imposed according to the following criteria established in section 2 of Article 76 “Sanctions and corrective measures” of the LOPDGDD:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The link between the infringer's activity and the processing of personal data (section b): it is a large company accustomed to processing personal data.  
The balance of the circumstances contemplated in Article 83.2 of the GDPR and 76.2 of the LOPDGDD, regarding the infringement committed by violating the provisions of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR, allows for the imposition of an administrative fine of €2,500,000 (two million five hundred thousand euros).  
VII  
Security of processing  
Article 32 “Security of processing” of the GDPR establishes:  
“1. Taking into account the state of the art, the costs of implementation, and the nature, scope, context, and purposes of the processing, as well as the risks of varying probability and severity for the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the controller and the processor shall implement appropriate technical and organizational measures to ensure a level of security appropriate to the risk, which may include, among others:  
a) the pseudonymization and encryption of personal data;  
b) the ability to ensure the ongoing confidentiality, integrity, availability, and resilience of processing systems and services;  
c) the ability to restore the availability and access to personal data quickly in the event of a physical or technical incident;  
d) a process for regularly testing, assessing, and evaluating the effectiveness of the technical and organizational measures to ensure the security of processing.  
2. In assessing the appropriateness of the level of security, particular consideration shall be given to the risks presented by the processing of data, in particular as a consequence of the accidental or unlawful destruction, loss, or alteration of personal data transmitted, stored, or otherwise processed, or the unauthorized communication or access to such data.  
3. Adherence to an approved code of conduct pursuant to Article 40 or to an approved certification mechanism pursuant to Article 42 may serve as an element to demonstrate compliance with the requirements set out in paragraph 1 of this article.  
4. The controller and the processor shall take measures to ensure that any person acting under the authority of the controller or the processor and having access to personal data only processes such data on instructions from the controller, unless required to do so by Union or Member State law.”  
In the present case, ENDESA issued an authorization to ***EMPRESA.1 for subcontracting in favor of ***EMPRESA.9, in which the start date for the subcontracted work was established as August 1, 2021.  
On August 24, 2021, ENDESA detected an advertisement on the social network Facebook where a user of the ***HERRAMIENTA.1 application was being sold with access to customer information.  
On September 8, 2021, ENDESA conducted a second assessment of the incident where it proposed (...).  
On September 10, 2021, ENDESA obtained verification of the truthfulness of the advertisement and the identification of the credentials of the application user that were being used by the advertiser to sell access.  
On September 13, 2021, ENDESA convened the Security Breach Committee and decided to adopt the following measures:  
  
(...)  
Also, in this Committee, it was proposed to adopt the following measures:  
  
(...)  
Therefore, by September 2021, ENDESA was already aware that a breach of the security of personal data had occurred in its systems and that it was possible to access the personal data of its customers and it was  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
185/214  
aware that the security measures it had implemented were not appropriate and needed to be improved, even by that date.  
In any case, this Agency considers that the measures adopted by ENDESA prior to the incident in question cannot be deemed appropriate in the terms of Article 32 of the GDPR, taking into account, among other circumstances, the large volume of data it processes, the number of affected individuals by the processing, and the involvement of data processors, given that the state of the art allowed for the implementation of adequate measures to, at a minimum, prevent the same user from having multiple sessions open at the same time, implement a multifactor login that would hinder unauthorized access, maintain a comprehensive log record to obtain details of the activities performed by its users, among others, and that the cost of implementing such measures was perfectly manageable for ENDESA, a company that had a sales volume of ***CANTIDAD.1 euros and an operating result of ***CANTIDAD.3 euros, especially considering the high risk to the rights and freedoms of the millions of individuals whose personal data ENDESA processes.  
Subsequently, on October 19, 2021, ***EMPRESA.1 detected that a user from ***EMPRESA.9 had attempted to carry out a fraudulent registration. Given the suspicion that this could have been carried out by other agents of ***EMPRESA.9, ***EMPRESA.1 initiated an internal audit process and communicated to the person responsible for their account at ENDESA what had happened via telephone, also sending an email with the initial inquiries made and the first measures taken in this regard. On the same day, a complete shutdown of all users from ***EMPRESA.9 and access to any tool of the ENDESA campaign was carried out, and an internal audit was initiated to assess the volume of customers affected by fraudulent contracts.  
***EMPRESA.1 terminated the contract with ***EMPRESA.9 effective October 19, 2021, the day it became aware of the fraudulent practice carried out by ***EMPRESA.9.  
However, ENDESA indicates that, once the possible origin of the allegedly irregular contracts was identified, it proceeded to limit the temporal scope of its actions, verifying that from July 1, 2021, the sales volume of ENDESA experienced an unusual and unjustified increase (specifically, sales increased by more than 500%), which could be representative of the irregular actions of the personnel contracted by ***EMPRESA.9. And that as of October 15, 2021, the activity of said subcontractor ceased – as a consequence of the termination of the contract with ***EMPRESA.9 – thus limiting the temporal perimeter of the analysis of the contracts to the period between July 1 and October 15, 2021.  
Despite what has been stated by ENDESA, it has been established in the present procedure that the activity of ***EMPRESA.9 ceased on October 19, 2021.  
As can be observed, between August 24, 2021 (the date on which ENDESA detects that an advertisement was published on the social network Facebook selling a user of the application ***HERRAMIENTA.1 with access to customer information) and October 19, 2021...

- The user ***USER.13, assigned to ***COMPANY.12, was reset or disabled in ***TOOL.1 on September 3, 2021, reset again on November 29, 2021, and deleted from the systems on August 24, 2022. However, it was only deleted from the ***TOOL.2 system on January 12, 2022, with its last access to the system being on November 26, 2021.  
That is to say, ***COMPANY.1 notified ENDESA that this user was compromised on October 27, 2021, but ENDESA had already reset the user in ***TOOL.1 on September 3, almost two months earlier (the reasons for this action are unknown to this Agency, but it is possible that ENDESA was already aware that this user was compromised; in fact, two days prior, ***COMPANY.16 had indicated that one of the users posting Facebook ads had been an agent of this company).  
In any case, if the user in question was used to carry out fraudulent contracts in October 2021, this Agency considers that the password reset in September 2021 was of little use, and the risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties remained intact. Furthermore, following the communication from ***COMPANY.1, the user was reset more than a month later.  
This Agency also finds it noteworthy that the user was reset in ***TOOL.1 on September 3, 2021, but was only deleted from the ***TOOL.2 platform on January 12, 2022, with its last access to it being on November 26, 2021. That is to say, even knowing that the user had been compromised in ***TOOL.1, the user had free access to ***TOOL.2 for one more month.  
- The user ***USER.15, assigned to ***COMPANY.12, was reset or disabled in ***TOOL.1 on September 21, 2021,  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6

sedeagpd.gob.es

reset again on November 29, 2021, and removed from the systems on August 24, 2022. However, it was only removed from the ***HERRAMIENTA.2 system on January 12, 2022, with its last access to the system on November 25, 2021.  
That is to say, ***EMPRESA.1 notified ENDESA that this user was compromised on October 27, 2021, but ENDESA had already reset the user in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 on September 21, more than a month earlier (the reasons for this action are unknown to this Agency, but it is possible that ENDESA already knew that this user was compromised; in fact, on that same day, it was indicated to ENDESA that one of the users posting Facebook ads had been an agent of ***EMPRESA.15).

-  
The user ***USUARIO.16, assigned to ***EMPRESA.5, was reset or disabled in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 on November 29, 2021, and removed from its systems on August 24, 2022. It was removed from the ***HERRAMIENTA.2 system on February 17, 2022, with its last access to the system on December 3, 2021.  
That is to say, this user was only reset more than a month after ***EMPRESA.1 had communicated to ENDESA that they had been compromised.  
Regarding the rest of the users, the date on which ENDESA became aware that such users had been compromised is unknown, but the following can be noted:  
-  
The user ***USUARIO.10, assigned to ***EMPRESA.15, was reset or disabled in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 on September 10, 2021, and removed from the systems on August 24, 2022. It was removed from the ***HERRAMIENTA.2 system on September 10, 2021, with its last access to the system on September 9, 2021.  
That is to say, ENDESA reset the user in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2 on September 10, 2021 (the reasons for this action are unknown to this Agency, but it is possible that ENDESA was aware that this user was compromised, as on that same day ENDESA sent an email referring to an active employee at ***EMPRESA.15, whose user was to be immediately deactivated).  
-  
The user ***USUARIO.2 (***USUARIO.2), assigned to ***EMPRESA.8, was reset or disabled in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and removed from its systems on November 19, 2021.

The user ***USUARIO.1, assigned to ***EMPRESA.13, was reset or disabled in ***HERRAMIENTA.1 on February 15, 2022, and removed from the systems on August 24, 2022. That is to say, they were not reset before the first burofax was sent to Facebook on February 8, 2022, as ENDESA claims in its allegations regarding the initiation agreement.  
All of the above demonstrates that, despite the fact that in October 2021 five of the compromised users had already been identified due to the violation of personal data security in question, ENDESA's lack of diligence in preventing access to its applications by the compromised users allowed for even more unauthorized access to personal data to occur for over a month beyond what had already taken place.  
Nor had the security measures proposed from the outset of the personal data security breach been implemented, which were supposedly adequate to mitigate the consequences of the data security breach, since it is not true that the accesses of the compromised users to ENDESA's tools had been diligently blocked, disabling their credentials.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
191/214  
nor that this took place before the first burofax was sent to Facebook.  
For its part, regarding the risk analysis conducted on the processing activity that has suffered the violation of personal data security prior to the incident that occurred, ENDESA provides, along with its written submission dated July 26, 2022, two documents, both generated by the tool "***HERRAMIENTA.1" used by the Enel Group to carry out the privacy risk assessment, concerning the tools ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2. In neither of these documents is there any indication that the possibility of an employee of a supplier or a subcontracting company misusing the personal data held in ENDESA's information systems was even considered.  
Furthermore, on September 15, 2022, the SGID verified that advertisements identified by ENDESA were still published on Facebook offering the sale of access credentials to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and databases. This evidences that more than a year after an advertisement selling user access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 was detected, ENDESA had not been sufficiently diligent in ensuring that such publications were removed from the social network in question, which was one of the first measures decided by the company a year prior.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA was not sufficiently diligent in implementing appropriate security measures to prevent security incidents like the one that occurred in this case.  
Therefore, it is considered that the known facts constitute an infringement attributable to ENDESA for violation of Article 32 of the GDPR.  

VIII  
Classification and Qualification of the Infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR  
In this case, it is considered that ENDESA did not apply the appropriate technical and organizational measures to ensure a level of security adequate to the risk of a personal data security breach such as the one that occurred.  
The known facts constitute an infringement attributable to ENDESA, classified under Article 83.4 of the GDPR, which stipulates the following:  
“Violations of the following provisions shall be sanctioned, in accordance with paragraph 2, with administrative fines of up to 10,000,000 EUR or, in the case of a company, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
a) the obligations of the controller and the processor under Articles 8, 11, 25 to 39, 42, and 43; (…)”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

192/214  
For the purposes of the statute of limitations for infringements, the infringement attributed prescribes after two years, in accordance with Article 73.f) of the LOPDGDD, which qualifies the following conduct as serious:  
“f) The failure to adopt those technical and organizational measures that are appropriate to ensure a level of security adequate to the risk of processing, in the terms required by Article 32.1 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679.”  

IX  
Sanction for the Infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR  
This infringement may be sanctioned with a fine of up to €10,000,000 or, in the case of a company, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount, in accordance with Article 83.4 of the GDPR.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed in accordance with the following criteria established by Article 83.2 of the GDPR:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The nature, gravity, and duration of the infringement, taking into account the nature, scope, or purpose of the processing operation in question as well as the number of affected parties and the level of damage and harm suffered (paragraph a): for not having implemented the appropriate security measures to the risk of a personal data security breach such as the one that has occurred in this case, which allowed the integrity of the data of at least 760 affected parties to be compromised between July 1, 2021, and October 15, 2021, and allowed the confidentiality of the data of 30.6 million electricity supply points and 8.6 million gas supply points of non-ENDESA customers and the confidentiality of identification data, address, phone number, email, bank account number, among others, of 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.8 million gas customers of ENDESA between August 2021 and February 2022 to be at risk.  
In this regard, it is noteworthy that ENDESA itself acknowledges that two or more people could simultaneously access ***HERRAMIENTA.1 with the same user, with the mult-session option enabled, and that no logs of the use of the tools ***HERRAMIENTA.1 or ***HERRAMIENTA.2 were maintained, which indicates a lack of adequate security measures to detect and prevent unauthorized access.  
Regarding the possible consequences of the lack of such personal data security measures, the main one is the loss of control over their data, with the consequent risk of being a victim of phishing, another cyberattack, and the contracting of unsolicited supplies, with the stress and  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
193/214  
additional costs that such situations could entail for them, as they could even be held liable for debts related to those unsolicited services.  
- Negligence in the infringement (section b): Regarding the alleged infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR, ENDESA's severely negligent behavior is evident in its conduct both before and after the incident occurred, as there was not even a consideration of the possibility that there could be misuse of users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and ***HERRAMIENTA.2, which meant that adequate measures were not implemented to ensure user authentication with the necessary guarantees, preventing unauthorized access to the systems and allowing for proper tracking of these users' activity within those systems. Users known to be compromised were neither reset nor removed immediately or effectively from either ***HERRAMIENTA.1 or ***HERRAMIENTA.2, as it took more than a month, which allowed for unauthorized access to ENDESA's personal data during that entire time and facilitated a significant number of fraudulent registrations. A preventive reset of all users in the systems was also not carried out in light of a potential threat (which later turned out to be true).  
Despite the fact that by September 2021 ENDESA was aware that a security incident had occurred that jeopardized the personal data under its ownership, its lack of diligence in adopting appropriate and necessary security measures to prevent the confidentiality of such data from being compromised allowed for a total of nine users to be compromised until January 2022 (as ENDESA has acknowledged), who were being used to sell access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 through Facebook from August 2021 to February 2022, at a minimum.  
Furthermore, as of October 27, 2021, ENDESA had already identified five of the users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 compromised in the violation of personal data security, who were reset more than a month after such communication. All of this demonstrates that, despite the fact that in October 2021 five of the users compromised by the violation of personal data security had already been identified, ENDESA's lack of diligence in preventing access to its applications by the compromised users allowed for even more unauthorized access to personal data to occur over the following months than had already taken place.  
For its part, on February 8, 2022, FACEBOOK SPAIN responded to ENDESA indicating that it was not the competent entity to remove the published ads, but rather FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED, and requested that the communication be directed to that entity. Nevertheless, it has not been established in the present file that ENDESA directed any communication in this regard to FACEBOOK IRELAND LIMITED, as requested by FACEBOOK SPAIN, despite having been agreed upon in the meeting of September 13, 2021, and in August 2021 to request the cancellation of the  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
194/214  
advertisements, due to the risks they posed, which also demonstrates a notable lack of diligence in implementing one of the first measures agreed upon in September 2021, namely the removal of the advertisements in question.  
Furthermore, on September 15, 2022, the SGID verified that advertisements identified by ENDESA were still published on Facebook offering the sale of access credentials to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 and databases. This shows that more than a year after an advertisement selling user access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 was detected, ENDESA had not been sufficiently diligent in requesting that such publications be removed from the social network in question, which was one of the first measures decided by the company a year earlier.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA was severely negligent in implementing appropriate security measures to prevent security incidents like the one that occurred in this case, and its actions following the incident in question were also negligent.  
As a mitigating factor:  
- Any other aggravating or mitigating factor applicable to the circumstances of the case, such as financial benefits obtained or losses avoided, directly or indirectly, through the infringement (section k): upon becoming aware of the publication of advertisements on Facebook selling users for access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1, ENDESA adopted a series of measures, such as resetting user passwords, monitoring the publications on that social network, attempting to remove the aforementioned advertisements, disabling the possibility of maintaining simultaneous sessions, and increasing the traceability of accesses to ***HERRAMIENTA.1, among others.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed according to the following criteria established in section 2 of Article 76 “Sanctions and corrective measures” of the LOPDGDD:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The link between the infringer's activity and the processing of personal data (section b): it is a large company accustomed to processing personal data.  
The balance of the circumstances contemplated in Article 83.2 of the GDPR and 76.2 of the LOPDGDD, regarding the infringement committed by violating the provisions of Article 32 of the GDPR, allows for the imposition of an administrative fine of €1,500,000 (one million five hundred thousand euros).  
X  
Notification to the supervisory authority  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

195/214  
Article 33 “Notification of a personal data breach to the supervisory authority” of the GDPR states:  
“1. In the event of a personal data breach, the data controller shall notify the competent supervisory authority in accordance with Article 55 without undue delay and, where feasible, not later than 72 hours after becoming aware of it, unless the breach is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons. If the notification to the supervisory authority does not take place within 72 hours, it shall be accompanied by reasons for the delay.  
(…).”  
In this case, it is recorded that ENDESA on August 24, 2021, had already detected that an advertisement selling user access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been published on the social network Facebook. And by September 10, 2021, ENDESA had already verified the truthfulness of such advertisement, to the point that on September 13, 2021, it convened a Committee to decide and propose the adoption of the corresponding security measures.  
Subsequently, on October 19, 2021, its provider ***EMPRESA.1 informed ENDESA that it had detected possible fraudulent registrations by its subcontractor ***EMPRESA.9 and even on October 27, 2021, ENDESA received an email from ***EMPRESA.1 informing that 5 users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been compromised and explaining how such fraudulent registrations could have occurred.  
Months later, in January 2022, ENDESA identified new advertisements published on the social network Facebook offering users for access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1. And on February 4, 2022, it requested FACEBOOK SPAIN to remove the aforementioned advertisements, as they could constitute a crime.  
Despite all of the above, ENDESA did not inform this Agency of the existence of a possible breach of personal data security until February 10, 2022, when it verified that the data of the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.2 had been compromised, without any justification provided for such delay.  
However, months earlier, it already knew that the security of the data contained in the tool ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been compromised, which allowed for a large number of fraudulent registrations, all without having made any notification to this Agency.  
As indicated in the response to the allegations regarding the initiation agreement and proposal for resolution of this sanctioning procedure, in this case, this Agency considers that ENDESA did not correctly classify the incident by deeming it non-notifiable and reiterates that the incident in question was indeed notifiable since ENDESA became aware on September 10, 2021, of the truthfulness of the August 2021 advertisement, in addition to knowing through ***EMPRESA.1 that five users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been compromised and that there had been improper use of the personal data contained in its systems, which  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
196/214  
took place on October 27, 2021. Regardless of whether the users had been reset or deleted (which has already been thoroughly analyzed and found not to have been done immediately or diligently), the reality is that the incident had already occurred, the confidentiality of the personal data processed by ENDESA had been lost, and there was a probable risk to the rights and freedoms of individuals, since ENDESA had lost control of access to that data, regardless of whether unauthorized access had occurred (or not), in addition to ***HERRAMIENTA.2. Therefore, this Agency reiterates that in this case, ENDESA should have notified the personal data breach within a maximum period of 72 hours from when it became aware of the veracity of the announcements in September 2021 or at least from when it was informed by ***EMPRESA.1 that such an incident had occurred, in October 2021.  
Therefore, it is considered that the known facts constitute an infringement, attributable to ENDESA, for violation of Article 33 of the GDPR.  

XI  
Classification and qualification of the infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR  
In this case, it is considered that ENDESA did not notify the supervisory authority in time that a personal data breach had occurred. The known facts constitute an infringement, attributable to ENDESA, classified under Article 83.4 of the GDPR, which stipulates the following:  
“Violations of the following provisions shall be subject to administrative fines of up to 10,000,000 EUR or, in the case of an enterprise, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total global annual turnover of the preceding financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
a) the obligations of the controller and the processor under Articles 8, 11, 25 to 39, 42, and 43; (…)”  
For the purposes of the statute of limitations for infringements, the infringement attributed prescribes after one year, in accordance with Article 74.m) of the LOPDGDD, which qualifies the following conduct as minor:  
“m) The incomplete, late, or defective notification to the data protection authority of information related to a security breach of personal data in accordance with the provisions of Article 33 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679.”

Sanction for the infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR

28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

197/214  
This infringement may be sanctioned with a fine of up to 10,000,000 EUR or, in the case of an enterprise, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total global annual turnover of the preceding financial year, opting for the higher amount, in accordance with Article 83.4 of the GDPR.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed in accordance with the following criteria established by Article 83.2 of the GDPR:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The nature, severity, and duration of the infringement, taking into account the nature, scope, or purpose of the processing operation in question as well as the number of affected individuals and the level of damage and harm they have suffered (paragraph a): for failing to notify the data protection authority that a personal data breach had occurred within 72 hours, of which there were indications as early as August 2021 and was confirmed in September 2021, meaning it was only communicated five months later, without any legitimate reasons justifying such delay.  
- Negligence in the infringement (paragraph b): ENDESA on August 24, 2021, had already detected that an advertisement selling user access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been published on the social network Facebook. And on September 10, 2021, ENDESA had already verified the truth of such an advertisement, to the extent that on September 13, 2021, it convened a Committee to decide and propose the adoption of the corresponding security measures.  
Subsequently, on October 19, 2021, its provider ***EMPRESA.1 informed ENDESA that it had detected possible fraudulent registrations by its subcontractor ***EMPRESA.9 and even on October 27, 2021, ENDESA received an email from ***EMPRESA.1 informing that 5 users of ***HERRAMIENTA.1 had been compromised and explaining how such fraudulent registrations could have occurred.  
Months later, in January 2022, ENDESA identified new advertisements published on the social network Facebook offering users for access to ***HERRAMIENTA.1. And on February 4, 2022, it requested FACEBOOK SPAIN to remove the aforementioned advertisements, as they could constitute a crime.  
Despite all the above, ENDESA did not inform this Agency of the existence of a possible personal data breach until February 10, 2022, and the reasons alleged by ENDESA cannot justify this delay.  
All of the above demonstrates, as previously indicated, that ENDESA was grossly negligent in notifying the relevant supervisory authority of the existence of a personal data breach.

28001 – Madrid
