Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed according to the following criteria established in section 2 of Article 76 "Sanctions and corrective measures" of the LOPDGDD:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The link between the infringer's activity and the processing of personal data (section b): it is a large company accustomed to processing personal data.  
The balance of the circumstances contemplated in Article 83.2 of the GDPR and 76.2 of the LOPDGDD, regarding the infringement committed by violating the provisions of Article 33 of the GDPR, allows for the imposition of an administrative fine of €800,000 (eight hundred thousand euros).  
XIII  
Communication to the affected party  
Article 34 "Communication of a personal data breach to the affected party" of the GDPR establishes:  
“1. When a personal data breach is likely to result in a high risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the data controller shall communicate the breach to the affected party without undue delay.  
2. The communication to the affected party referred to in paragraph 1 of this article shall describe in clear and plain language the nature of the personal data breach and shall contain at least the information and measures referred to in Article 33, paragraph 3, letters b), c) and d).  
3. The communication to the affected party referred to in paragraph 1 shall not be necessary if any of the following conditions are met:  
a) the data controller has implemented appropriate technical and organizational protection measures and those measures have been applied to the personal data affected by the personal data breach, in particular those that render the personal data unintelligible to any person not authorized to access them, such as encryption;  
b) the data controller has taken further measures to ensure that the high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected party referred to in paragraph 1 is no longer likely to materialize;  
c) it would involve a disproportionate effort. In this case, a public communication or a similar measure shall be adopted to inform the affected parties in an equally effective manner.  
4. When the data controller has not yet communicated the personal data breach to the affected party, the supervisory authority, after considering the likelihood that such a breach poses a high risk, may require the data controller to do so or may decide that one of the conditions mentioned in paragraph 3 is met.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

199/214  
In the present case, the personal data breach posed a high risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, and none of the circumstances listed in paragraph 3 of Article 34 of the GDPR that would exempt ENDESA from the duty to communicate to the affected parties that this personal data breach had occurred were present.  
In fact, in the notification of the incident in question to this Agency, dated February 10, 2022, ENDESA indicated that it would not communicate the personal data breach to those affected, believing that there was no high risk to their rights and freedoms, which is why this Agency issued an order (notified to ENDESA on March 8, 2022) for it to communicate to the affected parties in accordance with the terms of Article 34 of the GDPR.  
The content of the letter template sent by ENDESA was as follows:  
“March 31, 2022  
Dear customer,  
We are contacting you to inform you that we have detected a possible unauthorized access to certain commercial systems of Endesa Energía and that from the moment we became aware of this fact, we have adopted appropriate security measures, both technical and organizational, to prevent a high-risk impact on the rights and freedoms of our customers, ensuring that the confidentiality and integrity of your personal data has not been compromised.  
You can consult our privacy policy at www.endesa.com/es/proteccion-datos-endesa, where you will find information regarding the processing of your personal data. If you prefer, you can directly contact our Data Protection Officer by sending a communication to ***EMAIL.1 to learn more about the measures taken or to obtain further information.  
We take this opportunity to thank you for the trust placed in Endesa.  
Best regards,  
Endesa Energía.”  
As can be seen, in the communication sent to the affected parties, it is stated that “the confidentiality and integrity of your personal data has not been compromised.”  
However, this information provided is not true.  
In ENDESA's notification of the personal data breach to this Agency, dated February 10, 2022, it is indicated that as a result of the incident, the confidentiality and integrity of the processing have been affected. And during the investigation proceedings, it has been confirmed that the credentials of users of ENDESA applications compromised in the personal data breach were used by salespeople from another company (***EMPRESA.9), who did not have access to ENDESA's databases, to extract data from ENDESA customers that were used to carry out fraudulent contracts.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
200/214  
Furthermore, in the communication sent to the affected parties, ENDESA did not include a description of the possible consequences that the breach of personal data security could cause them and even goes so far as to deny that a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected individuals could arise. There is also no specific reference in the communication to the possibility of requesting additional information through another means regarding these possible consequences.  
Therefore, it is considered that the known facts constitute an infringement attributable to ENDESA for violation of Article 34 of the GDPR.  

XIV  
Classification and qualification of the infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR  
In this case, it is considered that ENDESA failed to fulfill the obligation to properly inform the affected parties that a breach of personal data security had occurred.  
The known facts constitute an infringement attributable to ENDESA, classified under Article 83.4 of the GDPR, which stipulates the following:  
“Violations of the following provisions shall be sanctioned, in accordance with paragraph 2, with administrative fines of up to 10,000,000 EUR or, in the case of an enterprise, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
a) the obligations of the controller and the processor under Articles 8, 11, 25 to 39, 42, and 43; (…)”  
For the purposes of the statute of limitations for infringements, the infringement attributed prescribes after one year, in accordance with Article 74.ñ) of the LOPDGDD, which qualifies the following conduct as minor:  
“ñ) The failure to communicate to the affected party a breach of data security that poses a high risk to the rights and freedoms of the affected parties, as required by Article 34 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679, unless the provisions of Article 73 s) of this organic law apply.”  

XV  
Sanction for the infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR  
This infringement may be sanctioned with a fine of up to €10,000,000 or, in the case of an enterprise, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 2% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount, in accordance with Article 83.4 of the GDPR.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed according to the following criteria established by Article 83.2 of the GDPR:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

201/214  
As aggravating factors:  
- The nature, severity, and duration of the infringement, taking into account the nature, scope, or purpose of the data processing operation in question as well as the number of affected parties and the level of damage and harm they have suffered (paragraph a): for not having properly provided, at least, to the 760 affected by the fraudulent registrations the information to which it was obligated regarding the breach of the personal data security in question.  
- Negligence in the infringement (paragraph b): initially, ENDESA was negligent in identifying the need for the communication to the affected parties regarding the breach of personal data security, in the terms of Article 34 of the GDPR to be sent. And once this Agency ordered ENDESA to send the aforementioned communication to the affected parties, ENDESA was severely negligent in providing the information required by the aforementioned Article 34 of the GDPR in a truthful and complete manner.  
As a mitigating factor:  
- Any other aggravating or mitigating factor applicable to the circumstances of the case, such as the financial benefits obtained or losses avoided, directly or indirectly, through the infringement (paragraph k): the contact of the Data Protection Officer was provided for obtaining more information.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed according to the following criteria established in paragraph 2 of Article 76 “Sanctions and corrective measures” of the LOPDGDD:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The link of the infringer's activity with the processing of personal data (paragraph b): it is a large company accustomed to processing personal data.  
The balance of the circumstances contemplated in Article 83.2 of the GDPR and 76.2 of the LOPDGDD, regarding the infringement committed by violating the provisions of Article 34 of the GDPR, allows for the imposition of an administrative fine of €800,000 (eight hundred thousand euros).  

XVI  
Transfers of data to third countries or international organizations  
Article 44 “General principle for transfers” of the GDPR establishes:  
“Personal data that are subject to processing or are to be processed after their transfer to a third country or international organization may only be transferred if, subject to the other provisions of this Regulation, the controller and the processor comply with the conditions established in this chapter, including those relating to further transfers of personal data.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
202/214  
from the third country or international organization to another third country or another international organization. All provisions of this chapter shall apply in order to ensure that the level of protection of natural persons guaranteed by this Regulation is not undermined.”  
In this regard, Article 45 “Transfers based on an adequacy decision” of the GDPR states that:  

“1. A transfer of personal data to a third country or international organization may take place when the Commission has decided that the third country, a territory or one or more specific sectors of that third country, or the international organization in question ensures an adequate level of protection. Such transfer shall not require any specific authorization.  
(…)”  
Whereas Article 46 “Transfers subject to adequate safeguards” of the GDPR states that:  

“1. In the absence of a decision pursuant to Article 45(3), the controller or processor may only transfer personal data to a third country or international organization if it has provided appropriate safeguards, and on condition that data subjects have enforceable rights and effective legal remedies.  
2. The appropriate safeguards referred to in paragraph 1 may be provided, without requiring any specific authorization from a supervisory authority, by:  
a) a legally binding and enforceable instrument between public authorities or bodies;  
b) binding corporate rules in accordance with Article 47;  
c) standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission in accordance with the examination procedure referred to in Article 93(2);  
d) standard data protection clauses adopted by a supervisory authority and approved by the Commission in accordance with the examination procedure referred to in Article 93(2);  
e) a code of conduct approved in accordance with Article 40, together with binding and enforceable commitments of the controller or processor in the third country to apply appropriate safeguards, including with respect to the rights of data subjects; or  
f) a certification mechanism approved in accordance with Article 42, together with binding and enforceable commitments of the controller or processor in the third country to apply appropriate safeguards, including with respect to the rights of data subjects.  
(…)”  
In this case, ENDESA in its response dated November 11, 2022, identifies for each of the users involved in the breach of the personal data security in question the ENDESA service provider with which they had entered into a contract:

That is to say, ENDESA was working with providers located in countries both within and outside the European Union. Specifically, the providers ***COMPANY.15 and ***COMPANY.12 are located outside the European Union, in ***COUNTRY.2 and ***COUNTRY.1, respectively. It is also noted in this procedure that ENDESA had authorized ***COMPANY.1 to subcontract with ***COMPANY.9, a company located in ***COUNTRY.1. Therefore, ENDESA was making international transfers of personal data that it owned to these countries.  
However, Article 45 of the GDPR allows for the international transfer of personal data without the need for specific authorization when the Commission has decided that the third country in question ensures an adequate level of protection. For other cases, Article 46 of the GDPR allows for these transfers provided that appropriate safeguards are offered and on the condition that data subjects have enforceable rights and effective legal remedies.  
So far, neither ***COUNTRY.1 nor ***COUNTRY.2 have been declared as having an adequate level of protection by the European Commission, which is why the transfers of personal data to these countries must comply with the appropriate safeguards of Article 46 of the GDPR.  
In this case, ENDESA has made international transfers to ***COUNTRY.1 and ***COUNTRY.2, and continues to make transfers to ***COUNTRY.2, countries for which the Commission has not declared that they ensure an adequate level of protection, in the terms set out in Article 45 of the GDPR.  
Recital (108) of the GDPR regarding transfers in the absence of an adequacy decision states that “the controller or processor must take measures to compensate for the lack of data protection in a third country through appropriate safeguards for the data subject. Such appropriate safeguards may consist of the use of binding corporate rules, standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission or by a supervisory authority, or contractual clauses authorized by a supervisory authority (…)”.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
204/214  
Article 46 of the GDPR addresses "transfers subject to adequate safeguards" and states in its paragraph 1 that, in the absence of a decision pursuant to Article 45, paragraph 3, the data controller or the processor may only transfer personal data to a third country or international organization if they have provided adequate safeguards and provided that data subjects have enforceable rights and effective legal remedies.  
Article 46, paragraph 2, of the GDPR adds that "adequate safeguards" may be provided, without the need for express authorization from a supervisory authority, among others, by standard contractual clauses for data protection adopted by a supervisory authority and approved by the Commission in accordance with the examination procedure referred to in Article 93, paragraph 2. (Article 46, paragraph 2, letter c).  
This article must be interpreted in the sense declared by the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU) in its judgment of July 16, 2020, delivered in case C-311/18 (Schrems II):  
“105 Therefore, it is appropriate to respond to the second, third, and sixth preliminary questions that Article 46, paragraphs 1 and 2, letter c), of the GDPR must be interpreted as meaning that the adequate safeguards, enforceable rights, and effective legal remedies required by those provisions must ensure that the rights of individuals whose personal data are transferred to a third country on the basis of standard contractual clauses for data protection enjoy a level of protection that is substantially equivalent to that guaranteed within the Union by the aforementioned Regulation, interpreted in light of the Charter. For this purpose, the assessment of the level of protection guaranteed in the context of a transfer of such characteristics must, in particular, take into account both the contractual stipulations agreed between the data controller or processor established in the Union and the recipient of the transfer established in the third country in question, as well as, regarding any potential access by public authorities of that third country to the personal data transferred in this way, the relevant elements of the legal system of that country and, in particular, those mentioned in Article 45, paragraph 2, of the aforementioned Regulation.”  
In this regard, the European Commission has adopted the Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914 of the Commission, of June 4, 2021, regarding standard contractual clauses for the transfer of personal data to third countries in accordance with Regulation (EU) 2016/679 of the European Parliament and of the Council.  
These standard contractual clauses adopted by the European Commission constitute a method to provide "adequate safeguards" in the absence of an adequacy decision pursuant to Article 46, paragraph 2, letter c), of the GDPR, establishing new standard contractual clauses that can be used by both the controller and the processor in order to offer adequate safeguards within the meaning of Article 46, paragraph 1, of the GDPR, with the previous decisions of 2001 and 2010 being repealed as of September 27, 2021. However, according to its clause 4.4, contracts entered into before September 27, 2021, under Decision 2001/497/EC or Decision 2010/87/EU are considered to provide adequate safeguards within the meaning of Article 46, paragraph 1, of the GDPR until December 27, 2022, provided that the processing operations that are the subject of the
205/214  
The contract remains unchanged and the standard contractual clauses ensure that the transfer of personal data is subject to adequate safeguards.

Having established the above, it is important to highlight, regarding the international transfer made to ***COUNTRY.2 under the contract signed between ENDESA and ***COMPANY.15, that clause 14 of the decision refers to the "Law and practices of the country affecting compliance with the clauses." This clause contains four modules:  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

206/214  
related to the transfer from controller to controller; transfer from controller to processor; transfer from processor to processor; and transfer from processor to controller, to which the provisions contained therein apply. It establishes the following:  
“MODULE ONE: transfer from controller to controller  
MODULE TWO: transfer from controller to processor  
MODULE THREE: transfer from processor to processor  
MODULE FOUR: transfer from processor to controller (only if the processor in the EU combines the personal data received from the controller in the third country with the personal data collected by the processor in the EU)  
a) The parties assure that they have no reason to believe that the law and practices of the third country of destination applicable to the processing of personal data by the data importer, especially the requirements for the communication of personal data or the measures for authorization of access by public authorities, prevent the data importer from fulfilling the obligations assigned to it by this set of clauses. This assertion is based on the premise that the law and practices that essentially respect fundamental rights and freedoms and do not exceed what is necessary and proportionate in a democratic society to safeguard one of the objectives listed in Article 23, paragraph 1, of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 do not oppose this set of clauses.  
b) The parties declare that, in providing the guarantee referred to in point a), they have duly taken into account, in particular, the following aspects:  
i) the specific circumstances of the transfer, such as the length of the processing chain, the number of parties involved, and the transmission channels used; the anticipated further transfers; the type of recipient; the purpose of the processing; the categories and format of the personal data transferred; the economic sector in which the transfer takes place; the location of the storage of the transferred data;  
ii) the law and practices of the third country of destination —especially those requiring the communication of data to public authorities or authorizing access by such authorities— that are relevant given the specific circumstances of the transfer, as well as the applicable limitations and safeguards (12);  
iii) the relevant contractual, technical, or organizational safeguards provided to complement the safeguards set out in this set of clauses, particularly including the measures applied during the transfer and the processing of personal data in the destination country.  
c) The data importer assures that, in carrying out the assessment referred to in point b), it has done everything possible to provide the data exporter with the  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
207/214  
relevant information and commits to continue collaborating with the data exporter to ensure compliance with this set of clauses.  
d) The parties agree to document the evaluation referred to in paragraph b) and make it available to the competent supervisory authority upon request.

[Module three: The data exporter will notify the controller.]  
f) If the notification referred to in paragraph e) is made or if the data exporter has reason to believe that the data importer can no longer fulfill the obligations assigned to it by this set of clauses, the data exporter will promptly determine the appropriate measures (for example, technical or organizational measures to ensure security and confidentiality) that must be adopted by the data exporter and/or the data importer to remedy the situation [module three: if applicable, after consulting the controller]. The data exporter will suspend the transfer of data if it considers that there are no adequate guarantees or if so ordered by [module three: the controller or] the competent supervisory authority. In this case, the data exporter will be entitled to terminate the contract regarding the processing of personal data under this set of clauses. If the contract has more than two contracting parties, the data exporter may only exercise this right of termination with respect to the relevant party, unless the parties have agreed otherwise. In the event of termination of the contract under this clause, clause 16, paragraphs d) and e) will apply.  
Thus, the aforementioned clause 14 establishes an obligation for both the exporter and importer to prepare an evaluation that takes into account all the issues determined by the clause itself.  
For its part, the CJEU has explained in its ruling in Schrems II:  
“132 Since, as is evident from paragraph 125 of this judgment, it is inherent to the contractual nature of standard data protection clauses that they cannot bind public authorities of third countries, but that Articles 44 and 46, paragraphs 1 and 2, letter c), of the GDPR, interpreted in light of Articles 7, 8, and 47 of the Charter, require that the level of protection of natural persons guaranteed by that Regulation is not compromised, it may be necessary to supplement the guarantees provided in those standard data protection clauses. In this regard, Recital 109 of the said Regulation states that ‘the possibility for [the] controllers […] of processing to rely on standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission […] should not prevent controllers […] from adding other clauses or additional guarantees’ and specifies, in particular, that ‘controllers […] should be encouraged to offer additional guarantees […] that complement the standard data protection clauses.’”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
208/214  
133 It is therefore evident that the standard data protection clauses adopted by the Commission pursuant to Article 46(2)(c) of the same Regulation are intended solely to provide data controllers or processors established in the Union with contractual guarantees that apply uniformly across all third countries and, therefore, regardless of the level of protection guaranteed in each of them. To the extent that these standard data protection clauses cannot provide, by their nature, guarantees that go beyond a contractual obligation to ensure compliance with the level of protection required by Union law, such clauses may require, depending on the situation of a specific third country, the adoption of additional measures by the data controller to ensure compliance with that level of protection.  
134 In this regard, as noted by the Advocate General in point 126 of his conclusions, the contractual mechanism provided for in Article 46(2)(c) of the GDPR is based on the accountability of the data controller or processor established in the Union, as well as, subsidiarily, of the competent supervisory authority. It is therefore primarily the responsibility of that data controller or processor to verify, on a case-by-case basis and, if necessary, in collaboration with the recipient of the transfer, whether the law of the destination third country guarantees an adequate level of protection, in light of Union law, for the personal data transferred based on standard data protection clauses, providing, when necessary, additional guarantees to those offered by such clauses.  
In this context, the Recommendations 01/2020 on measures that complement transfer instruments to ensure compliance with the level of protection of EU personal data, adopted on November 10, 2020, must also be taken into account, in which, when interpreting the aforementioned judgment of the CJEU, it is stated that “the Court leaves open the possibility for exporters to apply complementary measures that address these protection gaps, so that it reaches the level required by Union law. The Court does not specify what measures could be. However, the Court emphasizes that exporters will have to determine them on a case-by-case basis. This aligns with the principle of proactive accountability in Article 5(2) of the GDPR, which requires that data controllers be responsible and able to demonstrate compliance with the principles of the GDPR regarding the processing of personal data (...).”  
In the present case, ENDESA has provided the Impact Assessment on Transfers to ***COUNTRY.2, which aims to evaluate, in relation to the transfer of personal data from the exporter to the importer, the adequacy level of the destination third country, as well as the adoption of appropriate technical and organizational security measures to mitigate the risk of such transfer.  
In this regard, this assessment is limited to evaluating the technical and organizational security measures applied by the data importer, without addressing the necessity or otherwise of any other possible contractual complementary measures to the standard contractual clauses that may be necessary.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
209/214  
In this regard, it is important to highlight that, in this document, regarding the legislative evaluation of the importing country ***COUNTRY.2, the evaluation yields the result “3 – Partially adequate,” limiting itself to considering the existence of data protection legislation and an independent supervisory authority in the third country, but does not even analyze the functions and powers of this authority.  
Furthermore, the document titled “analysis of legislation and practices” of ***COUNTRY.2 is provided, aimed at assessing whether the personal data being transferred is adequately protected concerning the potential access of the authorities of the countries to which the data is transferred. This document states that the Constitution of ***COUNTRY.2 guarantees the fundamental right to personal and family privacy and that it requires a judicial order for the interception of communications. The existence of a data protection law is also taken into account in this document.  
After analyzing the circumstances addressed in the document, the following conclusion is reached:  
CONCLUSION  
It is understood that ***COUNTRY.2 has a regulatory framework that, while it cannot be considered equivalent to that established in the European Union regarding the protection of personal data, can be regarded as reasonably adequate.  
However, to reach a definitive conclusion on the suitability of international transfer, as well as on the signing of additional guarantee measures to ensure that the level of protection of the transferred data aligns with the essential equivalence standard of the European Union, it is necessary to consider and evaluate the other components related to the particularities of each of the transfers.  
In summary, from the analysis of this documentation, it cannot be asserted that the evaluation includes a sufficient analysis of the law and practices of ***COUNTRY.2 that would allow for an assessment of whether the destination third country offers an adequate level of protection. Thus, it does not contain any evaluation regarding the existence of norms that require the communication of data to public authorities or authorize access by such authorities, as well as the applicable limitations and guarantees. According to the aforementioned CJEU ruling, this analysis should include the elements outlined in Article 45.2 of the GDPR, which includes the analysis of the existence of international treaties signed by the third country or other international commitments adopted (Article 45.2.c GDPR), the study of the national legislation of the third country in the sense intended by Article 45.2.a) of the GDPR:  
“the rule of law, respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms, relevant legislation, both general and sectoral, including that related to public security, defense, national security, and criminal legislation, and the access of public authorities to personal data, as well as the application of such legislation, data protection standards, professional standards, and security measures, including standards on further transfers of personal data to another third country or international organization observed in that.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
210/214  
country or international organization, the jurisprudence, as well as the recognition of the  
interested parties whose personal data is being transferred of effective and enforceable rights and of administrative remedies and judicial actions that are effective.”  
In this regard, recital (20) of the Implementing Decision (EU) 2021/914 of the  
Commission states:  
“The parties must take into account, in particular, the specific circumstances of the  
transfer (such as the content and duration of the contract, the nature of the data  
transferred, the type of recipient, and the purpose of the processing), the law and  
practices of the third country of destination that are applicable given the circumstances  
of the transfer and the guarantees established to complement the guarantees  
contemplated in the standard contractual clauses (such as relevant contractual,  
technical, and organizational measures applicable to the transfer and processing of  
personal data in the destination country). Regarding the effect of the mentioned law  
and practices on compliance with the standard contractual clauses, different elements  
can be assessed in a global evaluation; for example, reliable information about the  
application of the law in practice (jurisprudence, reports from independent supervisory  
bodies, etc.), the existence or absence of requests in the same sector, and, under strict  
conditions, the documented practical experience of the data exporter and/or the data  
importer.”  
However, none of these circumstances have been taken into account. The  
deficiencies observed in the Data Protection Impact Assessment of ***COUNTRY.2 as a  
country importing personal data prevent it from being considered that the guarantee of  
clause 14 has been provided.  
Therefore, it is considered that the known facts constitute an infringement,  
attributable to ENDESA, for violation of Article 44 of the GDPR.  
XVII  
Classification and qualification of the infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR  
In this case, it is considered that ENDESA has not complied with the requirements  
necessary to carry out international data transfers to its suppliers and subcontractors  
located in ***COUNTRY.2.  
The known facts constitute an infringement, attributable to ENDESA, classified under  
Article 83.5 of the GDPR, which stipulates the following:  
“Violations of the following provisions shall be sanctioned, in accordance with  
paragraph 2, with administrative fines of up to 20,000,000 EUR or, in the case of a  
company, an amount equivalent to 4% of the total annual global turnover of the  
previous financial year, opting for the higher amount:  
(…)  
c) the transfers of personal data to a recipient in a third country or an  
international organization pursuant to Articles 44 to 49; (…)”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
211/214  
For the purposes of the statute of limitations for violations, the alleged violation is subject to a three-year limitation period, in accordance with Article 72.1.l) of the LOPDGDD, which qualifies the following conduct as very serious:  
“l) The international transfer of personal data to a recipient located in a third country or to an international organization, when the guarantees, requirements, or exceptions established in Articles 44 to 49 of Regulation (EU) 2016/679 are not met.”  

XVIII  
Sanction for the violation of Article 44 of the GDPR  
This violation may be sanctioned with a fine of up to €20,000,000 or, in the case of a company, an amount equivalent to a maximum of 4% of the total annual global turnover of the previous financial year, opting for the higher amount, in accordance with Article 83.5 of the GDPR.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed in accordance with the following criteria established in Article 83.2 of the GDPR:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The nature, gravity, and duration of the violation, taking into account the nature, scope, or purpose of the processing operation in question as well as the number of affected parties and the level of damage and harm suffered (paragraph a): for failing to comply with the requirements necessary for carrying out international transfers to its suppliers and subcontractors located in ***COUNTRY.2, affected by the security breach in question, of the data of 30.6 million electricity supply points and 8.6 million gas supply points of non-ENDESA customers, and the identification data, address, phone number, email, bank account number, among others, of 4.8 million electricity customers and 1.8 million gas customers of ENDESA.  
- Negligence in the violation (paragraph b): ENDESA is a large company in its business sector, which must ensure the fundamental right to the protection of personal data it owns. In this case, this Agency considers that it has been severely negligent in analyzing whether ***COUNTRY.2 as an importing country of personal data enjoys adequate protection. In particular, considering that it is not only a large company from which greater professionalism could be expected, but also an entity accustomed to processing personal data. In this regard, the SAN of October 17, 2007 (rec. 63/2006) is very illustrative, which indicates that “…the Supreme Court has understood that there is recklessness whenever a legal duty of care is neglected, that is, when the offender does not behave with the required diligence. And in assessing the degree of diligence, the professionalism or lack thereof of the subject must be especially weighed, and there is no doubt that, in the case now examined, when the activity of the appellant involves constant and abundant handling of personal data, it must insist on rigor and exquisite care to comply with the legal provisions in this regard.”  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es  

212/214  
Denying the existence of negligent conduct on the part of the appellant would be equivalent to acknowledging that its conduct - by action or omission - has been diligent. Obviously, this perspective of the facts is not shared, as the serious lack of due diligence has been evidenced.  
Throughout this sanctioning procedure, it has been established that ENDESA has not conducted a proper evaluation of ***COUNTRY.2 as an importing country of personal data (to which personal data continues to be sent currently).  
Due to the importance of the protected legal asset, ENDESA was obliged to do so, yet it has failed to comply with this duty. Therefore, it is considered that its behavior has been severely negligent.  
Furthermore, it is considered appropriate to adjust the sanction to be imposed in accordance with the following criteria established in paragraph 2 of Article 76 “Sanctions and corrective measures” of the LOPDGDD:  
As aggravating factors:  
- The link of the infringer's activity with the processing of personal data (paragraph b): it is a large company accustomed to processing personal data.  
The balance of the circumstances contemplated in Article 83.2 of the GDPR and 76.2 of the LOPDGDD, regarding the violation committed by infringing the provisions of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR, allows for the imposition of an administrative fine of €500,000 (five hundred thousand euros).  

XIX  
Adoption of measures  
Upon confirming the violation, it is appropriate to impose on the responsible party the obligation to adapt the international transfers of personal data that ENDESA carries out to ***COUNTRY.2 to the provisions of Articles 44 and following of the GDPR within a period of 6 months. The imposition of this measure is compatible with the sanction consisting of an administrative fine, as provided in Article 83.2 of the GDPR.  
The text of the resolution establishes what the violations committed have been and the facts that have led to the breach of data protection regulations, from which it is clearly inferred what measures are to be adopted, without prejudice to the fact that the type of procedures, mechanisms, or specific instruments for implementing them corresponds to the sanctioned party, as it is the data controller who fully knows its organization and must decide, based on proactive responsibility and a risk-based approach, how to comply with the GDPR and the LOPDGDD.  
C/ Jorge Juan, 6  
www.aepd.es  
28001 – Madrid  
sedeagpd.gob.es
213/214  
It is warned that failure to comply with the adoption order of measures imposed by this agency in the sanctioning resolution may be considered an administrative infringement in accordance with the provisions of the GDPR, classified as an infringement in its articles 83.5 and 83.6, which may lead to the initiation of further administrative sanctioning proceedings.  
Therefore, in accordance with the applicable legislation and having assessed the criteria for the graduation of sanctions whose existence has been established,  
the Director of the Spanish Agency for Data Protection RESOLVES:

- For an infringement of Article 5.1.f) of the GDPR, classified in Article 83.5 of said regulation, an administrative fine of 2,500,000.00 euros.  
- For an infringement of Article 32 of the GDPR, classified in Article 83.4 of said regulation, an administrative fine of 1,500,000.00 euros.  
- For an infringement of Article 33 of the GDPR, classified in Article 83.4 of said regulation, an administrative fine of 800,000.00 euros.  
- For an infringement of Article 34 of the GDPR, classified in Article 83.4 of said regulation, an administrative fine of 800,000.00 euros.  
- For an infringement of Article 44 of the GDPR, classified in Article 83.5 of said regulation, an administrative fine of 500,000.00 euros.  
SECOND: TO ORDER ENDESA ENERGÍA, S.A.U., with NIF A81948077, that pursuant to Article 58.2.d) of the GDPR, within a period of 6 months, prove that it has proceeded to adapt the international transfers of personal data it makes to ***COUNTRY.2 to the provisions of Articles 44 and following of the GDPR.

the banking entity CAIXABANK, S.A. In the absence of this, collection will proceed in the executive period.  
Upon receipt of the notification and once it is executable, if the date of executability falls between the 1st and the 15th of each month, inclusive, the deadline for voluntary payment will be until the 20th of the following month or the next business day, and if it falls between the 16th and the last day of each month, inclusive, the payment deadline will be until the 5th of the second following month or the next business day.  
In accordance with the provisions of Article 76.4 of the LOPDGDD and given that the amount of the imposed sanction exceeds one million euros, the information identifying the infringer, the infringement committed, and the amount of the sanction will be published in the Official State Gazette.  
In accordance with the provisions of Article 50 of the LOPDGDD, this Resolution will be made public once it has been notified to the interested parties.  
Against this resolution, which concludes the administrative procedure in accordance with Article 48.6 of the LOPDGDD, and in accordance with the provisions of Article 123 of the LPACAP, the interested parties may optionally file an appeal for reconsideration before the Director of the Spanish Agency for Data Protection within one month from the day following the notification of this resolution or directly file a contentious-administrative appeal before the Contentious-Administrative Chamber of the National Court, in accordance with the provisions of Article 25 and Section 5 of the fourth additional provision of Law 29/1998, of July 13, regulating the Contentious-Administrative Jurisdiction, within two months from the day following the notification of this act, as provided in Article 46.1 of the aforementioned Law.  
Finally, it is noted that in accordance with the provisions of Article 90.3 a) of the LPACAP, the firm resolution in the administrative procedure may be provisionally suspended if the interested party expresses their intention to file a contentious-administrative appeal.  
If this is the case, the interested party must formally communicate this fact by writing to the Spanish Agency for Data Protection, submitting it through the Electronic Registry of the Agency [https://sedeagpd.gob.es/sede-electronica-web/], or through any of the other registries provided for in Article 16.4 of Law 39/2015, of October 1. They must also provide the Agency with documentation proving the effective filing of the contentious-administrative appeal. If the Agency does not become aware of the filing of the contentious-administrative appeal within two months from the day following the notification of this resolution, it will consider the provisional suspension terminated.
